The McKay Conjecture on character degrees
Marc Cabanes, Britta Späth

Abstract
We prove that for any prime ℓ \ell , any finite group has as many irreducible complex characters of degree prime to ℓ \ell as the normalizers of its Sylow ℓ \ell -subgroups. This equality was conjectured by John McKay. The conjecture was reduced by Isaacs–Malle–Navarro (2007) to a conjecture on representations, linear and projective, of finite simple groups that we finish proving here using the classification of those groups. We study mainly characters of normalizers N 𝐆 ​ ( 𝐒 ) F {\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F} of Sylow d d -tori 𝐒 {\mathbf{S}} ( d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 ) in a simply-connected algebraic group 𝐆 {{\mathbf{G}}} of type D l \mathrm{D}_{l} ( l ≥ 4 l\geq 4 ) for which F F is a Frobenius endomorphism. We also introduce a certain class of F F -stable reductive subgroups 𝐌 ≤ 𝐆 {\mathbf{M}}\leq{{\mathbf{G}}} of maximal rank where 𝐌 ∘ {\mathbf{M}}^{\circ} is of type D k × D l − k \mathrm{D}_{k}\times\mathrm{D}_{l-k} . The finite groups 𝐌 F {\mathbf{M}}^{F} are an efficient substitute for N 𝐆 ​ ( 𝐒 ) F {\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F} or the ℓ \ell -local subgroups of 𝐆 F {{\mathbf{G}}}^{F} relevant to McKay’s abstract statement. For a general class of those subgroups 𝐌 F {\mathbf{M}}^{F} we describe their characters and the action of Aut ​ ( 𝐆 F ) 𝐌 F \mathrm{Aut}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})_{{\mathbf{M}}^{F}} on them, showing in particular that Irr ⁡ ( 𝐌 F ) \mathrm{Irr}({\mathbf{M}}^{F}) and Irr ⁡ ( 𝐆 F ) \mathrm{Irr}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}) share some key features in that regard.

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020):
20C15 (20C33 20G40)

1
Introduction

The main theorem of this paper is as follows.

Theorem
\@upn
A

Let
X
be a finite group,
\ell
a prime and
S
a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
X
. Let
\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)
denote the set of complex irreducible characters of
X
whose degree is prime to
\ell
. Then

|\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)|=|\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S))|.

[formula]

Every algebraist may have recognized that this is John McKay’s conjecture on character degrees of finite groups. This paper provides the last of many steps in a proof of this conjecture using the classification of finite simple groups (CFSG). The other steps are mainly contained in the papers
[
IMN07
]
,
[
Ma07
]
,
[
Ma08
]
,
[
S12
]
,
[
CS13
]
,
[
KS16
]
,
[
MS16
]
,
[
CS17a
]
,
[
CS17b
]
,
[
CS19
]
,
[
S23a
]
,
[
S23b
]
, adding more than 400 pages to the CFSG and the background knowledge on representations of quasisimple groups – thus fulfilling Jon Alperin’s prediction that “
we have here a very easily stated conjecture about all finite groups which is not easily decided from a possible classification of all simple groups
”
[
A76
]
.

1.A
The McKay conjecture

McKay’s equality (
MK
) relates two numbers, one
global
in the sense that it pertains to
X
, the other
local
in that it is the same for the
\ell
-local subgroup N
{}_{X}(S)
.

It seems to originate in McKay’s research on character tables of sporadic simple groups
[
MK71
]
, an interest that would also lead him to the “
\mathrm{E}_{8}
-observation"
[
MK80
, p. 185]
and the so-called “(monstrous) Moonshine” on the character degrees of the Monster sporadic group (see
[
CoNo79
]
). It could now be argued that the idea itself of the McKay Conjecture owes a lot to the CFSG as a project, and its proof now draws from the CFSG as a theorem.

While a proof of (
MK
) as elementary as the statement itself seems unattainable, a legitimate wish is to find more structural statements implying character theoretic ones like (
MK
), (
AWC
) below or Dade’s conjecture
[
Da92
, Conj. 6.3]
. This direction of research is exemplified by Broué’s conjecture
[
Br90a
, Conj. 6.1]
for blocks with abelian defect, see
[
Rou23
]
for a recent survey of the issues raised. However, once those character theoretic equalities are checked (see
[
S17
]
for a reduction of Dade’s conjecture), they may well be helpful in establishing module theoretic statements, using arguments in the vein of
[
Br90b
, Thm 1.1]
.

1.B
Early results and perspectives

Soon after the conjecture was sketched by McKay and made precise by Alperin, important verifications of (
MK
) followed: For symmetric groups and
\ell=2
already in
[
Mac71
]
and
[
MK72
]
, for a large class of solvable groups by Isaacs in
[
Is73
]
, for general linear groups in characteristic
\ell
in
[
A76
]
, for many other finite groups of Lie type in characteristic
\ell
by Lehrer in
[
L78
]
, for symmetric groups and general linear groups for arbitrary primes by Olsson in
[
O76
]
. A strong form of (
MK
) was proven for
\ell
-solvable groups by Wolf
[
Wo78
]
.

The statement (
MK
) itself has similarities with the so-called Harish-Chandra theory of cusp forms for finite groups of Lie type
[
Sp70
]
. Fix
G={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
a finite reductive group of Lie type where
F\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{{\mathbf{G}}}
is a Frobenius endomorphism defining the reductive group
{{\mathbf{G}}}
over a finite field. For an
F
-split Levi subgroup
{{\mathbf{L}}}
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and a so-called
cuspidal
character
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}({{\mathbf{L}}}^{F})
, parabolic induction allows us to define a subset
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},({{\mathbf{L}}},\lambda))\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
which turns out to be parametrized via

\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},({{\mathbf{L}}},\lambda))\longleftrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}({{\mathbf{L}}},\lambda)/{{\mathbf{L}}}^{F})

[formula]

thanks to the Howlett–Lehrer–Lusztig theory of Hecke algebras (see for instance
[
GM
, Thm 3.2.5]
). This was generalized in the wake of the determination of
\ell
-blocks as partitions of
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
for a classical group
G
in characteristic
p\neq\ell
by Fong–Srinivasan
[
FoSr82
]
,
[
FoSr86
]
,
[
FoSr89
]
. In this generalization formalized by Broué–Malle–Michel in all types, see
[
BMM93
]
, one gets for any integer
d\geq 1
similar subsets of unipotent characters
\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},({{\mathbf{L}}},\lambda))
where
{{\mathbf{L}}}
(non
F
-split but still
F
-stable when
d\neq 1
) is the centralizer of a so-called
d
-torus

[
BM92
]
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
\lambda
is a so-called
d
-
cuspidal
unipotent character of
{{\mathbf{L}}}^{F}
. The above (
HC
) is then true up to replacing parabolic induction by the Lusztig functor
R_{{\mathbf{L}}}^{{\mathbf{G}}}
in the definition of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},({{\mathbf{L}}},\lambda))
.

In the meantime, a cluster of conjectures emerged around McKay’s, starting with Alperin’s weight conjecture
[
A87
]
. Considering Brauer characters of a finite group
X
with respect to the prime
\ell
, the conjecture posits that

|\text{IBr}(X)|=|\text{Alp}_{\ell}(X)|

[formula]

where Alp
{}_{\ell}(X)
is the set of
X
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(Q,\pi)
with
Q
an
\ell
-subgroup of
X
and
\pi
an element of
\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(Q)/Q)
with codegree
|{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(Q)|/|Q|\pi(1)
prime to
\ell
. Knörr and Robinson reduced (
AWC
) to a remarkable statement about ordinary characters, see
[
KnRo89
]
,
[
N
, Thm 9.24]
. This was in turn generalized by E.C. Dade into a broad conjecture
[
Da92
, Conj. 6.3]
implying both (
MK
) and (
AWC
), see
[
N
, Thms 9.26 and 9.27]
.

This and the many refinements brought to Dade’s conjecture lead Broué to introduce a strengthened version including all the extra refinements known in 2006
[
Br06
]
, which he referred to as MAKRODINU (an acronym from the names of the authors of
[
MK71
]
,
[
A87
]
,
[
KnRo89
]
,
[
Da92
]
,
[
IN02
]
,
[
Uno04
]
). See
[
Tu08
]
and
[
N04
]
for other refinements. All suggest equivalences of algebras of a geometric nature over
\ell
-adic rings of coefficients.
Indeed, Broué’s own conjecture on
\ell
-blocks with abelian defect
[
Br90a
, Conj. 6.1]
asserts an equivalence of derived module categories

D(B)\cong D(b)

[formula]

between an
\ell
-block
B
of a finite group with abelian defect and its Brauer correspondent
b
, where both blocks are seen as algebras over
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}_{\ell}
. See
[
Ok00
]
for the case of SL
{}_{2}(\ell^{m})
,
[
ChR08
, Thm 7.6]
for the case of symmetric groups,
[
CR13
, Thm 4.33]
for a proof in some cases of principal blocks using CFSG.

1.C
A conjecture made (quasi) simple

In reducing (
MK
) to a statement about simple groups, it seems impossible not to involve
quasisimple
groups, i.e. perfect groups
G
such that
G/\operatorname{Z}(G)
is simple. To illustrate why, consider even the alternating groups: characters of
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
(
n\geq 5
) are not of much help to find faithful characters of their double covers
2{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}
, let alone to prove counting statements about them.

In a major breakthrough, the reduction theorem by Isaacs–Malle–Navarro for McKay’s conjecture
[
IMN07
]
appeared in 2007. It introduces a so-called “inductive McKay condition”, formally stronger than (
MK
), which, once checked for a given
\ell
and all quasisimple groups
G
, implies (
MK
) for
\ell
and any finite group
X
. See the surveys
[
Ti14
]
,
[
KM19
]
,
[
Ma17b
]
and the book
[
N
]
for developments after
[
IMN07
]
. This condition was reformulated in terms of centrally isomorphic character triples, a notion devised by Navarro and the second author
[
NS14
]
, see
[
N
, Def 10.14]
.

Let us recall that a
character triple
is any
(A,X,\chi)
with finite groups
X\unlhd A
and
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
an irreducible character of
X
invariant under the conjugation action of
A
on
X
, a terminology due to Isaacs
[
Is
, Ch. 11]
. The inductive condition (
iMK
) for a finite group
X
and a prime
\ell
is as follows (see Definition
2.5
below for the relation
\geq_{c}
between character triples).

First, we assume the group theoretic condition that for a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup
S
of
X

there exists
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\leq N\leq X
such that
N
is stable under the stabilizer
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S}
of
S
in
\mathrm{Aut}(X)
, with
N\neq X
when
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\neq X
,
and second, we assume that

(iMK)

there exists a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection

\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)

such that
(X\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},X,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},N,\chi^{\prime})
whenever
\chi\mapsto\chi^{\prime}
.

The group
N
can be taken to be
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)
but it is important to keep the freedom in many quasisimple groups to choose a nicer overgroup
N
.
Then the Reduction Theorem
2.10
below simply states that for a given prime
\ell
, once (
iMK
) is checked for all quasisimple groups
X
, then it is true for any finite group, see
[
Ro23a
, Thm B]
.
It is clear by induction that (
iMK
) implies McKay’s equality (
MK
).

It is this (
iMK
) that we indeed prove for all finite groups and primes.

Theorem
\@upn
B

Let
X
be a finite group and
\ell
a prime. For any Sylow
\ell
-subgroup
S
of
X
and
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S}
there exists a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection

\Omega:\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)),

such that every
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)
satisfies

(X\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},X,\chi)\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S),\Omega(\chi)).\text{\ \ \ \ }

1.D
Quasisimple groups of Lie type

Whenever Out
(X)
is cyclic then (
iMK
) above boils down to the existence of an equivariant bijection
\chi\mapsto\chi^{\prime}
such that the restrictions of
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
to Z
(X)
have same irreducible constituent. This essentially reduces (
iMK
) for this type of quasisimple groups to cases previously checked for (
MK
), see
[
Ma08
, §3, §5]
.
It then remains to check (
iMK
) for quasisimple groups of Lie type, thus making the proof of McKay’s conjecture a Lie theoretic effort.

The reformulation of the inductive condition of
[
IMN07
]
in terms of character triple equivalences originated in
[
S12
]
, and the main application given there was to check (
iMK
) for quasisimple groups of Lie type whose defining characteristic is the same prime
\ell
.

After that, the main question is, of course, to prove (
iMK
) in the cases where
X
is a quasisimple group of Lie type in characteristic
p\neq\ell
.

Apart from a few exceptions, the universal coverings of simple groups of Lie type are of the form
G={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
where
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is a simple simply connected linear algebraic group endowed with a Frobenius endomorphism
F\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{{\mathbf{G}}}
defining
{{\mathbf{G}}}
over a finite field of order
q
, a power of
p
. To account for so-called diagonal automorphisms one defines the inclusion
G\unlhd{\widetilde{G}}
associated to a regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
of the algebraic groups. One also defines
E
acting on
{\widetilde{G}}
as a group of field and graph automorphisms of
G
, so that
G=[{\widetilde{G}},{\widetilde{G}}]
and
{\widetilde{G}}\rtimes E
induces the whole of
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
on
G
.

In such a situation, we define the integer
d_{\ell}(q)
as the multiplicative order of
q
mod
\ell
when
\ell\geq 3
, mod 4 when
\ell=2
. To check (
iMK
) an important idea from
[
Ma07
]
is to take for
N
the normalizer in
G={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
of a Sylow
d_{\ell}(q)
-torus
{\mathbf{S}}
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. The strong relation between
{\mathrm{N}}_{G}({\mathbf{S}})
and a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
G
is ensured by Broué–Malle’s results
[
BM92
]
and a remark of the first author
[
Ca94
]
. Let us mention that the relevance of
d_{\ell}(q)
-tori to the
\ell
-local analysis of
G
goes much deeper, see
[
Br06
, §4]
,
[
Ro23b
]
.

1.E
Proving (
iMK
) for the non-defining primes

Malle and the second author checked the inductive condition (
iMK
) for the prime 2 and all quasisimple groups in
[
MS16
]
, thus proving McKay’s equality (
MK
) for the prime
2
. Proving (
iMK
) for the prime 2 is made simpler by (
HC
) providing an equivariant bijection and by the fact that character triples
(A,X,\chi)
are easier to describe whenever
X
is perfect and
|A/X|
is prime to
\chi(1)
, see
[
Is
, 6.25]
.

Returning to the general case, the choice of a bijection

\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(G)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)

[formula]

has been described in
[
Ma07
]
drawing mainly on
[
BMM93
]
augmented with a discussion of character degrees in (
HC
) to give a common indexing set to both sides of
(\mathbf{\Omega})
, see also
[
S09
]
,
[
S10a
]
and
[
S10b
]
for the
N
-side. This choice of the map being relatively settled, the main effort to check (
iMK
) for quasisimple groups then adresses the control of the character triples on either side and the action of
\mathrm{Out}(G)
using a method introduced as
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
and recalled here through the variant
Proposition

2.12
.

Recall the choice of
N
as
{\mathrm{N}}_{G}({\mathbf{S}})
for
{\mathbf{S}}
a Sylow
d_{\ell}(q)
-torus of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. Through an elementary application of Clifford theory, the representations of
N
are strongly related with the ones of
{\mathrm{C}}_{G}({\mathbf{S}})
once certain extendibility questions are solved.

For any given
d\geq 1
and Sylow
d
-torus
{\mathbf{S}}
of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
we single out in
[
CS19
]
the following conditions where

\widetilde{N}={\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})\unrhd N={\mathrm{N}}_{G}({\mathbf{S}})\unlhd\widehat{N}={\mathrm{N}}_{GE}({\mathbf{S}}).

\mathbf{A}(d)
.
There is an
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(N)
where each element extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}
.

\mathbf{B}(d)
.
Every
\theta\in\mathrm{Irr}(N)\cup\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}))
extends to its stabilizer
\widetilde{N}_{\theta}
. In the case of
\theta\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}))
this can be done in an
\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N}/N)\rtimes{\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{G}}E}({\mathbf{S}})
-equivariant way.

For large values of
d
– forcing
{\mathbf{S}}=\{1\}
– the condition
\mathbf{A}(d)
becomes a quite challenging condition entirely about
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
that can also be written as follows (see
[
S23b
, Sect. 1.C]
):

\textbf{A}(\infty)
.
Any element of
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
has a
{\widetilde{G}}
-conjugate
\chi
such that
({\widetilde{G}}E)_{\chi}={\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}E_{\chi}
and
\chi
extends to
GE_{\chi}
.

For
\ell\geq 3
and prime to
q
, what has been said above explains why (
iMK
) for
G
and
\ell
is then implied by the conjunction of
\textbf{A}(\infty)
,
\textbf{A}(d_{\ell}(q))
and
\textbf{B}(d_{\ell}(q))
, see
[
CS19
, 2.4]
.

Condition
\textbf{A}(\infty)
was finally reached in all types as
[
S23b
, Thm A]
. As noted in
[
CS19
, 3.5]
the stabilizer statement in
\textbf{A}(\infty)
settles the question of determining the action of
\mathrm{Out}(G)
on
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
for all quasisimple groups
G
. This question has been a natural one since the completion of the CFSG. The answer, and in fact the stronger statement
A
(\infty)
, is expected to have applications to any counting conjecture, see already
[
FeSp23
]
or
[
Ru24
]
through
[
Ru22
]
. The proof of
A
(\infty)
for all types is spread in
[
CS17a
]
,
[
CS17b
]
,
[
CS19
]
,
[
S23a
]
,
[
S23b
]
(see also
[
Ta18
]
,
[
Ma17a
]
) with the main part being devoted to the types
\mathrm{D}
and
{}^{2}\!\mathrm{D}
. We refer to the introductions of those papers for the main issues raised and the methods used.

1.F
The present paper

The above leaves us to prove the conditions
\textbf{A}(d)
and
\textbf{B}(d)
for every
d\geq 1
and all groups
G
of Lie type. In types
not
\mathrm{D}
nor

{}^{2}\mathrm{D}
this is done in
[
CS17a
]
,
[
CS17b
]
,
[
CS19
]
, accounting for the most technical part of those texts. The main goal of the present paper is to show
\textbf{A}(d)
and
\textbf{B}(d)
in types
\mathrm{D}
and
{}^{2}\!\mathrm{D}
.

In Chapter
2
we recall more precisely than above the setting, the background results, and some of the main notation. In Section 2.A the results concern general character theory and in particular the existence of extensions of characters. Then we state the condition (
iMK
) in terms of centrally isomorphic character triples. We also recall the main criterion used in types already solved,
Proposition

2.12
being a slightly generalized form that will be useful later. General finite groups of Lie type are introduced in
Section

2.C
with their generators and automorphisms. We then define the conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
matching the criterion for (
iMK
) just mentioned. We also comment on the case of groups of type
\mathrm{D}
and the important inclusion
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
of a group of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
into a group of type
\mathrm{B}_{l}
with a common maximal torus.

In Chapter
3
we extend some of the results of
[
S23b
]
. We start by studying the centralizers of semisimple elements in
\mathrm{D}_{l,\text{sc}}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
for
l\geq 4
. This leads to further results on the characters of
\mathrm{D}_{l,\text{sc}}(q)
and
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}_{l,\text{sc}}(q)
. This includes groups of ranks
\leq 3
that will appear in our study of local subgroups and will help us to give a uniform treatment in the part of Chapter
5
dealing with characters. An important feature is the partition

\mathrm{Irr}(G)=\overline{\mathbb{T}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}\sqcup\mathbb{D}

deduced naturally from
A
(\infty)
in terms of stabilizers and extendibility of those characters with respect to
G\unlhd{\widetilde{G}}E
. In Sections
3.C
and
3.D
we get precise results on stabilizers and kernels of characters specific to each of the three subsets above, see
Theorem

3.16
.

In the proofs of the local Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
for types other than
\mathrm{D}
and
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}
, the discussion splits into two main cases according to whether or not the Levi subgroup
{\mathbf{C}}:=\mathrm{C}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})
is a torus, the solution in the latter case –
non-regular
d
’s
– usually using
\textbf{A}(\infty)
in smaller ranks. Here we will have to use more than just
\textbf{A}(\infty)
, drawing on Chapter
3
and
[
S23b
, Ch. 5]
, in a slightly different dichotomy also introducing an overgroup of
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
in Chapter
5
.

In Chapter 4, the Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
are proven first for integers
d\geq 3
that are additionally
doubly regular
for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.
The integer
d
is called doubly regular if
{\mathbf{C}}
and
\overline{{\mathbf{C}}}:=\mathrm{C}_{\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
are tori (hence equal) when
{\mathbf{S}}
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. The proof in that case is simpler than the usual regular case in other types and takes advantage of the case of type
\mathrm{B}_{l}
being already known from
[
CS19
]
.
This finally ensures (
iMK
) for quasisimple groups
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
or
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}_{l}
and primes
\ell
such that
d_{\ell}(q)
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
Theorem

4.1
.

In the non-doubly regular case, where
\overline{{\mathbf{C}}}
is not a torus, we bring here a simplification that would also simplify the proofs given for other types and helps keeping the case of types
\mathrm{D}
and
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}
to a reasonable size.
We introduce a finite group
M
, a subgroup of
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F^{\prime}}
where
F^{\prime}
is a version of
F
slightly altered to fit the technicality of the non-doubly regular case (notation is slightly different in Chapters 5 and 6). This group
M
is isomorphic to a subgroup
M^{\prime}
of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
containing
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}})
and normalizing
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}^{\prime}:={{\mathbf{G}}}\cap[\overline{{\mathbf{C}}},\overline{{\mathbf{C}}}]
and
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}^{\prime}:=[{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}^{\prime}),{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}^{\prime})]
.
The groups
{{\mathbf{K}}}^{\prime}_{i}{}^{F}
(
i=1,2
) are of types
\mathrm{D}
in possibly small ranks and their images
K_{i}
in
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F^{\prime}}
define a central product

K_{1}.K_{2}
which is normal of index
2
or
4
in
M
. We then use the knowledge of the character theory of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
gathered from
[
S23b
]
and Chapter
3
to derive crucial information about
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
. The groups
\widehat{M}
and
\widetilde{M}
being defined from
M
similarly to
\widehat{N}
and
\widetilde{N}
of
1.E
above from
N
, we establish a theorem that reads roughly as follows (the precise statement is
Theorem

5.20
).

Theorem
\@upn
C

There exists some
\widehat{M}
-stable
\widetilde{M}
-transversal
{\mathbb{T}}(M)
in
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
such that moreover any element of
{\mathbb{T}}(M)
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{M}
.

In fact a larger class of groups
M
with no reference to an integer
d
is defined in Section
5.A
. The relevance of this class of subgroups to the non-doubly regular case is given in the trichotomy of
Lemma

5.8
, showing how Theorem C along with
Theorem

4.1
and the known case of cyclic defect
[
KS16
]
essentially exhaust all cases to consider. The action of automorphisms on
M
is given in Section
5.B
. Afterwards, we study the stabilizers and extendibility of the characters of
M
in Section
5.C
.
The proof of Theorem C as
Theorem

5.20
is our Sections 5.D-E where we discuss several relevant subsets of
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
defined according to the restriction of characters to
K_{1}^{\prime}.K_{2}^{\prime}
and the various subsets
{\mathbb{T}}_{i}
,
{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}
, and
{\mathbb{D}}_{i}
of
\mathrm{Irr}(K_{i})
selected from the description given in
[
S23b
]
.

From there the end of the proof of Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
in Chapter
6
uses the fact that
d
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},F^{\prime})
where
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}
is a factor of a central product
{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}={{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}.{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}
of
F^{\prime}
-stable simple simply-connected groups of type
\mathrm{D}
. Let
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},F^{\prime})
. Thanks to Chapter 4, (
iMK
) holds in
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}^{F^{\prime}}
, thus providing a bijection
\Omega_{1}\colon\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(K_{1})\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}))
with strong properties in terms of the
\geq_{c}
relation, yielding more character correspondences and establishing a version of
\textbf{A}(\infty)
for
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{M}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F^{\prime}}
, see Section 6.A. This is completed in Section 6.C with results showing that indeed
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})\leq{\mathbf{M}}
and translating the results obtained for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F^{\prime})
into similar ones for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
with a special care for automorphisms. This essentially completes the proof of
\mathbf{A}(d)
. Meanwhile, Section 6.B establishes
\mathbf{B}(d)
by applying results on the Clifford theory for
({\mathbf{M}}^{\circ})^{F^{\prime}}
gathered in
Section
5.E
.

2
Background results

The aim of this chapter is twofold. On the one hand, we recall some notation used later, as well as the inductive McKay condition (
iMK
), with a criterion for verifying it in
2.D
.
We also state the relevant results for groups of Lie type that ensure part of the required assumptions. We conclude with some group-theoretic results for groups of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
.

2.A
Characters and extensions

Our notation tries to be as classical as possible, some being recalled in
[
S23b
, 1.A]
. We are dealing a lot with situations where
X\unlhd A
are finite groups and
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
is invariant under the conjugation action of
A
, i.e.
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)^{A}
. Extendibility is then a major issue.

Notation
\@upn
2.1

Let
X\unlhd A
be finite groups. Let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(X)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(A)
.

We denote by
{{\mathrm{Irr}(A\mid\mathcal{X})}}
the set of irreducible components of induced characters
\chi^{A}
for
\chi\in\mathcal{X}
, and by
{{\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\psi\right\rceil_{X})}}
the set of irreducible components of the restriction
\left.\psi\right\rceil_{X}
.

We say that
maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
for
\mathcal{X}
, if
\mathcal{X}
is
A
-stable and every
\chi\in\mathcal{X}
extends to its stabilizer
A_{\chi}
. When this occurs, an
extension map
is any
A
-equivariant
\Lambda\colon\mathcal{X}\to\sqcup_{X\leq I\leq A}\mathrm{Irr}(I)
such that for any
\chi\in\mathcal{X}
,
\Lambda(\chi)\in\mathrm{Irr}(A_{\chi})
is an extension of
\chi
. Such a map (in particular satisfying
A
-equivariance) always exists as soon as maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
for
\mathcal{X}
. When no set
\mathcal{X}
is specified, maximal extendibility with respect to
X\unlhd A
means it holds for
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Irr}(X)
.

We denote by
{{\mathrm{Lin}(X)}}
the set of linear characters of
X
.

Whenever maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
for a subset
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathrm{Irr}(X)
, let Cliff
(A\mid\mathcal{X})
be the set of
A
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(\chi,\eta)
with
\chi\in\mathcal{X}
and
\eta\in\mathrm{Irr}(A_{\chi}\mid 1_{X})
. After an extension map
\Lambda
has been chosen, Clifford theory (see for instance
[
N
, Sect. 1.8]
) leads to the bijection

\displaystyle\text{Cliff}(A\mid\mathcal{X})\ {\xrightarrow{\sim}}\ \mathrm{Irr}(A\mid\mathcal{X})\text{\ \ \ by \ \ \ }(\chi,\eta)\mapsto(\Lambda(\chi)\eta)^{A}.

(2.1)

This fact and its variants are key for exploring the characters of local subgroups such as the group
M
defined later in the paper.

Let us gather here some situations where extendibility is ensured.

Proposition
\@upn
2.2

Assume
X\unlhd A
.

(a)

If
A/X\operatorname{Z}(A)
is cyclic, then maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
.

(b)

If
A/X
is abelian and maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
, then for any subgroup
X\leq J\leq A
we have maximal extendibility with respect to
X\unlhd J
and to
J\unlhd A
.

(c)

Let
n\geq 1
and denote by
{{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}}
the corresponding symmetric group. If maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
for some set
\mathbb{T}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(X)
then it holds with respect to
X^{n}\unlhd A\wr{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
for the subset
\mathbb{T}^{n}:=\{\chi_{1}\times\dots\times\chi_{n}\mid\chi_{i}\in\mathbb{T}\}
of
\mathrm{Irr}(X^{n})
.

(d)

If
X
is abelian and
E\leq A
satisfies
A=XE
, then maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X\unlhd A
for
\{\lambda\in\mathrm{Lin}(X)\mid\lambda([E_{\lambda},E_{\lambda}]\cap X)=1\}
. The latter is the whole
\mathrm{Irr}(X)
when in addition maximal extendibility holds for
E\cap X\unlhd E
, e.g. when
A
is a semidirect product
A=X\rtimes E
.

(e)

Let
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
and assume
A=XV
where
V\leq A
is such that
\chi_{0}:=\left.\chi\right\rceil_{V\cap X}\in\mathrm{Irr}(V\cap X)
and extends to some
\widetilde{\chi}_{0}\in\mathrm{Irr}(V_{\chi})
. Then
\chi
extends to
A_{\chi}=XV_{\chi}
and there exists some
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(XV_{\chi})
extending
\chi
with
\left.\widetilde{\chi}\right\rceil_{V_{\chi}}={\widetilde{\chi}_{0}}
.

Proof.

For (a), use
[
Is
, Cor. 11.22]
. For (c), see
[
S23b
, Lem. 2.6]
. For (e), see
[
S10b
, Lem. 4.1]
.

In (b) only maximal extendibility with respect to
J\unlhd A
is nontrivial. But then we can use the fact that since
A/X
is abelian then maximal extendibility is equivalent to elements of
\mathrm{Irr}(A)
having multiplicity-free restrictions to
X
, see for example
[
S23b
, 1.A]
. Now restrictions to
J
can’t have any multiplicity
\geq 2
.

For (d) note first that
[X,E_{\lambda}]
is a subgroup of
\ker(\lambda)
. So if moreover
\lambda([E_{\lambda},E_{\lambda}]\cap X)=1
then indeed
[A_{\lambda},A_{\lambda}]\cap X\leq\ker(\lambda)
since
A_{\lambda}=XE_{\lambda}
. We can then change
X\unlhd A_{\lambda}
into
X/(X\cap[A_{\lambda},A_{\lambda}])\unlhd A_{\lambda}/[A_{\lambda},A_{\lambda}]
thus reducing the problem to the case when
A_{\lambda}
is abelian. The extension problem is then easy. When in addition maximal extendibility holds for
E\cap X\unlhd E
, then for any
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
, one has
\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{E\cap X}=\left.\widetilde{}\lambda\right\rceil_{E\cap X}
for some (linear) character
\widetilde{}\lambda
of
E_{\lambda}\leq E_{\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{E\cap X}}
and therefore
\lambda([E_{\lambda},E_{\lambda}]\cap X)=1
since
\widetilde{}\lambda([E_{\lambda},E_{\lambda}])=1
.
∎

When a semidirect product
B\rtimes C
acts on a group
X
we are interested in characters of
X
whose stabilizer in
BC
decomposes as
B^{\prime}C^{\prime}
with
B^{\prime}\leq B
and
C^{\prime}\leq C
. This property can be given a different formulation in terms of
C
-stable
B
-transversals (
[
S23a
, Lem. 2.4]
). See also the reformulation of the
\textbf{A}(\infty)
condition in
2.17
or
Proposition

2.12
(iii).

The following statement allows one to deduce an extension map from a given extension map for a given transversal.

Proposition
\@upn
2.3

Assume
X\unlhd A\unlhd\widehat{A}
with
X\unlhd\widehat{A}
. Let
\widehat{A}_{0}\leq\widehat{A}
with
\widehat{A}=A\widehat{A}_{0}
and
A=A_{0}X
for
A_{0}:=\widehat{A}_{0}\cap A
. Let
X_{0}:=\widehat{A}_{0}\cap X
and assume
A/A_{0}\cong X/X_{0}
abelian.

Assume maximal extendibility holds with respect to
X_{0}\unlhd X
and assume there is an
\widehat{A}_{0}
-equivariant extension map with respect to
X_{0}\unlhd A_{0}
for
\mathbb{T}
an
\widehat{A}_{0}
-stable
X
-transversal of
\mathrm{Irr}(X_{0})
.

Then
X\unlhd A
satisfies maximal extendibility with a
\mathrm{Lin}(A/A_{0})\rtimes\widehat{A}_{0}
-equivariant extension map
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. Namely when
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
,
a\in\widehat{A}_{0}
and
\lambda\in\mathrm{Lin}(A/A_{0})
is seen as character of
A
then

\widetilde{}\Lambda(\rho^{a})=\widetilde{}\Lambda(\rho)^{a}\text{\ \ and \ \ }\widetilde{}\Lambda(\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{X}\rho)=\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{A_{\chi}}\widetilde{}\Lambda(\rho).

Proof.
We essentially recall the proof of
[
CS19
, Thm 4.2]
.
Let
\Lambda
be the extension map with respect to
X_{0}\unlhd A_{0}
for
\mathbb{T}
. Let
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
. By the transversality property of
\mathbb{T}
,
\rho
lies over a unique character
\rho_{0}\in\mathbb{T}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(X_{0})
. Since maximal extendibility is assumed with respect to
X_{0}\unlhd X
, Clifford theory ensures that there exists an extension
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\in\mathrm{Irr}(X_{\rho_{0}})
of
\rho_{0}
with
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}^{X}=\rho
, see (
2.1
). Note that according to
Proposition

2.2
(e) there exists a common extension
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
and
\left.\Lambda(\rho_{0})\right\rceil_{A_{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}}
to
X_{\rho_{0}}A_{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}
. Then
\left.(\rho_{0}^{\prime})^{A_{\rho}}\right\rceil_{X}=(\left.\rho_{0}^{\prime}\right\rceil_{X_{\rho_{0}}})^{X}=\rho
since
A_{\rho}=XA_{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}
and
A_{\rho_{0}}=X_{\rho_{0}}(A_{0})_{\rho_{0}}
holds by the assumption on
\mathbb{T}
as recalled before the Proposition. So
\widetilde{\Lambda}(\rho):=(\rho_{0}^{\prime})^{A_{\rho}}
is an extension of
\rho
to
A_{\rho}
. It is then easy to check from its construction and the
\widehat{A}_{0}
-equivariance of
\Lambda
that
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is
\mathrm{Lin}(A/A_{0})\rtimes\widehat{A}_{0}
-equivariant.∎

\textstyle{\widehat{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{A}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{0}}

The following statement describes situations where the stabilizer of a character writes as a semidirect product and has extensions whose stabilizers have analogous properties.

Lemma
\@upn
2.4

Let
A
be a finite group,
X\unlhd A
and
\widetilde{X}\unlhd A
such that
X\leq\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}/X
is abelian. Suppose that there exists a subgroup
Y\leq A
such that
A=\widetilde{X}Y
,
X=Y\cap\widetilde{X}
and
Y/X
is abelian. Let
L\lhd A
with
L=J(Y\cap L)
for
J:=\widetilde{X}\cap L
and abelian
L/X
. Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
with
A_{\phi}=\widetilde{X}_{\phi}Y_{\phi}
. Assume that
\phi
extends to
\widetilde{X}_{\phi}
and to
(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}
for every
\widehat{\phi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(J_{\phi}\mid\phi)
.

(a)

Then
\phi
extends to
J_{\phi}

and every extension
\widehat{\phi}
of
\phi
to
J_{\phi}
satisfies

(A_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}=\widetilde{X}_{\widehat{\phi}}(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}.

(b)

Every
\kappa\in\mathrm{Irr}(J\mid\phi)
satisfies
(\widetilde{X}Y)_{\kappa}=\widetilde{X}_{\kappa}Y_{\kappa}
and extends to
JY_{\kappa}
.

(c)

Every
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(L\mid\phi)
satisfies
(\widetilde{X}Y)_{\chi}=\widetilde{X}_{\chi}Y_{\chi}
and extends to
LY_{\chi}
.

(d)

Let
z\in\operatorname{Z}(A)\cap Y
. Assume that
\phi
has an extension
\phi^{\prime}
to
(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}
with
z\in\ker(\phi^{\prime})
. Then every
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(L\mid\phi)
has an extension to
LY_{\chi}
that contains
z
in its kernel.

Note that
Y_{\phi}
in this situation stabilizes
J_{\phi}
and hence acts on
\mathrm{Irr}(J_{\phi}\mid\phi)
. Now
(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}
denotes the stabilizer of
\widehat{\phi}
in
Y_{\phi}
. By this construction
(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}
does not in general contain
J_{\phi}
, the group on which
\widehat{\phi}
is defined.

Proof.
We first prove part (a): As
J\leq\widetilde{X}
and
\phi
extends to
\widetilde{X}_{\phi}
by assumption it is clear that
\phi
extends to
J_{\phi}
.
Let
\beta\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{X}_{\phi})
be an extension of
\phi
. Then
\widehat{\phi}
and
\left.\beta\right\rceil_{J_{\phi}}
are extensions of
\phi
so
\widehat{\phi}=\lambda(\left.\beta\right\rceil_{J_{\phi}})
for some
\lambda\in\mathrm{Lin}(J_{\phi}/X)
. Since
\widetilde{X}/X
is abelian,
\lambda
is
\widetilde{X}_{\phi}
-invariant, but then
\lambda(\left.\beta\right\rceil_{J_{\phi}})=\widehat{\phi}
is also
\widetilde{X}_{\phi}
-invariant, i.e.,
\widetilde{X}_{\widehat{\phi}}=\widetilde{X}_{\phi}
. As

\widetilde{X}_{\phi}Y_{\phi}=A_{\phi}\geq(A_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}\geq\widetilde{X}_{\widehat{\phi}},

this implies
(A_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}=\widetilde{X}_{\widehat{\phi}}(Y_{\phi})_{\widehat{\phi}}
as required.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}
​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​ ​​​​
\textstyle{Y\cap L}

Let now
\kappa=(\widehat{\phi})^{J}
. As
\phi
extends to
Y_{\widehat{\phi}}
there exists some extension
\widetilde{\phi}
of
\widehat{\phi}
to
Z_{\widehat{\phi}}=J_{\phi}Y_{\widehat{\phi}}
for
Z=JY
according to
Proposition

2.2
(e). Because of
Z_{\widehat{\phi}}=J_{\widehat{\phi}}Y_{\widehat{\phi}}
, we have
JY_{\widehat{\phi}}=JY_{\kappa}=Z_{\kappa}
and
(\widetilde{\phi})^{JZ_{\widehat{\phi}}}
is an extension of
\kappa
to
Z_{\kappa}=JY_{\kappa}
. Then every extension of
{\widehat{\phi}}
to
Z_{\widehat{\phi}}=(JY)_{\widehat{\phi}}
defines by induction a character of
Z_{\kappa}=JY_{\kappa}
. This proves the statement in (b).

Since
Y/X
is abelian, we see that every extension
\widetilde{\kappa}
of
\kappa
to
L_{\kappa}
is
Y_{\kappa}
-invariant. The character
\chi=\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}
is irreducible and
Y_{\kappa}
-invariant. The group
A_{\widetilde{\kappa}}
contains
Y_{\widetilde{\kappa}}=Y_{\kappa}
. Recall
A_{\kappa}=\widetilde{X}_{\kappa}Y_{\kappa}
and hence

Y_{\kappa}=Y_{\widetilde{\kappa}}\leq A_{\widetilde{\kappa}}\leq A_{\kappa}=\widetilde{X}_{\kappa}Y_{\kappa}.

This shows
A_{\widetilde{\kappa}}=\widetilde{X}_{\widetilde{\kappa}}Y_{\widetilde{\kappa}}
, implying
A_{\chi}=\widetilde{X}_{\chi}Y_{\chi}
.
Let
\widehat{\kappa}
be an extension of
\widetilde{\kappa}
to
Z_{\kappa}
, which exists as
\kappa
extends to
Z_{\kappa}
and
Y/X
is abelian. Then
\widehat{\kappa}^{Z_{\kappa}L}
is irreducible and an extension of
\chi
. This proves part (c).

For part (d), by assumption we have that
\phi
extends to
Y_{{\widehat{\phi}}}
such that
z
is contained in the kernel of some extension or equivalenty that
\phi
extends to
Z_{{\widehat{\phi}}}/\left\langle z\right\rangle
. By the above construction,
\widehat{\phi}
extends to
J_{\phi}Y_{\widehat{\phi}}/\left\langle z\right\rangle
and hence
\kappa=(\widehat{\kappa})^{J}
extends to
Z_{\kappa}/\left\langle z\right\rangle
and accordingly
\chi=\widetilde{\kappa}^{L}
extends to
Z_{\chi}/\left\langle z\right\rangle
.
∎

2.B
Character triples and inductive McKay condition

Character triples
(A,X,\chi)
(where
X\unlhd A
and
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)^{A}
) and certain relations on them help characterizing the situations where
\chi
does not extend to
A
. See [Isa, 11.24] for some early occurrence of the notion and
Definition

2.5
below for the relation
{{\geq_{c}}}
. We recall more recent results and a reduction theorem for McKay’s conjecture in that language (see
[
N
]
,
[
S18
]
,
[
Ro23a
]
).

By a classical application of Schur’s lemma, each character triple
(A,X,\chi)
defines a projective representation
\mathcal{P}\colon A\to\operatorname{GL}_{\chi(1)}(\mathbb{C})
whose restriction to
X
is a linear representation affording the character
\chi
, see
[
Is
, Ch. 11]
,
[
N
, Def. 5.2]
.

Definition
\@upn
2.5

— Centrally isomorphic character triples
[
N
, Def. 10.14]
.

Let
(A,X,\chi)
and
(H,M,\chi^{\prime})
be two character triples with
{\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X)\leq H\leq A
,
A=XH
, and
H\cap X=M
. We write

(A,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(H,M,\chi^{\prime}),

if two projective representations
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
A
and
H
associated with
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
exist such that the factor sets of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
coincide on
H\times H
and such that for every
x\in{\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X)
, the matrices
\mathcal{P}(x)
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(x)
are scalar matrices to the same scalar.

When dealing with
\geq_{c}
the following lemma will be useful.

Lemma
\@upn
2.6

Let
X\unlhd A
and
H\leq A
with
A=XH
and
{\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X)\leq H
. We write
M:=X\cap H
. Let
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(X)
and
\chi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(M)
.

(a)

Then

(A,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(H,M,\chi^{\prime}),

if there exist
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(A)
and
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H)

with
\left.\widetilde{\chi}\right\rceil_{X}=\chi
,
\left.\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\right\rceil_{M}=\chi^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\widetilde{\chi}\right\rceil_{{\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X)})=\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\right\rceil_{{\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X)})
.

(b)

Assume
(A,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(H,M,\chi^{\prime})
. Let
\kappa\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{A}(X))
. Then
\chi
has an extension to
A
that belongs to
\mathrm{Irr}(A\mid\kappa)
if and only if
\chi^{\prime}
has an extension to
H
that belongs to
\mathrm{Irr}(H\mid\kappa)
.

(c)

Assume
(A,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(H,M,\chi^{\prime})
and let
J
be a group with
X\leq J\leq A
. Then
(J,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(J\cap H,M,\chi^{\prime})

Proof.

Parts (a) and (b) follow from Lemma 2.15 of
[
S18
]
. Part (c) is straightforward.
∎

Let us now recall the inductive McKay condition (
iMK
) from our introduction.

Definition
\@upn
2.7

(iMK)
Let
X
be a finite group,
\ell
a prime,
S
a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
X
and
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S}
. We say that
X

satisfies the inductive McKay condition
(
iMK
) for the prime
\ell
whenever

(a)

there exists
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\leq N\leq X
such that
N
is stable under
\Gamma
, with
N\neq X
when
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\neq X
, and

(b)

there exists a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection

\Omega_{X,\ell}\colon\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)

such that
(X\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},X,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},N,\Omega_{X,\ell}(\chi))
for any
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)
.

In our later considerations, when proving (
iMK
) for a given
X
it will be crucial to use the fact that it is known already for certain subgroups of
X
.

An important tool to deal with character triple equivalences is the following Butterfly Theorem
[
S18
, Thm 2.16]
,
[
N
, Thm 10.18]
. Among other things, it is crucial to navigate through various variants of (
iMK
).

Theorem
\@upn
2.8

Let
(A,X,\chi)
and
(H,M,\chi^{\prime})
be two character triples with

(A,X,\chi)\geq_{c}(H,M,\chi^{\prime}).

We assume that

X\unlhd A^{\prime}
and define
\kappa\colon A\to\mathrm{Aut}(X)
,
\kappa^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\to\mathrm{Aut}(X)
the maps induced by conjugation. If
\kappa(A)=\kappa^{\prime}(A^{\prime})
then

(A^{\prime},X,\chi)\geq_{c}(\kappa^{\prime}{}^{-1}\kappa(H),M,\chi^{\prime}).

Remark 2.9

(
Relation with
[
IMN07
]
)
.

Let
\ell
be a prime and let
X
be the universal covering of a finite simple group
\overline{X}:=X/\operatorname{Z}(X)
. As an application of the above one can see that
\overline{X}
satisfies the inductive McKay condition of
[
IMN07
, §10]
for
\ell
if, and only if,
X
satisfies the condition (
iMK
) of
Definition

2.7
for
\ell
. See also a version of the reduction theorem of
[
IMN07
]
using the
\geq_{c}
relation in
[
N
, Def. 10.23, Thm 10.26]
.

Indeed, as explained in
[
S12
, Prop. 2.3]
,
\overline{X}
satisfies the conditions (1-8) of
[
IMN07
, §10]
if and only if the group theoretic conditions of
Definition

2.7
(a) are satisfied by
X
,
\ell
, a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup
S
of
X
, along with a subgroup
N
, and there is an
\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S}
-equivariant bijection
\Omega\colon\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)
such that each pair
(\chi,\Omega(\chi))
satisfies the condition (
cohom
) introduced in Definition 2.4 of
[
S12
]
. The latter means that there exists a group
\overline{A}
with
\overline{G}:=X/Z\unlhd\overline{A}
for
Z:=\ker(\chi)\cap{\operatorname{Z}(G)}=\ker(\Omega(\chi))\cap{\operatorname{Z}(G)}
such that, by the construction in the proof of
[
S12
, Prop. 2.8]
,
\overline{A}
induces the whole
\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi}
on
X/Z
, and the characters
\overline{\chi}
and
\overline{\Omega(\chi)}
of
X/Z
and
\overline{N}:=N/Z
associated with
\chi
and
\Omega(\chi)
have
extensions
to
\overline{A}
, resp.
{\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{A}}(\overline{N})
lying above the same
{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{\overline{A}}(\overline{G}))
. According to
Lemma

2.6
(a) and thanks also to the group theoretic properties of
N
and
X
, this condition (
cohom
) is equivalent to the existence of an overgroup
\overline{A}
of
\overline{G}
inducing
\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi}
on
X/Z
and such that

(\overline{A},\overline{G},\overline{\chi})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{A}}(\overline{N}),\overline{N},\overline{\Omega(\chi)}).

But then the Butterfly
Theorem

2.8
implies that

((X/Z)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi},X/Z,\overline{\chi})\geq_{c}((N/Z)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi},N/Z,\overline{\Omega{}(\chi)}).

It is then trivial to lift that into the relation

(X\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi},X,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S,\chi},N,\Omega{}(\chi))

defining (
iMK
).

The above is key to rephrase Isaacs–Malle–Navarro’s reduction theorem on the McKay conjecture as the following criterion taken from
[
Ro23a
]
.

Theorem
\@upn
2.10

Let
\ell
be a prime.
If any universal covering
X
of a finite nonabelian simple group (see
[
GLS
, Def 5.1.1]
) satisfies the above (
iMK
) for
\ell
, then any finite group satisfies it.

Proof.

To see the equivalence between this and
[
Ro23a
, Thm B]
we must, for
X
the universal covering of a nonabelian simple group, replace the overgroup
X\rtimes\Gamma
in our (
iMK
) by any overgroup
A
. As noted in the comment after Conjecture A in
[
Ro23a
]
, this is a straightforward application of the above
Lemma

2.6
(c) and the Butterfly
Theorem

2.8
.∎

Theorem
\@upn
2.11

Let
\ell
be a prime and
X
the universal covering of a finite simple group
\overline{X}
. If
\ell\geq 5
, assume
\overline{X}
is not a group of Lie type
\mathrm{D}
or
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}
in characteristic
\neq\ell
. Then
X
satisfies the above (
iMK
) for
\ell
.

Proof.

By the Classification of Finite Simple Groups, a finite nonabelian simple group is either an alternating group, a sporadic group or a simple group of Lie type, see
[
Asch
, Ch. 47]
. We have seen in
Remark

2.9
that for the universal cover
X
of a simple group and a prime
\ell
, the condition (
iMK
) is equivalent to the simple group
X/\operatorname{Z}(X)
satisfying the inductive McKay condition of
[
IMN07
]
. This is the condition checked in
[
Ma08
, Thm 3.1 and Thm 5.1]
for alternating groups and sporadic groups. For simple groups of Lie type in characteristic
\ell
, this is checked as
[
S12
, Thm 1.1]
. For
\ell=2
and all types, this is the main result of
[
MS16
]
. For
\ell=3
and all types, see
[
S23b
, Thm C]
and its proof. For other cases of groups of Lie type,
[
IMN07
, Ch. 16 and 17]
covers the types
{}^{2}\mathrm{B}_{2}
and
{}^{2}\mathrm{G}_{2}
,
[
CS13
, Thm A]
covers the types
{}^{3}\mathrm{D}_{4}
,
\mathrm{E}_{8}
,
\mathrm{F}_{4}
,
{}^{2}\mathrm{F}_{4}
, and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
,
[
CS17a
]
the types
\mathrm{A}
and
{}^{2}\mathrm{A}
,
[
CS17b
]
the type
\mathrm{C}
,
[
CS19
, Thm A]
the types
\mathrm{B}
,
\mathrm{E}_{6}
,
{}^{2}\mathrm{E}_{6}
and
\mathrm{E}_{7}
. This clearly leaves out only types
\mathrm{D}
or
{}^{2}\mathrm{D}
for primes
\ell\geq 5
different from the defining characteristic.
∎

In the cases reviewed in the above proof, (
iMK
) was mostly ensured via the criterion
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
leading to the conditions
A
(\infty)
,
A
(d)
and
B
(d)
described in the Introduction, see also
Theorem

2.21
. At some point we will need the following slight reinterpretation of
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
.

Proposition
\@upn
2.12

Let
{\widetilde{G}}\unrhd G\geq N
and
E
be finite groups, such that
E
acts on
{\widetilde{G}}
normalizing
G
,

{\mathrm{N}}_{G}(N)=N
,
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{G}}\rtimes E}(G)=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})
,
G\rtimes E=G\widehat{N}
and
{\widetilde{G}}=G\widetilde{N}
,
where
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{G}}(N)
and
\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{GE}(N)
.
Let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(G)
and
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(N)
be
\widetilde{N}\widehat{N}
-stable. Assume the following:

(i)

The quotient
{\widetilde{G}}/G
is abelian. Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
G\unlhd{\widetilde{G}}
for
\mathcal{G}
and with respect to
N\unlhd\widetilde{N}
for
\mathcal{N}
.

(ii)

Denoting
\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}:=\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{G}}\mid\mathcal{G})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}:=\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N}\mid\mathcal{N})
, there exists some
\mathrm{Lin}({\widetilde{G}}/G)\rtimes\widehat{N}
-equivariant bijection

\widetilde{\Omega}:\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{N}},

such that
\widetilde{\Omega}\big(\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}\cap\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{G}}\mid\xi)\big)=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\cap\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N}\mid\xi)
for every
\xi\in\mathrm{Irr}(\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}}))
.

(iii)

There exists some
E
-stable
{\widetilde{G}}
-transversal
\mathcal{G}_{0}
in
\mathcal{G}
, such that every
\chi\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
extends to
GE_{\chi}
.

(iv)

There exists some
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal
\mathcal{N}_{0}
in
\mathcal{N}
, such that every
\psi\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
extends to
\widehat{N}_{\psi}
.

Then there exists some
\widetilde{N}\widehat{N}
-equivariant bijection

\Omega:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N},

such that

(({\widetilde{G}}E)_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}((\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi},N,\Omega(\chi)){\text{\quad\quad for every }}\chi\in\mathcal{G}.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the one of Theorem 2.12 in
[
S12
]
. Let us comment on the differences between the two statements.
In
[
S12
]
the focus was on the characters in
\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(G)
for some prime
\ell
but the degree of the characters did not play any role in the arguments used. The group theoretical assumptions in
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
are also related to a prime
\ell
and the corresponding Sylow subgroup but they are only used through the fact that
N
is self-normalizing in
G
along with
GE=G\widehat{N}
and
{\widetilde{G}}=G\widetilde{N}
in
{\widetilde{G}}E
. The assumptions made in (iii) and (iv) are equivalent to the original assumptions of
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
in terms of stabilizers thanks to
[
S23a
, Lemma 2.4]
.

The proof of
[
S12
, Thm 2.12]
then yields a
\widetilde{N}\widehat{N}
-equivariant bijection
\Omega:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
such that for any
\chi\in\mathcal{G}
the pair
(\chi,\Omega(\chi))
satisfies the condition (
cohom
) introduced in Definition 2.4 of
[
S12
]
. Then our discussion is very similar to the one given in
Remark

2.9
above. The condition (
cohom
) implies then that there exists a group
\overline{A}
with
\overline{G}:=G/Z\unlhd\overline{A}
for
Z:=\ker(\chi)\cap{\operatorname{Z}(G)}
such that
\overline{A}
has the same image as
G(\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi}
in
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
, and the characters
\overline{\chi}
and
\overline{\Omega(\chi)}
of
\overline{G}
and
\overline{N}:=N/Z
, deflations of
\chi
and
\Omega(\chi)
, satisfy

(\overline{A},\overline{G},\overline{\chi})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{A}}(\overline{N}),\overline{N},\overline{\Omega(\chi)}).

Now taking into account the Butterfly Theorem
2.8
we get

\displaystyle((\widetilde{G}E)_{\chi}/Z,G/Z,\overline{\chi})

\displaystyle\geq_{c}((\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi}/Z,N/Z,\overline{\Omega(\chi)}).

The sought for relation
(({\widetilde{G}}E)_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}((\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi},N,\Omega(\chi))
is then an easy consequence of the definition of
\geq_{c}
.
∎

2.C
Groups of Lie type, generators and automorphisms

We now make more precise the notation for
G
,
{\widetilde{G}}
,
E
and the conditions
A
(\infty)
,
A
(d)
and
B
(d)
from our Introduction. Additional notation will be given in
Section

2.E
for type
\mathrm{D}
.

Let
{p}
be a prime,
{q}=p^{f}
for some
{f}\geq 1
,
{{{\mathbb{F}}}}
be an algebraic closure of
{{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}}
, the field with
q
elements. We consider
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}}}
a simple simply connected linear algebraic group over
{\mathbb{F}}
with the choice of a maximal torus and a Borel subgroup
{{{\mathbf{T}}}}\leq{\mathbf{B}}
, thus fixing a root system
{{\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})}}
with a basis
{{\Delta}}
. There is a presentation by the generators
{{\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t_{1})}}
,
{{\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(t_{2})}}:=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t_{2})\mathbf{x}_{-{\alpha}}(-t_{2}^{-1})\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t_{2})\in{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
and
{{\mathbf{h}_{\alpha}(t_{2})}}:=\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(t_{2})\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)^{-1}\in{\mathbf{T}}
for
{\alpha}\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
,
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}
, and
t_{2}\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
, subject to the Chevalley relations, see
[
GLS
, Thm 1.12.1]
. In particular the commutator formula
[
GLS
, Thm 1.12.1(b)]
has consequences that we use repeatedly, see also
2.24
(d) below.

\@upn
2.13

If
{\alpha},\beta\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
are such that
(\mathbb{Z}{\alpha}+\mathbb{Z}\beta)\cap\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})=\{\pm{\alpha},\pm\beta\}
, then

[\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t),\mathbf{x}_{\beta}(t^{\prime})]=1

for any
t,t^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{F}}
. This is in particular the case if
{\alpha}\perp\beta
in type
\mathrm{D}
, or in type
\mathrm{B}
with
{\alpha}\perp\beta
and
\beta
a long root.

We denote by
{{F_{p}}}
the bijective endomorphism of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
sending any
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t)
to
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t^{p})
. We denote by
{{E({{\mathbf{G}}})}}
the group of abstract group automorphisms of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
generated by
F_{p}
and the graph automorphisms of type
\mathbf{x}_{{\epsilon}\delta}(t)\mapsto\mathbf{x}_{{\epsilon}\delta^{\prime}}(t)
for
t\in{\mathbb{F}}
,
\delta\in\Delta
,
{\epsilon}\in\{1,-1\}
and
\delta\mapsto\delta^{\prime}
is a symmetry of the Dynkin diagram of
\Delta
.

We denote by
{{F\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{{\mathbf{G}}}}}
a Frobenius endomorphism defining some
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-structure on
{{\mathbf{G}}}
preserving
{\mathbf{T}}\leq{\mathbf{B}}
, namely
F=F_{p}^{f}\circ\sigma
with
\sigma
some graph automorphism (possibly trivial) as above. We denote by
{{\mathcal{L}}}
the Lang map on
{{\mathbf{G}}}
defined by
\mathcal{L}(g)=g^{-1}F(g)
for
g\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
.

Let
{{E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}}
be the image of
{\mathrm{C}}_{E({{\mathbf{G}}})}(F)
in
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
by restrictions to
G={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
. The kernel of the latter is the subgroup generated by
F
(see
[
GLS
, Lem. 2.5.7]
), so for instance stabilizers
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{{\mathbf{S}}}
for
F
-stable subsets
{\mathbf{S}}
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
make sense.

We assume chosen a so-called regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}}
with
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
also defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
with Frobenius endomorphism
F
extending the one of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
, and such that
\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})
is connected and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}={{\mathbf{G}}}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})
. We can assume that the action of
E({{\mathbf{G}}})
extends to
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
, see
[
MS16
, Sect. 2]
or
[
GM
, Prop. 1.7.5]
. The action of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
on
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
by conjugation provides all diagonal automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
, while
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}\rtimes E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
can be formed and induces the whole
\mathrm{Aut}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
on
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
in the case where
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is quasisimple not of type
\mathrm{B}_{2}
,
\mathrm{G}_{2}
or
\mathrm{F}_{4}
, see
[
GLS
, Thm 2.5.12]
.

Notation
\@upn
2.14 — Diagonal automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
.

Concerning
\mathrm{Out}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
, note that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
(see
[
B06
, Lem. 6.1]
) with
{{\mathbf{G}}}\cap\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
. Therefore
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}/{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=({{\mathbf{G}}}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}))^{F}/{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
is isomorphic to the group of cofixed points
{{{\widetilde{Z}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}}:=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
by Lang’s theorem, the map being explicitly
gz\mapsto g^{-1}F(g)[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
whenever
g\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
z\in\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})
with
gz\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
. Thus, the group
{\widetilde{Z}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})\rtimes E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
acts on
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
.

Note that
|\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}|=|\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]|
and indeed since
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
is either cyclic or of order 4, the two groups
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
and
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
are isomorphic even as
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
-groups.

We often abbreviate
{{G}}:={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
{{{\widetilde{G}}}}:={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
.

Notation
\@upn
2.15 — Overgroups
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.792pt$\hss\resizebox{379736}{235746}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.792pt$\hss\resizebox{379736}{235746}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.792pt$\hss\resizebox{314749}{198790}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.792pt$\hss\resizebox{185499}{114688}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}
.

A slightly different way of dealing with diagonal automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is as follows, see
[
S23a
, Rem. 2.16(a)]
. From the fact that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
(and therefore
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
) recalled above, it is easy to see that
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}))\leq{{\mathbf{G}}}
. We set

{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})).

Observe more generally that
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(1)\unlhd\mathcal{L}^{-1}(Z)\unlhd{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}
with
|\mathcal{L}^{-1}(Z)/{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}|=|Z|
for any subgroup
Z\leq\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
. Moreover

\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
induces on
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
the whole group of diagonal automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
since
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}/\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})\cong({{\mathbf{G}}}/\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}))^{F}
by the natural map. We denote by
E({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}})\leq\mathrm{Aut}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}})
the restriction of
{\mathrm{C}}_{E({{\mathbf{G}}})}(F)
to
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}
. Then, in a way similar to
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}\rtimes E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
, the overgroup
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\rtimes E({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}})
induces the whole
\mathrm{Aut}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
on
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
. Note however that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}E(G)}(G)=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
while
{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{201988}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{144277}{89202}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}E(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{201988}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{144277}{89202}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})}(G)=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})\left\langle\left.F\right\rceil_{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{201988}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{144277}{89202}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\right\rangle
.

Remark 2.16
.

Note that in the above construction, one has
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\leq{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F^{e}}
for
e
the exponent of the finite group
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})\rtimes E^{\prime}
where
E^{\prime}
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}))
generated by
\left.F\right\rceil_{\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})}
. Indeed, if
g\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
g^{-1}F(g)=z\in\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
, then
g^{-1}F^{e}(g)=zF(z)\dots F^{e-1}(z)
can be written as
(zF)^{e}F^{-e}
in
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})\rtimes E^{\prime}
.

2.D
The conditions (
iMK
),
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)

Following the road map suggested by
Proposition

2.12
and starting with
G={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
as in the preceding section, we review the choices of groups
{\widetilde{G}}
,
E
,
N
to be made and how the assumptions translate for a quasisimple group of Lie type.
The choice for the group
{\widetilde{G}}
is then obviously
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
, while
E
is
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
.

Assumption
2.12
(iii) has the following generalization, introduced in
[
CS17b
]
.

\@upn
2.17 — Condition.

{{\mathbf{A}(\infty)}}
:

There exists some
E(G)
-stable
{\widetilde{G}}
-transversal
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, where every
\chi\in\mathbb{T}
extends to
GE(G)_{\chi}
.

For the equivalence between the above version of
A
(\infty)
and the version in terms of stabilizers used in the Introduction, see
[
S23a
, Lem. 2.4]
.

Theorem
\@upn
2.18
—
[
S23b
, Theorem A]
.

Condition
\mathbf{A}(\infty)
holds for any simple simply connected
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and Frobenius endomorphism
F
.

Let us fix
d\geq 1
. Recall the notion of (
F
-stable)
d
-tori in
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
[
MT
, Def. 25.6]
and the corresponding Sylow theory.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow (i.e. maximal)
d
-torus in
{{\mathbf{G}}}
.
Let
{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
,
{\widehat{N}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}({\mathbf{S}})
,
{\widetilde{N}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
{\widetilde{C}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
. We recall the following conditions already seen in our introduction:

\@upn
2.19 — Condition
{{\mathbf{A}(d)}}
.

There exists some
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal
\mathbb{M}
in
\mathrm{Irr}(N)
, where every
\chi\in\mathbb{M}
extends to
({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{{\mathbf{S}},\chi}
.

\@upn
2.20 — Condition
{{\mathbf{B}(d)}}
.

(a)

Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N\unlhd\widetilde{N}
and
\widetilde{C}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
.

(b)

There exists some
\mathrm{Lin}({\widetilde{G}}/G)\rtimes\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map
\widetilde{\Lambda}
with respect to
\widetilde{C}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
.

For
\ell
an odd prime not dividing
q
, denote by
{{d:=d_{\ell}(q)}}
the multiplicative order of
q
in
(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
. Then the strong relation between
d
-tori and
\ell
-subgroups allows us to choose
N
as above to apply
Proposition

2.12
with conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
essentially completing the assumptions
2.12
(i) and (iv). We get

Theorem
\@upn
2.21
—
[
CS19
, Thm 2.4]
.

Assume that
\ell
is a prime with
\ell\nmid 6q
,
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}/\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is simple and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is its universal covering group. Assume
B
(d_{\ell}(q))
and
A
(d_{\ell}(q))
are satisfied. Then the condition (
iMK
) of
Definition

2.7
holds for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
\ell
, taking for
N
the subgroup
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
where
{\mathbf{S}}
is a Sylow
d_{\ell}(q)
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.

Proof.

Since
A
(\infty)
is always satisfied thanks to
Theorem

2.18
,
[
CS19
, Thm 2.4]
tells us that the inductive McKay Condition of
[
IMN07
]
is satisfied for the simple group
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}/\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
and the prime
\ell
with the above choice of
N
. But we have seen that it is equivalent to (
iMK
) in
Remark

2.9
.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

If
d\in\{1,2\}
,
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
and
F
is a Frobenius endomorphism of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
defining it over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, then the Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
are known for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
[
MS16
, Thm 3.1]
.

2.E
The curious case of types
\mathrm{D}_{l}
and
\mathrm{B}_{l}

In addition to what has been said about general groups
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
with
{{\mathbf{G}}}
simple simply connected, we introduce here more notation, in particular related to the inclusion
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
where
{{\mathbf{G}}}
has type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
and
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
type
B_{l}
for some
l\geq 4
, see also
[
S10a
, 10.1]
,
[
MS16
, 2.C]
,
[
S23a
, Sect. 2.1]
.

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}
be the orthonormal basis of the
l
-dimensional Euclidean vector space
\oplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{R}e_{i}
(
l\geq 4
). Set
{{{\underline{l}}}}=\{1,\dots,l\}
and

{{\Phi}}:=\{\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}\mid i,j\in{\underline{l}},i\neq j\}\subseteq{{{\overline{\Phi}}}}:=\{\pm e_{i},\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}\mid i,j\in{\underline{l}},i\neq j\}.

These are root systems of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
and
\mathrm{B}_{l}
, respectively, with bases
{\Delta}:=\{{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},\ldots,{\alpha}_{l}\}
and
{{\overline{\Delta}}}:=\{\overline{{\alpha}}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},\ldots,{\alpha}_{l}\}
, where
{\alpha}_{1}:=e_{1}+e_{2}
,
\overline{{\alpha}}_{1}:=e_{1}
and
{\alpha}_{i}:=e_{i}-e_{i-1}
(
i\geq 2
), see
[
GLS
, Rem. 1.8.8]
. Let
{{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}}}:=\mathrm{B}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
be the simple simply connected linear algebraic group with root system
\overline{\Phi}=\Phi({\overline{\mathbf{G}}},\overline{{\mathbf{T}}})
for some maximal torus
\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}
. We recall
{\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t_{1})}
,
{{\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(t_{2})}}
and
{{\mathbf{h}_{\alpha}(t_{2})}}
its Chevalley generators, where
{\alpha}\in\overline{\Phi}
,
t_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}
and
t_{2}\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
. We denote by
{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}
the group
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}({\mathbb{F}})
,
{{\overline{{\mathbf{N}}}}}:=\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(t)\mid{\alpha}\in\overline{\Phi},t\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}\right\rangle={\mathrm{N}}_{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}}(\overline{{\mathbf{T}}})
and

{{{\overline{W}}}}:=\overline{{\mathbf{N}}}/\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}
the Weyl group of
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
together with the canonical epimorphism
{{{\overline{\rho}}:\overline{{\mathbf{N}}}\longrightarrow\overline{W}}}
. As explained in
[
S10a
, 10.1]
and
[
MS16
, 2.C]
the subgroup
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}}}:=\left\langle\left.{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\vphantom{{\alpha}\in\Phi}\hskip 1.00006pt\right.\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi\right\rangle
is a simply connected simple group over
{\mathbb{F}}
with same maximal torus
\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}={\mathbf{T}}
and the root system
\Phi=\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
. We set
{{{\mathbf{N}}}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
,

{{{W}}}:={\mathbf{N}}/{\mathbf{T}}
and the surjection
{{\rho}}:{\mathbf{N}}\longrightarrow W
.

For
I\subseteq{\underline{l}}
and
\zeta\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
we set
{{\mathbf{h}_{I}(\zeta):=\prod_{i\in I}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(\zeta)}}
.

We assume chosen
{{\varpi}}\in{\mathbb{F}^{\times}}
with
\varpi^{2}=-1
(note that
\varpi=1
when
p=2
). We set
{{n_{1}^{\circ}:=\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(\varpi)}}\in{\mathrm{N}}_{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
. The Chevalley relations give easily the following statement.

Lemma
\@upn
2.23
—
[
MS16
, 2.C]
.

Let
{{\gamma}}:{{\mathbf{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}
be the graph automorphism defined by
\mathbf{x}_{\epsilon{\alpha}_{i}}(t)\mapsto\mathbf{x}_{\epsilon{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime}}(t)
for
t\in{\mathbb{F}}
,
{\epsilon}=\pm 1
,
i\in{\underline{l}}
and
({\alpha}_{1}^{\prime},{\alpha}_{2}^{\prime},{\alpha}_{3}^{\prime},{\alpha}_{4}^{\prime},\dots,{\alpha}_{l}^{\prime}):=({\alpha}_{2},{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{3},{\alpha}_{4},\dots,{\alpha}_{l})
.
Then
n_{1}^{\circ}
normalizes
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and induces
\gamma
on it, namely
\gamma(x)=x^{n_{1}^{\circ}}
for any
x\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
.

Recall
{f}\geq 1
,
{q}=p^{f}
, and let
F\in\{F_{p}^{f},F_{p}^{f}\circ\gamma\}
a Frobenius endomorphism of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. Then in the standard notation
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}^{\epsilon}(p^{f})
where
{{{\epsilon}}}=1
or
-1
according to
F=F_{p}^{f}
or
F_{p}^{f}\circ\gamma
. We choose an extension of
F
to
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
as follows. If
F=F_{p}^{f}
then
\overline{F}
is defined the same on the generators
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}(t)
as in the preceding section. By contrast when
F=F_{p}^{f}\circ\gamma
we define
{{\overline{F}}}:=F_{p}^{f}\circ\overline{\gamma}
where
\overline{\gamma}
is the inner automorphism of
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
defined by
\overline{\gamma}(x)=x^{n_{1}^{\circ}}
for
x\in{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
. In both cases
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{\overline{F}}=\mathrm{B}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(p^{f})
.

We denote by
{{\underline{E}({{\mathbf{G}}})}}
the subgroup of
E({{\mathbf{G}}})
generated by
F_{p}
and
\gamma
.
We denote by
{{{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}}
,
respectively
{{\underline{E}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})}}
, the corresponding subgroup of
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
, respectively
E(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})
.

We describe below properties related to the centers of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
. These can easily be deduced from
[
GLS
, Thm 1.12.6]
.

\@upn
2.24

(a)

According to
[
GLS
, Table 2.2]
the center of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is the
2
-group generated by
\mathbf{h}_{\underline{l}}(\varpi)
and
{{h_{0}}}:=\mathbf{h}_{{\alpha}_{1}}(-1)\mathbf{h}_{{\alpha}_{2}}(-1)=\mathbf{h}_{e_{1}}(-1)=(n_{1}^{\circ})^{2}
.

(b)

We have
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle=[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),\gamma]=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}))=\operatorname{Z}({\overline{\mathbf{G}}})
.

(c)

|\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})|=4
if and only if
p\neq 2
and

(i)

{\epsilon}=1
and
4\mid(q-1)l
; or

(ii)

{\epsilon}=-1
and
4\nmid(q-1)l
, in particular
2\nmid f
.

In all other cases:
|\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})|=\gcd(2,q-1)
.

(d)

If
{\alpha},\beta\in\Phi({\overline{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
