are both short roots and
{\alpha}\perp\beta
, then
[\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(t),\mathbf{n}_{\beta}(t^{\prime})]=h_{0}
for any
t,t^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{F}^{\times}}
, see
[
S07
, Bem. 2.1.7]
.

3
Centralizers of semisimple elements and consequences for
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})

From now on the groups
{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
G={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
are as defined in
Section

2.E
and we use the notation introduced there for groups of type
\mathrm{D}
.

The aim of this chapter is to complement the results of
[
S23b
]
on elements of
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
that don’t extend to their stabilizer in
G{\underline{E}}(G)
. We introduce the sets
{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathbb{D}
in
Definition

3.9
that will be of constant use in Chapter
5
. Through the equivariant Jordan decomposition of characters of
[
S23b
, Thm B]
, analyzing elements of
{\mathbb{E}}\cup\mathbb{D}
leads us to a study of centralizers of semisimple elements in the adjoint group of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. This extends the results of
[
CS24
]
. It will be also of some use in our study of relative Weyl groups in
Section

4.D
.

Our main theorem is
Theorem

3.16
dealing with kernel and stabilizers of characters outside the transversal
\mathbb{T}
from
Theorem

2.18
. This includes a study of characters of groups of rank
1\leq k\leq 3
, called
G_{\underline{k}}
in
3.D
, that occur naturally in centralizers of Sylow
d
-tori.

3.A
Centralizers of semisimple elements

We describe here properties of the centralizers of semisimple elements
s_{0}\in\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
. We keep
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
,
F
,
{\mathbf{T}}
, and the notation for the associated roots as in
Section

2.E
.

Notation
\@upn
3.1

Let
\pi\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{{\mathbf{H}}}
be the adjoint quotient of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
with
F_{p}
,
\gamma
and
F
acting accordingly also on
{{\mathbf{H}}}
. Let
{{\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}}}\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})=\text{Spin}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})\to{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
be the the natural morphism with kernel
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
, see
2.24
.

For every
I\subseteq\underline{l}
we set
{{\overline{R}_{I}}}:=\overline{\Phi}\cap\left\langle e_{i}\mid i\in I\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},\,{{R_{I}}}:=\Phi\cap\overline{R}_{I}=\Phi\cap\left\langle e_{i}\mid i\in I\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{{{\mathbf{T}}_{I}}}:={\mathbf{T}}\cap\left\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in\overline{R}_{I}\right\rangle
.

Remark 3.2
.

(a)

Whenever
t_{1},\dots,t_{l}\in{\mathbb{F}^{\times}}
, then
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}(\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i}))
has the eigenvalues
\{t_{i}^{\pm 2}\mid i\in{\underline{l}}\}
as an element of
{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
, see the description of
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}(\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i}))
in
[
GLS
, 2.7]
.

(b)

Assume
p
is odd. Let
s_{0}\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
be semisimple. According to
[
FoSr89
, 2A]
,
s_{0}
and
s_{0}h_{0}
are
{{\mathbf{G}}}
-conjugate if and only if
1
and
-1
are both eigenvalues of
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}(s_{0})
. Using then (a) above, an element
s_{0}\in{\mathbf{T}}
is
{{\mathbf{G}}}
-conjugate to
s_{0}h_{0}
if and only if
s_{0}
can be written as
\prod_{i}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})
with
\{1,\varpi\}\subseteq\{\pm t_{i}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in{\underline{l}}\}
.

\@upn
3.3 — Weyl groups and parabolic subgroups.

We denote by
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
the subgroup of the permutation group of
{\underline{l}}\cup-{\underline{l}}
whose elements
\sigma
satisfy
\sigma(-x)=-\sigma(x)
for any
x\in{\underline{l}}\cup-{\underline{l}}
.
The Weyl group
{\overline{W}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}
can be identified with
{{{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}}}
as in
[
GLS
, Rem. 1.8.8]
or
[
S23a
, Sect. 3.2]
.

We write

{{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm l}}}

for the normal subgroup of
\overline{W}
consisting of permutations
\sigma
with even
|-{\underline{l}}\cap\sigma({\underline{l}})|
. This coincides with
W={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}
.

If
I\subseteq{\underline{l}}
, then the ordinary symmetric group
{{{\mathcal{S}}_{I}}}
can be identified with the (parabolic) subgroup
{{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}}}
of
\overline{W}={\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
fixing every element of
{\underline{l}}\setminus I
and stabilising
I
. For example
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}
is the trivial group if
|I|=1
.
We define
{{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm I}}}
as the subgroup of elements in
W
which fix any element of
\underline{l}\setminus I
(hence also of
-\underline{l}\setminus-I
and stabilize
I\cup-I
).

Given a partition
\mathbb{M}
of a set
M\subseteq{\underline{l}}
we set
{{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathbb{M}}}}=\prod_{I\in\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}
the direct product of subgroups
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}
with
I\in\mathbb{M}
.

\@upn
3.4 — Elements of
{\mathbf{T}}
and the action of the Weyl group.

The elements
\left\langle\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t)\mid t\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times},i\in{\underline{l}}\right\rangle
generate
{\mathbf{T}}
. Chevalley relations show

\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t^{\prime}_{i})

if and only if
t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}=\pm 1
for every
i\in{\underline{l}}
and
\prod_{i=1}^{l}t^{\prime}_{i}/t_{i}=1
. So the value of each
t_{i}
is determined by
\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})
up to multiplication with
-1
.

The Weyl group
\overline{W}={\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}

acts on those elements in the following way:

\big(\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})\big)^{(j,-j)}=\mathbf{h}_{e_{j}}(t^{-2}_{j})\,\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i}){\text{ and }}\big(\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})\big)^{(k,k^{\prime})}=\mathbf{h}_{e_{k}}(t_{k^{\prime}})\mathbf{h}_{e_{k^{\prime}}}(t_{k})\big(\prod_{i\in{\underline{l}}\setminus\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t_{i})\big),

where
j,k,k^{\prime}\in{\underline{l}}
with
k\neq k^{\prime}
.

From the action of
\overline{W}
on
{\mathbf{T}}
described above we easily get the following.

Lemma
\@upn
3.5

Let
{{\mathcal{F}}}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
be a set of representatives in
{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
under
x\mapsto-x
and inversion
x\mapsto x^{-1}
, and such that
\{1,\varpi\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
. Then every
s\in{\mathbf{T}}
has some
\overline{W}
-conjugate
s^{\prime}
such that

s^{\prime}\in\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t^{\prime}_{i})\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle,

where
t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
for every
i\in{\underline{l}}
.

The corresponding centralizers are then described as follows. Note that whenever
X_{1},X_{2}\leq X
are finite groups with
[X_{1},X_{2}]=1
we write
X_{1}.X_{2}
for the central product of the groups
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.

Lemma
\@upn
3.6

Let
s^{\prime}
be as in
Lemma

3.5
. We write
I_{\zeta}(s^{\prime}):=\{i\mid t^{\prime}_{i}\in\{\pm\zeta\}\}
for
\zeta\in\mathcal{F}
. We abbreviate
I_{1}:=I_{1}(s^{\prime})
,
I_{\varpi}:=I_{\varpi}(s^{\prime})
and set
R^{\prime}:=\bigsqcup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{1,\varpi\}}\{e_{i}-e_{i^{\prime}}\mid i,i^{\prime}\in I_{\zeta}(s^{\prime})\text{ with }i\neq i^{\prime}\}.

Then
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s^{\prime})
is a central product of reductive groups normalized by
{\mathbf{T}}
:

{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s^{\prime})={\mathbf{C}}_{1}.{\mathbf{C}}_{4}.{\mathbf{C}}_{R^{\prime}},

where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle{\mathbf{T}}_{I_{1}},{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R_{I_{1}}\right\rangle,\,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{4}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\left\langle{\mathbf{T}}_{I_{\varpi}},{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R_{I_{\varpi}}\right\rangle\ \ \text{if $p$ is odd}\\
1\ \ \ \ \text{if $p=2$, }\end{cases}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\text{ and }}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{R^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle{\mathbf{T}}_{{\underline{l}}\setminus(I_{\varpi}\cup I_{1})},{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R^{\prime}\right\rangle.

Then, for
{\mathbf{N}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
the group
W_{s^{\prime}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{N}}}(s^{\prime})/{\mathbf{T}}
satisfies

W_{s^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm I_{1}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm I_{\varpi}}\times\prod_{\zeta\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{1,\varpi\}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I_{\zeta}(s^{\prime})}\ \ \text{if $p$ is odd}\\
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm I_{1}}\times\prod_{\zeta\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\{1\}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I_{\zeta}(s^{\prime})}\ \ \ \ \text{if $p=2$. }\end{cases}

Proof.

We can compute the centralizer of
s^{\prime}
from the root system as in
[
C
, 3.5.3]
, noting that the first statement reduces to the one about Weyl groups which can in turn be checked thanks to
3.4
above.

Another way is to note that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s^{\prime})
is connected and therefore
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s^{\prime})=\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}^{-1}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})}^{\circ}(s^{\prime}))
so our claim reduces to a computation in
{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
. The groups
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
,
{\mathbf{C}}_{4}
(the latter when
\varpi\neq 1
, i.e.
p\neq 2
) and
{\mathbf{C}}_{R^{\prime}}
then correspond to the orthogonal groups on the eigenspaces associated with
1
and
\varpi
, while
{\mathbf{C}}_{R^{\prime}}
corresponds to the other eigenspaces of
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}(s^{\prime})
in
{\mathbb{F}}^{2l}
, see also
[
FoSr89
, 1.13]
.
∎

The following statement is used to apply later
Proposition

4.9
in the calculations of relative Weyl groups. We denote by
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}})
the group of automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}
generated by
F_{p}
and
\gamma
.

We use the term
d
-
regular
in the sense of
[
Br
, Ch. 5]
and
[
Sp74
]
for certain elements of
W
, see also

Definition

4.2
below.

Corollary
\@upn
3.7

Let
{\varphi_{0}}
be the automorphism of the character lattice
X({\mathbf{T}})
, such that
F
acts there as
q\varphi_{0}
. Let
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
be a maximal torus of
{{\mathbf{H}}}
, let
s\in{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
and set
{\mathbf{C}}:={\mathrm{C}}^{\circ}_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)\geq{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
,
P:={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})/{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
. Let
d\geq 1
be an integer and
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{H}}},F)
.

(a)

There is an inner automorphism
\iota
of
{{\mathbf{H}}}
sending
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
to
\pi({\mathbf{T}})
, and which induces an isomorphism

P\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathbb{I}}\leq W,

for some
J,J^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{l}
with
J\cap J^{\prime}=\emptyset
and some partition
\mathbb{I}
of
\underline{l}\setminus(J\cup J^{\prime})
.

(b)

Assume additionally
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})
is the torus
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
and
s\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
. Then
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})\leq{\mathbf{C}}
and there exists some
d
-regular element
w\varphi_{0}\in W\varphi_{0}
normalising
\iota(P)
such that
{\mathrm{C}}_{\iota(P)}(w\varphi_{0})
is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}/{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
.

(c)

Keep the assumptions about
s
and
{\mathbf{S}}
from (b). Set
K:={\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{W}}(\iota(P))
and
\widehat{C}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}\rtimes\underline{E}({{\mathbf{H}}}^{F})}(s)
. There is a morphism

{\mathrm{N}}_{\widehat{C}}({\mathbf{S}})/{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{C}}_{K}(w\varphi_{0}),

restricts into an isomorphism
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}/{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}\cong{\mathrm{C}}_{\iota(P)}(w\varphi_{0})
.

Proof.

Up to inner automorphism, we can assume that
s=\pi(s^{\prime})
where
s^{\prime}
is as in
Lemma

3.6
which then gives us the structure of the Weyl group of
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}(s^{\prime})
, hence (a).

The maximal torus of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
corresponding to
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})
is the centralizer of a Sylow
d
-torus. This torus
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
is obtained from the maximally split torus
\pi({\mathbf{T}})
by twisting with
w\varphi_{0}
, where
\iota(F)\pi({\mathbf{T}})=wF
in the semidirect product
{{\mathbf{H}}}E({{\mathbf{H}}})
and
w\varphi_{0}
is some regular element of
W\varphi_{0}
of order
d
, see
[
C
, §3.3]
and
[
GM
, 3.5.7]
. The quotient
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}/{\mathrm{C}}_{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}({\mathbf{S}})
is therefore isomorphic to
{\mathrm{C}}_{P}(w\varphi_{0})
according to
[
C
, 3.3.6]
, which gives (b). The group
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}({\mathbf{S}})/{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}({\mathbf{S}})
is isomorphic to
\overline{W}
. With the standard discussion, see the proof of
[
C
, 3.3.6]
, we easily get the statement in (c).
∎

3.B
An automorphism of the centraliser of a semisimple element

The adjoint group
{{\mathbf{H}}}
being the dual of the simply-connected group
{{\mathbf{G}}}
, Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition of characters associates with each element of
\mathrm{Irr}(\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q))
the
{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}
-orbit of a pair
(s,\phi)
, where
s\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}
is semisimple and
\phi
is a unipotent character of
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}(s)
. Under some assumptions on
s
we find an automorphism of
{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}
fixing
s
and related to the graph automorphism
\gamma
of
\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
. This result complements
[
CS24
, Cor. 3.2]
.

When dealing with the semidirect product
{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
, recall that we consider any element of
{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\setminus{{\mathbf{G}}}
(or
{{\mathbf{H}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{H}}})\setminus{{\mathbf{H}}}
) as an element of
\mathrm{Aut}({{\mathbf{G}}})
whence the notation
\sigma(g)
for the product
\sigma g\sigma^{-1}
whenever
\sigma\in{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\setminus{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
g\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
. Recall the element
h_{0}\in\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
from
2.24
(a). We use the notation
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\text{ss}}}}
and
{{{{\mathbf{H}}}_{\text{ss}}}}
to denote the sets of semisimple elements of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{{\mathbf{H}}}
.

Proposition
\@upn
3.8

Let
s_{0}\in{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\text{ss}}
be such that
F(s_{0})\in s_{0}\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
and
s_{0}
is
{{\mathbf{G}}}
-conjugate to
s_{0}h_{0}
.
Set
s=\pi(s_{0})\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}_{\text{ss}}
.

Then there exist some
a\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}
with
s_{0}^{a}=s_{0}h_{0}
,
some
\gamma^{\prime}\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
,
and
F
-stable connected reductive subgroups
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
of
{{\mathbf{H}}}
such that

(i)

{\mathrm{C}}^{\circ}_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)={\mathbf{C}}_{1}.{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
(central product);

(ii)

[\gamma^{\prime},s_{0}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
and therefore
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=s
;

(iii)

[a\gamma^{\prime},{\mathbf{C}}_{1}]=1
, and

(iv)

[\gamma^{\prime},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}]=1
.

Proof.

Note that the statement is essentially about
{{\mathbf{H}}}
which is a quotient of
{{\mathbf{G}}}/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle={\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
. The automorphism
\gamma
is induced by conjugation by the element
n_{1}^{\circ}\in\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
thanks to
Lemma

2.23
, so the considerations below could be seen as happening in
\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle={\operatorname{SO}}_{2l+1}({\mathbb{F}})
thus making more concrete the commutation arguments used.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}_{0}
be an
F
-stable maximal torus of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
with
s_{0}\in{\mathbf{T}}_{0}
. Since all maximal tori of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
are
{{\mathbf{G}}}
-conjugate, there exists some inner automorphism

\iota_{0}:{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})

with
\iota_{0}({\mathbf{T}}_{0})={\mathbf{T}}
.

Now
3.4
implies that
\iota_{0}(s_{0})
is
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}({\mathbf{T}})
-conjugate to some element of the
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
-coset
(\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t^{\prime}_{i}))\ \left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle

with all
t^{\prime}_{i}
’s in
\mathcal{F}
. So there is some

\iota_{1}:{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
, which is the conjugation by some element of
{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
such that
\iota_{1}({\mathbf{T}}_{0})={\mathbf{T}}
and

s^{\prime}:=\iota_{1}(s_{0})\in\left(\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(t^{\prime}_{i})\right)\quad\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle

with all
t^{\prime}_{i}
’s in
\mathcal{F}
.
Then
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}(s^{\prime})
can be written as

{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}(s^{\prime})={\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4},

where
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbf{C}}_{4}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
are defined as in
Lemma

3.6
.

Since
s_{0}
and
s_{0}h_{0}
are
{{\mathbf{G}}}
-conjugate we can assume without loss of generality that
t^{\prime}_{1}=\pm 1
and
t^{\prime}_{l}=\pm\varpi
thanks to
Remark

3.2
(b).

We have
\iota_{1}(F)\in{{\mathbf{G}}}F
in
{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
since
\iota_{1}
is an inner automorphism there and
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})={{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})/{{\mathbf{G}}}
is abelian. Moreover
\iota_{1}(F)\in{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})F
since
F({\mathbf{T}}_{0})={\mathbf{T}}_{0}
implies
\iota_{1}(F)({\mathbf{T}})={\mathbf{T}}
. Let
n\in{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
be such that
\iota_{1}(F)=nF
.

The assumption
[s_{0},F]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
implies
[s^{\prime},\iota_{1}(F)]=[\iota_{1}(s_{0}),\iota_{1}(F)]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
by
2.24
(b). Then
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}(s^{\prime})
is fixed under the Frobenius endomorphism
nF=\iota_{1}(F)
of
{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
and its eigenspaces are permuted by
nF
as the corresponding eigenvalues. From the definition of
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}
,
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
, we get that their images under
\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}
are
\iota_{1}(F)
-stable.

Note that
h_{0}\in{\mathbf{T}}_{I}
for any nonempty
I\subseteq{\underline{l}}
and therefore
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}^{-1}(\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}))
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}=\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}^{-1}(\pi_{\operatorname{SO}}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}))
while
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
also contains
h_{0}
unless it is trivial. So from what we have seen in
{\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
we get that
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}
,
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}
are all
\iota_{1}(F)
-stable.

What we said above about
\iota_{1}(F)=nF
implies that
n\in{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
can be written in the form
n_{1}(n_{1}^{\circ})^{i_{1}}n_{4}(n_{2}^{\circ})^{i_{2}}n^{\prime}
, where
n_{1}\in{\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
n_{4}\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}
,
{n_{1}^{\circ}:=\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(\varpi)}\in\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
,
{{n_{2}^{\circ}:=\mathbf{n}_{e_{l}}(\varpi)}}\in n_{1}^{\circ}{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{0,1\}
and
n^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4})
.

Since
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbf{C}}_{4}^{\prime}
are connected
\iota_{1}(F)
-stable, Lang’s Theorem (
[
MT
, 21.7]
) implies that there exists some
c\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}(s^{\prime})
such that
(\iota_{1}(F))^{c}=vF
where
v=(n_{1}^{\circ})^{i_{1}}(n_{2}^{\circ})^{i_{2}}n^{\prime}
. Let
\iota\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
be defined by
\iota(x)=\iota_{1}(x)^{c}
. Then
\iota(F)=vF
and
\iota({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})=\iota({{\mathbf{G}}}^{vF})
. Note that
\iota(s_{0})=(s^{\prime})^{c}=s^{\prime}
.

We have
a^{\prime}:=n_{1}^{\circ}n_{2}^{\circ}\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
by checking its class mod
{\mathbf{T}}
. Moreover using
3.4
we get
s^{\prime a^{\prime}}=s^{\prime}h_{0}
while
[a^{\prime},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}]=1
thanks to Chevalley’s commutator formula. Note also that
[F,a^{\prime}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
. Let us now take

a:=\pi(\iota^{-1}(a^{\prime}))\ ,\ \ {\mathbf{C}}_{1}:=\pi(\iota^{-1}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1})),\ {\text{ and }}\ \ {\mathbf{C}}_{2}:=\pi(\iota^{-1}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{2}))\ \ \text{for}\ \ {\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{2}:={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4}.

We clearly have
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{\circ}(s)=\pi({\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s_{0}))=\pi\circ\iota^{-1}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(s^{\prime}))=\pi\circ\iota^{-1}({\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{R^{\prime}}.{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{4})={\mathbf{C}}_{1}{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
.

Recall
F\in\{F_{q},\gamma F_{q}\}
. For
F^{\prime}:=\iota(F)=vF
we have
[F^{\prime},a^{\prime}]=[F,a^{\prime}][v,a^{\prime}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
since

[n^{\prime},a^{\prime}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
, see
2.24
(d). Therefore
a\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}
.

Recall that
\gamma
and
n_{1}^{\circ}
induce the same automorphism on
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. Hence we get
[\gamma,{\mathbf{C}}_{2}^{\prime}]=[n_{1}^{\circ},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}^{\prime}]=1
thanks to the Chevalley commutator formula, and analogously
[a^{\prime}\gamma,{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}]=[n_{1}^{\circ}n_{2}^{\circ}\gamma,{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{1}]=1
. Applying
\pi\circ\iota^{-1}
, this gives our claims (iii)–(iv) that
[{\mathbf{C}}_{1},a\gamma^{\prime}]=[{\mathbf{C}}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}]=1
in
{{\mathbf{H}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{H}}})
, where

\gamma^{\prime}\in{{\mathbf{H}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{H}}})
is the image of
\iota^{-1}(\gamma)\in{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
under
\pi
.
Note that
[\gamma,\pi(s^{\prime})]=1
since
[n_{1}^{\circ},s^{\prime}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
by
3.4
, hence (ii).

It remains to show that
\gamma^{\prime}\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
. By its definition,
\gamma^{\prime}\in{{\mathbf{H}}}\gamma
. Additionally
vF
commutes with
\gamma
in
{{\mathbf{H}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{H}}})
, as
2.24
implies
[\gamma,\pi(v)]=\pi([n_{1}^{\circ},n^{\prime}])=1
. Now
\gamma\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}\gamma}(vF)={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}\gamma}(\iota(F))
implies
\gamma^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}\gamma}(F)={{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
. This completes our proof. ∎

3.C
Consequences on characters of
\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
be given as in
2.E
. We assume that a regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
is chosen such that
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
acts on
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
. This implies that
F
and therefore
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
acts on
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
.
The
\textbf{A}(\infty)
property of

Theorem

2.18
was originally introduced as saying that any element of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
has a
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
-conjugate
\chi
such that
({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\chi}={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}_{\chi}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
and
\chi
extends to
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
, see
[
S12
, Thm 2.12(v)]
and our Introduction. This condition on
\chi
defines a subset
\overline{\mathbb{T}}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
that we complete below into a partition

\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\overline{\mathbb{T}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}\sqcup\mathbb{D}

that will be crucial in our description of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
. This will be particularly useful when studying characters of certain local subgroups where
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is replaced by groups
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}
for
k\leq l
(see
3.D
) occuring as
[{{\mathbf{L}}},{{\mathbf{L}}}]
for
{{\mathbf{L}}}
a minimal
d
-split Levi subgroup of our
{{\mathbf{G}}}
.

Most of the properties we single out revolve around the value of characters at
h_{0}
(see
2.24
) and the action of the diagonal outer automorphism associated with
h_{0}
through
Notation

2.14
.

Definition
\@upn
3.9

Let
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathbb{D}
be the following subsets of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
:

\displaystyle{{{\overline{\mathbb{T}}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\chi\,\left|\,{({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\chi}={{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}_{\chi}{{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}_{\chi}\text{ and }}{\chi\text{ extends to }{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}}\right.\right\},

\displaystyle{{{{\mathbb{E}}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\chi\,\left|\,{({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\chi}={{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}_{\chi}{{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}_{\chi}\text{ and }}{\chi\text{ has no extension to }{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}}\right.\right\},{\text{ and }}

\displaystyle{{{\mathbb{D}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\chi\,\left|\,({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\chi}\neq{{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}_{\chi}{{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}_{\chi}\right.\right\},

so that
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\overline{\mathbb{T}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}\sqcup\mathbb{D}
.

We also define
{{{{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}}}}:=\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cap\big(\bigcup_{x\in{\widetilde{G}}}{}^{x}{\mathbb{E}}\big)
and
{{{\mathbb{D}^{\prime}}}}:=\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cap\big(\bigcup_{x\in{\widetilde{G}}}{}^{x}\mathbb{D}\big)
.

Notation
\@upn
3.10

Let
{{{\widehat{t}}}}\in{\mathbf{T}}
with
F(\widehat{t})=h_{0}\widehat{t}
and set
\widehat{G}:={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle\widehat{t}\right\rangle={{\mathbf{G}}}\cap\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle)
, where
\mathcal{L}:{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
is given by
x\mapsto x^{-1}F(x)
.

In the following, we prove that any element of
{\mathbb{E}}\cup{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}\cup\mathbb{D}\cup\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
is contained in a
\gamma
-stable
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
-orbit, and is lying under a
\gamma
-invariant character of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle\widehat{t}\right\rangle
. This generalizes
[
S23a
, Prop. 5.3]
where
{\epsilon}=1
,
\chi
is cuspidal,
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
and
\operatorname{N}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}\leq{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle\widehat{t}\right\rangle
.

Theorem
\@upn
3.11

Let
l\geq 4
,
{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}
,
{{\mathbf{G}}}:=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
with
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\cong\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}^{\epsilon}(q)
.

(a)

Let
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}\cup{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
. Then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
while
\chi
is invariant under and extends to
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle\widehat{t},\gamma\right\rangle
.

(b)

Let
\chi\in\mathbb{D}\cup\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
. Then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
and
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi^{\widehat{t}}\neq\chi
or
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
according to
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
or
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
.

Here are some first properties coming mostly from the structure of the outer automorphism group of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
.

Lemma
\@upn
3.12

Recall
{\widetilde{Z}}(G)=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
and its action on
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
by diagonal outer automorphisms (see
Notation

2.14
).

(a)

If
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
, then
|{\widetilde{Z}}(G):{\widetilde{Z}}(G)_{\chi}|=4
and
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
.

(b)

If
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}
, then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
,
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
and
{\widetilde{Z}}(G)_{\chi}=\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle
for
\widehat{h}_{0}=h_{0}[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]\in{\widetilde{Z}}(G)
.

Proof.

First we prove part (a), so assume
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
. We abbreviate
{\widetilde{Z}}={\widetilde{Z}}(G)
and
E={\underline{E}}(G)
. By
Theorem

2.18
there is
\chi^{\prime}=\chi^{z}
with
z\in{\widetilde{Z}}
such that
({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi^{\prime}}={\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi^{\prime}}E_{\chi^{\prime}}
. Since
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
, one has
({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi}\neq{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi}E_{\chi}
while

\displaystyle\big(({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi}\big)^{z}=({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi^{\prime}}.

(3.1)

This forces
{\widetilde{Z}}E
to be nonabelian and therefore
|{\widetilde{Z}}|=4
. Then
F
acts trivially on
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
and
{\widetilde{Z}}=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
.
Also one can’t have
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi^{\prime}}=\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
since the latter is central in
{\widetilde{Z}}E
. But by (
3.1
) above neither
z
nor
zh_{0}
belongs to
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi^{\prime}}
, forcing
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi^{\prime}}=1
, hence our statement that
|{\widetilde{Z}}:{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi}|=4
.
Since
({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi^{\prime}}=E_{\chi^{\prime}}
can’t centralize
z
, let us take
e^{\prime}{}\in E_{\chi^{\prime}}
with
e^{\prime}{}\neq{}^{z}e^{\prime}{}\in({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi}
.
Concerning
\ker(\chi)
note that
[\operatorname{Z}(G),e^{\prime}]=\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
by
2.24
and therefore
[{\widetilde{Z}},e^{\prime}{}]=\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
in
{\widetilde{Z}}
since
[{\widetilde{Z}},e^{\prime}{}]\neq 1
. Then
{}^{e^{\prime}{}}\chi^{\prime}=\chi^{\prime}
implies
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle\leq\ker(\chi^{\prime})
and also
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle\leq\operatorname{Z}(G)\cap\ker(\chi^{\prime})=\operatorname{Z}(G)\cap\ker(\chi^{z})=\operatorname{Z}(G)\cap\ker(\chi)
since diagonal automorphisms act trivially on
\operatorname{Z}(G)
.

Next, we prove part (b). Let
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}
. Note that by
Proposition

2.2
(a) and the definition of
{\mathbb{E}}
this forces
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
to be noncyclic. Then
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
,
F=F_{p}^{f}
for some even
f
and
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})^{D}
for a subgroup
D\leq{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
satisfying
[
S23b
, Hyp. 5.5]
. Now our claims that
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
and
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi}=\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle
are known from
[
S23b
, Lem. 5.11 and Prop. 5.21]
.
∎

Let us recall briefly Lusztig’s parametrization of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
(for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
any connected reductive group defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
). Let
({{\mathbf{G}}}^{*},F^{*})
be dual to
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
in the sense of
[
DM20
, Def. 11.1.10]
or
[
GM
, 1.5.17]
. Then the partition of
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
into Lusztig’s
rational series
is

\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\bigsqcup_{[s]}\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[s])

where
[s]
ranges over the
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}{}^{F^{*}}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple elements
s\in{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}{}^{F^{*}}
. We set
\operatorname{Uch}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})=\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[1])
. Moreover for any
s\in{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}_{\text{ss}}{}^{F^{*}}
, one has a bijection

\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[s])\xrightarrow{\sim}{{\operatorname{Uch}}}({\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}):=\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}\mid\mathcal{E}({\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}^{\circ}(s)^{F^{*}},[1]))

thus allowing us to associate to each
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
a
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}^{F^{*}}
-class of pairs
(s,\phi)
with
s\in{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}_{\text{ss}}^{F^{*}}
and
\phi\in\operatorname{Uch}{({\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(s)^{F^{*}}})
. This Jordan decomposition of characters can be chosen to be
\mathrm{Out}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
-equivariant when
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is simple simply connected, see
[
S23b
, Thm B]
.

All elements of
\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[s])
have the same restriction to
\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
, see
[
B06
, Prop. 9.11(b)]
. We also recall in the next lemma some standard related facts. The proof is basic theory of dual groups (see more details in proofs of
[
NT13
, Lem. 4.4(ii)]
and
[
B06
, Lem. 9.14]
). We state everything in the case where
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is as defined in
2.E
and
({{\mathbf{G}}}^{*},F^{*})
can then be taken to be
({{\mathbf{H}}},F)
, thus resulting in more identifications.

Lemma
\@upn
3.13

The duality between
({{\mathbf{H}}},F)
and
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
induces an
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{Lin}(\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))\cong\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F].

If
s\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}_{\text{ss}}
,
s_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(s)
and
\chi\in\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[s])
, then the element of
\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\chi\right\rceil_{\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})})
corresponds to
[s_{0},F][\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
.

We can now prove
Theorem

3.11
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.11
.

We let
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}\cup{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}\cup\mathbb{D}\cup\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
. In view of the claim made and
[\widetilde{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),\gamma]\leq\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
(see
2.24
) we can assume
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}\cup\mathbb{D}\cup\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
. We have
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
thanks to
Lemma

3.12
.

Let
s\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}_{\text{ss}}
and
s_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(s)
with
\chi\in\mathcal{E}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},[s])
. Set
\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\chi\right\rceil_{\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}})=:\{\xi\}
. Then
\xi(h_{0})=1
, hence
\xi
is
\gamma
-fixed by
2.24
. Now
Lemma

3.13
implies that
[s_{0},F]
is
\gamma
-fixed, hence an element of
\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
by
2.24
again.

Let us show that
s_{0}^{g}=s_{0}h_{0}
for some
g\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
. Using the injective map
{{\omega_{s}}}\colon{{A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)/{\mathrm{C}}^{\circ}_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)\to\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
from
[
S23b
, (1.5)]
, this means by definition that we must prove that
h_{0}
is in the image
{B(s)}
of
\omega_{s}
. To see this note that by
Lemma

3.12
again,
\widetilde{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
is trivial or generated by the class of
h_{0}
.
Taking
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}\mid\chi)
and seeing
B(s)^{F}
as acting by linear characters on
\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
(see
[
S23b
, Not. 2.14]
), one has by Clifford theory that
B(s)_{\widetilde{\chi}}^{F}
is the orthogonal of
\widetilde{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
(see
[
S23b
, Lem. 2.13(c)]
). Therefore
h_{0}\in B(s)_{\widetilde{\chi}}^{F}
which gives our claim.

All assumptions of
Proposition

3.8
are now satisfied. Let
a\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
,
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
be such that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{\circ}(s)={\mathbf{C}}_{1}{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
(central product) as there.

Let
({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*},F^{*})
be dual to
({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and let
\widetilde{s}\in\widetilde{{{\mathbf{G}}}}^{*}{}^{F^{*}}
such that
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathcal{E}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F},[\widetilde{s}])
with
\widetilde{s}\mapsto s
by the map dual to the inclusion
{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
. Let
\phi\in\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F^{*}})
, also be seen as an element of
\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}^{\circ}_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F})
since
{{\mathbf{H}}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}
are isogeneous, associated to
\widetilde{\chi}
by the Jordan decomposition
\widetilde{J}
of
[
S23b
, Prop. 2.15]
.
From the definition of
\omega_{s}

[
S23b
, Equ. (1.5)]
,
\phi
is
c
-invariant under any
c\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}(s)
with
s_{0}^{c}=s_{0}h_{0}
since
A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F}_{\phi}=\omega_{s}^{-1}(B(s)^{F}_{\widetilde{\chi}})
and we have seen that
h_{0}\in B(s)_{\widetilde{\chi}}^{F}
. Then
\phi^{a}=\phi
.

Let us show that
\phi
is also
\gamma^{\prime}
-invariant. Restrictions induce a bijection

\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{\circ}(s)^{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Uch}({\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{F})\times\operatorname{Uch}({\mathbf{C}}_{2}^{F}),\text{\ \ by\ \ }\phi\mapsto(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})

see
[
S23b
, 1.10]
. Let
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
be the character corresponding to
\phi
. Then
\phi^{a}=\phi
implies
\phi_{i}^{a}=\phi_{i}
for
i=1,2
and therefore
{a\gamma^{\prime}}
fixes
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
thanks to
Proposition

3.8
(iii) and (iv). Then it also fixes
\phi
by the above bijection and we get our claim that

(s,\phi)^{\gamma^{\prime}}=(s,\phi).

By the
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
-equivariance of
\widetilde{J}
and its compatibility with
\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
-orbits on
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
(see
[
S23b
, Prop. 2.16]
) we finally get that the
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
-orbit containing
\chi
is
\gamma
-stable, as
\gamma^{\prime}\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
. By the definition of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, this also implies
\chi=\chi^{\gamma}
whenever
\chi\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, which is the case when
\chi\in\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
. If on the contrary
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
, then
\chi^{\prime}:=\chi^{g}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
for some
g\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
and we can’t have
\chi=\chi^{\gamma}
since this would imply
g\gamma g^{-1}\gamma^{-1}\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}_{\chi}=G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
and therefore that
[g,{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})]\leq{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
thanks to the structure of
{\widetilde{Z}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
. But then
\chi
would have the same stabilizer as
\chi^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
. So
\chi^{\gamma}\neq\chi
and therefore
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi^{[g,\gamma]}=\chi^{\widehat{t}}
by
2.24
. This gives the two cases of our statement (b).

Assume now
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}
and therefore
\operatorname{N}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(G,\chi)=\widehat{G}
by
Lemma

3.12
(b). Recall that
[\gamma^{\prime},s_{0}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
according to
Proposition

3.8
. Then we can apply
[
S23b
, Cor. 6.6]
and obtain that
\chi
has some extension
\widehat{\chi}
to
\widehat{G}
that is
\gamma
-invariant. This gives our claim (a).
∎

Remark 3.14
.

The above proof gives the following slightly stronger statement for

\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\mid 1_{\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle})
:
If
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}_{\chi}\neq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}_{\chi}
is
\gamma
-stable, then every
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\left\langle{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\widehat{t}\right\rangle\mid\chi)
is
\gamma
-invariant.

3.D
Characters of
\mathrm{D}_{k,\mathrm{sc}}^{\epsilon}(q)
with
1\leq k\leq l

In the following we establish a slight generalization of
Theorem

3.11
including groups of rank
\leq 3
that appear naturally when studying
d
-split Levi subgroups of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
, see
Theorem

3.16
. The groups added to the picture are essentially semisimple simply connected of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\times\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
\mathrm{A}_{3}
but we need some results about them that are not formally contained in the
A
(\infty)
condition known already for groups of type
\mathrm{A}
, see
[
CS17a
, Thm 4.1]
. For those groups the proofs are an adaptation of what has been done in the preceding section.

For

recall

Theorem
\@upn
3.16

Let
\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}\in{{\mathbf{T}}}_{\underline{k}}
be such that

.

(a)

0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}))_{\chi}=({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}
and
\chi
extends to
G_{\underline{k}}{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}
.

(b)

Let
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{k}}\cup{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}_{\underline{k}}
. Then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
and
\chi
is invariant under and extends to
G_{\underline{k}}\left\langle\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}},\gamma\right\rangle
.

(c)

Let
\chi\in\mathbb{D}_{\underline{k}}\cup\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{\underline{k}}
. Then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
and
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi^{\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}}\neq\chi
or
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
according to
\chi\in\mathbb{D}_{\underline{k}}
or
\chi\in\mathbb{D}_{\underline{k}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1) Note that for
k\geq 4
the groups
G_{\underline{k}}
are just of the type studied before with
l=k
since

{\mathbf{T}}_{{\underline{k}}}\leq\left\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R_{{\underline{k}}}\right\rangle
by the Steinberg relations as soon as
k\geq 2
and therefore
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}=\left\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R_{{\underline{k}}}\right\rangle
is the derived subgroup of the Levi subgroup
{\mathbf{T}}{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}
hence of type
\mathrm{D}_{k,\mathrm{sc}}
thanks to
[
MT
, 12.14]
. The action of
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}
on characters of
G_{\underline{k}}
induces all and only diagonal outer automorphisms, so the stabilizer statement of (a) is covered by
Theorem

2.18
. The extension statement is also a consequence of the extension statement of
Theorem

2.18
since the groups
{\underline{E}}(G_{\underline{k}})
and
{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
are such that
{\underline{E}}(G_{\underline{k}})\to\!\!\!\!\to{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
with a kernel
K
acting trivially on
G_{\underline{k}}
and therefore if
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{\underline{k}})
extends to a character
\chi^{\prime}
of
G_{\underline{k}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}(G_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}=(G_{\underline{k}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi})/K
then one can inflate
\chi^{\prime}
into a character of
G_{\underline{k}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}
also extending
\chi
. As for statements (b) and (c), they are covered by
Theorem

3.11
.

There remains to prove the theorem in the cases where
k\in\{1,2,3\}
. Set
{\epsilon}=1
or
-1
according to
F=F_{q}
or
\gamma F_{q}
.

(2) Let us assume
k=1
. The group
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{1}}
is a torus of dimension
1
. Then every
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{\underline{1}})
is linear, hence extends to its stabilizer in the semidirect product
G_{\underline{1}}{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{1}})
by
Proposition

2.2
(d). This gives (a) since
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{1}}
acts trivially on
G_{\underline{1}}
. Note also that
{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{1}}=\mathbb{D}_{\underline{1}}=\emptyset
.

(3) Next, we study the case where
k\in\{2,3\}
.
Formally one could argue that for
k=3
the proof of
Theorem

2.18
could be transferred and still applies but we give here an independent proof that does not require to check that the relevant parts of
[
S23b
]
apply for
k=3
.
Recall that by (1) above

{\mathbf{T}}_{{\underline{k}}}\leq\left\langle{\mathbf{X}}_{\alpha}\mid{\alpha}\in R_{{\underline{k}}}\right\rangle
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}
is then a simply connected group of type
\mathrm{A}_{1}\times\mathrm{A}_{1}
or
\mathrm{A}_{3}
.
We get that
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{2}}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{3}}=\operatorname{SL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
with
F_{p}
acting as
x\mapsto x^{p}
on matrix entries, while
\gamma
acts by swapping the two components in the first case and by the graph automorphism of order 2 preserving the usual torus/Borel subgroups of 2.C above in the second case, see also the proof of
[
S23a
, Lem. 6.30]
.
It follows that

\displaystyle G_{\underline{2}}\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)&\text{if }{\epsilon}=1,\\
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q^{2})&\text{if }{\epsilon}=-1,\end{cases}\ \ {\text{ and }}\ \ G_{\underline{3}}\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{SL}_{4}(q)&\text{if }{\epsilon}=1,\\
\operatorname{SU}_{4}(q)&\text{if }{\epsilon}=-1.\end{cases}

(3.2)

Note that
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}
acts by diagonal automorphisms of
G_{\underline{k}}
. Applying
[
CS17a
, Thm 4.1]
to types
\mathrm{A}_{1}
(
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
) and
\mathrm{A}_{3}
gives our claim (a) in the cases
k=3
and
(k,{\epsilon})=(2,-1)
, using what has been said in (1) about the
{\underline{E}}(G_{\underline{k}})
versus
{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
question. In the case of
(k,{\epsilon})=(2,1)
,
[
CS17a
, Thm 4.1]
indeed shows that
there exists an
\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
-stable
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(q)
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q))
.
This implies that
\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{\underline{2}}
contains some
\left\langle F_{p},\gamma\right\rangle
-stable
\widetilde{G}_{\underline{2}}
-transversal. Moreover the group
G{\underline{E}}(G)
being a subgroup of the wreath product
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle\wr\mathrm{C}_{2}
, the extendibility claim holds by
Proposition

2.2
(c). We then get part (a) in all cases.

(4) We now turn to statements (b), (c) in the case when
k=2,3
and make some preliminary remarks.

Notice first that whenever
\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}})^{F}
is of order
\leq 2
then (a) and the commutativity of
({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}/\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}).G_{\underline{k}})\rtimes{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
imply that
\overline{\mathbb{T}}=\mathrm{Irr}(G_{\underline{k}})
and therefore
{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{k}}=\mathbb{D}_{\underline{k}}=\emptyset
, so (b) and (c) are trivial.

So we assume that
\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})
is of order 4, and in particular
q
is odd. Then
\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}})
and therefore
h_{0}\in{[\gamma,\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})]}
since
h_{0}=[\gamma,\mathbf{h}_{\underline{k}}(\varpi)]
, see
2.24
. This implies that the second part of (b) or (c) implies also that
h_{0}\in\ker\chi
. This is because, if
\chi
is invariant under the composition of a diagonal automorphism with
\gamma
, then its restriction to
\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}})
has the same property and indeed the restriction of
\chi
to
\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})
is
\gamma
-invariant, hence is trivial on
{[\gamma,\operatorname{Z}(G_{\underline{k}})]}
.

Concerning (b), as in the proof of
Theorem

3.11
(b), notice that one can consider
{\mathbb{E}}_{\underline{k}}
alone.

From now on we omit the index
{\underline{k}}
.

(5) Assume
k=2
. If
{\epsilon}=-1
, we have
|\operatorname{Z}(G)|=2
so (b) and (c) are trivial as said in (4). So we assume
{\epsilon}=1
. Then the group
G
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)
, see above.
What has been said about the wreath product action of
\gamma
implies
{\mathbb{E}}=\emptyset
hence (b).

To prove (c) let’s take
\chi\in\mathbb{D}{}
and let
\chi_{0}
be a
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
-conjugate of
\chi
such that
\chi_{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}{}
. That the stabilizers of
\chi
and
\chi_{0}
are distinct implies that
{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi_{0}}\nleq{\mathrm{C}}_{{\underline{E}}(G)}(\operatorname{Z}(G))=\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
and
\chi_{0}
is not
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
-invariant.
The character
\chi_{0}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q))
being now written as
\chi_{1}.\chi_{2}
, we see that at least one of
\{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\}
is not
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(q)
-invariant.
But
{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi_{0}}\nleq\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
implies that
\chi_{0}
is invariant under some element of
\gamma\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
, leading to
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
being both not
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(q)
-invariant.

Now every character of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)
that is not
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(q)
-invariant is
\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
-invariant, see the character table of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)
in
[
B
, Table 5.4]
. So we get that
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
are both
\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
-invariant. We can conclude that
\chi_{0}
is
\left\langle F_{p},\gamma\right\rangle
-invariant, which implies the second case of (c) for
\chi_{0}
. But
\chi_{0}
being the general element of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
, this settles the second case of (c) in general. Noting that
[F_{p},\operatorname{Z}(G)]=1
we also get that since
\chi_{0}=\chi^{z}
for some
z\in\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
,
\chi
is
F_{p}
-invariant. This implies that it can’t be
\gamma
-invariant, by the definition of
\mathbb{D}
. Then
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi^{\widehat{t}}
since
[z,\gamma]\in\left\langle\widehat{t}\right\rangle G=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle)
. This finishes the proof of (c) when
k=2
.

From now on, we assume
k=3
and we consider the regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\operatorname{SL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}=\operatorname{GL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
with extended Frobenius
F
defined by the same formula on matrix entries. The action of
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
on
G
is also induced by the one of
{\widetilde{G}}={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
and the action of
\widehat{t}
is also the one of any
\widetilde{t}\in{\widetilde{G}}
corresponding with
h_{0}=-\operatorname{Id}_{4}
in the isomorphism
{\widetilde{G}}/G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})\cong\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})/{[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]}=\operatorname{Z}(G)=\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
, the last equalities a consequence of
|\operatorname{Z}(G)|=4
.

(6) We consider first the case where
k=3{\text{ and }}{\epsilon}=-1.
Note that
{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})
is cyclic thus implying
{\mathbb{E}}=\emptyset
and (b) trivially.
Concerning (c) note that
|\operatorname{Z}(G)|=4
implies
4\mid(q+1)
and
q=p^{f}
with an odd
f\geq 1
. Then
{\underline{E}}(G)_{2}=\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
and
{\underline{E}}(G)_{2^{\prime}}=\left\langle F_{p}\gamma\right\rangle={\mathrm{C}}_{{\underline{E}}(G)}(\operatorname{Z}(G))
. Let
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
and
\chi_{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
a
{\widetilde{G}}
-conjugate of
\chi
. Then
{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi_{0}}\lneq{\mathrm{C}}_{{\underline{E}}(G)}(\operatorname{Z}(G))
since otherwise any
{\widetilde{G}}
-conjugate would also be in
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
. This forces
\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle={\underline{E}}(G)_{2}\leq{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
. We get
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
but also
\chi_{0}^{\gamma}\neq\chi_{0}
since the stabilizers of
\chi
and
\chi_{0}
in
({\widetilde{G}}/G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}}))\rtimes{\underline{E}}(G)\cong\operatorname{Z}(G){\underline{E}}(G)
can’t have the same Sylow 2-subgroup, otherwise they would be equal, the
2^{\prime}
-part being central. This implies
\chi_{0}^{\gamma}=\chi_{0}^{t}
for some
t\in{[\gamma,{\widetilde{G}}]}\setminus G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})
hence our claim (c) since
[\operatorname{Z}(G),\gamma]=\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
.

(7) From now on we assume
k=3
and
{\epsilon}=1
, so that
G=\operatorname{SL}_{4}(q)
.

For part (c) we consider a character
\chi\in\mathbb{D}
. The arguments used to prove
Lemma

3.12
(a) apply also here and we get that the
{\widetilde{G}}
-orbit of
\chi
has 4 elements. Let us show that this
{\widetilde{G}}
-orbit is
\gamma
-stable. Let
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q)\mid\chi)
. The fact that
{\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}=G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})
implies that
\widetilde{\chi}
is in a Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q),[\widetilde{s}])
such that the centralizer of the image of
\widetilde{s}

in
\operatorname{PGL}_{4}(q)
has a component group of order 4 (see for instance
[
S23b
, Prop. 2.15]
). This implies that the eigenvalues of
\widetilde{s}
are an orbit under the multiplication by the 4th root of unity
\varpi
. But then the transpose-inverse automorphism
{\gamma_{0}}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q)
sends
\widetilde{s}
to some conjugate times a scalar. This means that
\widetilde{\chi}^{\gamma_{0}}
is in the same Lusztig series as some
\lambda\widetilde{\chi}
with
\lambda\in\mathrm{Lin}(\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q))
by the action of automorphisms on Lusztig series in the connected center case (
[
S23b
, Prop. 2.15]
again). But those Lusztig series have a single element since the centraliser of
\widetilde{s}
is a torus. So
\widetilde{\chi}
and
\widetilde{\chi}^{\gamma}
have the same restriction to
G=\operatorname{SL}_{4}(q)
which gives our claim. We then proceed like in the end of the proof of
Theorem

3.16
(b). The above implies that if
\chi_{0}=\chi^{z}
is an element of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
for some
z\in\widetilde{Z}(G)={\widetilde{G}}/\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})G
then
\chi_{0}^{\gamma}=\chi^{z^{\prime}}_{0}
for some
z^{\prime}\in{\widetilde{Z}}(G)
. This implies
\chi_{0}^{\gamma}=\chi^{z^{\prime}}_{0}=\chi_{0}
by the definition of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, hence the second case of (c). Concerning the first case, note that
\chi^{\gamma}=\chi
would imply that
z\gamma z^{-1}\gamma^{-1}\in{\widetilde{Z}}(G)_{\chi}=1
and in turn that
z
centralizes
\gamma
hence the whole
{\widetilde{Z}}(G).{\underline{E}}(G)
. But then
{}^{z}\chi_{0}=\chi
would have the same stabilizer as
\chi_{0}
. So
\chi^{\gamma}\neq\chi
and this implies

\chi^{\gamma}=\chi^{\widehat{t}}
since
[{\widetilde{Z}}(G),\gamma]
is the group of order 2 generated by the image of
\widehat{t}
. We have proven part (c) in this case.

(8) We keep
k=3
,
{\epsilon}=1
and address (b). Let
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}
. We then have
({\widetilde{G}}{\underline{E}}(G))_{\chi}={\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
with
\chi
not extending to
G{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
. This forces
{\underline{E}}(G)
to be non-cyclic, hence
q
to be a square and
F
to act trivially on
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
. It also implies that
{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
is not cyclic and therefore
\gamma\in{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
. As pointed out in (4) above this implies
h_{0}\in\ker\chi
.

First we show that
{\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}\notin\{G\operatorname{Z}(\widetilde{G}),{\widetilde{G}}\}
.
Let
\chi^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
be a
{\widetilde{G}}
-conjugate of
\chi
(which exists by (a)). The equality
{\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}={\widetilde{G}}
is not possible as then
\chi=\chi^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}
.

Assume next that
\widetilde{G}_{\chi}=G\operatorname{Z}(\widetilde{G})
or equivalently
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\chi}=G
and hence
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\chi^{\prime}}=G
.
By definition of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
we have
(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}))_{\chi^{\prime}}=G{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})_{\chi^{\prime}}
and
\chi^{\prime}
has an extension to
G{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi^{\prime}}
.
Via
{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})/\left\langle\left.F\right\rceil_{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{201988}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{144277}{89202}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\right\rangle={\underline{E}}(G)
we see that
\chi^{\prime}
extends to some
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})_{\chi^{\prime}})
such that
\left\langle h_{0},\left.F\right\rceil_{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{201988}{124882}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.63779pt$\hss\resizebox{144277}{89202}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}\right\rangle\leq\ker(\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime})
.
Let us write
\chi=\chi^{\prime t}
for some
t\in\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
. We have

(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}))_{\chi}=(G{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})_{\chi^{\prime}})^{t}=G({\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern with also

(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}_{\chi}{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})_{\chi}
. Then
[\gamma,t]\in G
since the above implies
G\gamma^{t}\subseteq G{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})\cap{{\mathbf{G}}}\gamma=G\gamma
. Then
[[\gamma,t],F]=[[F,\gamma],t]=1
and by the Three-Subgroup lemma
[
Asch
, (8.7)]

[F,t]\in{\mathrm{C}}_{\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(\gamma)=\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
. Since
\chi\neq\chi^{\prime}
we actually have
[F,t]=h_{0}
and we can assume
t=\widehat{t}
.
If now
\chi=(\chi^{\prime})^{\widehat{t}}
, then
\widehat{\chi}:=(\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime})^{\widehat{t}}
is an extension of
\chi
to
G{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})_{\chi}
with
\left\langle h_{0},F\right\rangle^{\widehat{t}}=\left\langle h_{0},h_{0}F\right\rangle\leq\ker(\widetilde{\chi})
. In particular
F\in\ker(\widetilde{\chi})
. Via
{\underline{E}}(\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits})/\left\langle F\right\rangle={\underline{E}}(G)
the character
\widehat{\chi}
defines also an extension of
\chi
to
G{\underline{E}}(G)_{\chi}
, contradicting
\chi\in{\mathbb{E}}
. This contradiction establishes our claim that
{\widetilde{G}}_{\chi}\notin\{G\operatorname{Z}(\widetilde{G}),{\widetilde{G}}\}
, or equivalently that
\widetilde{G}_{\chi}
has index
2
in
\widetilde{G}
while being also
\gamma
-stable.

Let
s\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}
be such that
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,[s])
. Denote
\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}=\operatorname{GL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
. As in the above proof of (c), let
\widetilde{s}\in\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}
and
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q)\mid\chi)
