, such that
\widetilde{\chi}\in\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{GL}_{4}(q),[\widetilde{s}])
.
Then
|\widetilde{G}_{\chi}:G\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{G}})|=2
implies

\displaystyle|A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F}_{\phi}|=2,

(3.3)

where
\phi:=\Psi^{{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}}_{\widetilde{s}}(\widetilde{\chi})\in\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})
is associated to
\widetilde{\chi}
via Jordan decomposition of characters
{{\Psi^{({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}})}_{\widetilde{s}}}}:\mathcal{E}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}},[\widetilde{s}])\longrightarrow\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})
, see
[
S23b
, Prop. 2.15]
.

Assume
|A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)|=4
. Then as in the discussion made for (c) one gets that
{\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{{{\mathbf{H}}}}}(\widetilde{s})
is a torus and
\phi
is the trivial character. This leads to
A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F}_{\phi}=A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F}=A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)
since
F
acts trivially on
\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})
hence on
A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)
. This contradicts (
3.3
) above.

We therefore get that

\displaystyle A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)=A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)^{F}_{\phi}\text{ and has order 2}.

(3.4)

Like for
Theorem

3.11
(a), our aim is to apply
[
S23b
, Cor. 6.6]
whose original proof was for types
\mathrm{D}_{l}
(
l\geq 4
) but is easily seen to apply to our
G
of type
\mathrm{A}_{3}
. We have proven assumptions (i) and (ii) of
[
S23b
, Cor. 6.6]
. Letting
\pi\colon{{\mathbf{H}}}_{0}=[{\widetilde{{{\mathbf{H}}}}},{\widetilde{{{\mathbf{H}}}}}]\to{{\mathbf{H}}}
be the canonical surjection
\operatorname{SL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})\to\operatorname{PGL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
, there remains to show that there exists
\gamma^{*}\in{{{{\mathbf{H}}}}^{F}}\gamma
such that
\gamma^{*}(s,\phi)=(s,\phi)
and
[s_{0},\gamma^{*}]\in\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
for some (in fact any)
s_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(s)
. The first part is ensured by the equivariant Jordan decomposition for type
\mathrm{A}
(see
[
CS17a
, Thm 8.2]
or
[
S23b
, Thm B]
), so we concentrate on the second point, namely having
[\pi^{-1}(s),\gamma^{*}]\subseteq\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
.

Let
s_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(s)\subseteq{{\mathbf{H}}}_{0}=\operatorname{SL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
lifting
s
. Then
|A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)|=2
from (
3.4
) above implies that the set of eigenvalues of
s_{0}
is closed under multiplication with
-1
. Let
\kappa
,
\kappa^{\prime}
be two of them, such that
\{\kappa,-\kappa,\kappa^{\prime},-\kappa^{\prime}\}
is the set of all eigenvalues of
s_{0}
with multiplicities. Because of
\det(s_{0})=1
we get
\kappa^{\prime}=\pm\kappa^{-1}
and hence the set of eigenvalues is
\{\kappa,-\kappa,\kappa^{-1},-\kappa^{-1}\}
. Writing
\sim
for the conjugation in
{{\mathbf{H}}}_{0}=\operatorname{SL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
, which for semisimple elements coincides with conjugation in
\operatorname{GL}_{4}({\mathbb{F}})
, we see from the eigenvalues that

s_{0}\sim s_{0}^{-1}\sim\gamma^{*}(s_{0}).

Since
\gamma^{*}(s)=s
then
\gamma^{*}(s_{0})=\omega s_{0}
for some
\omega\in{\mathbb{F}^{\times}}
, and
s_{0}\sim\omega s_{0}
by the above. This forces in turn
\omega=\pm 1
since
A_{{\mathbf{H}}}(s)
has order 2. So
\gamma^{*}(s_{0})=\pm s_{0}
and we get our claim that
[\pi^{-1}(s),\gamma^{*}]\subseteq\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle=\{\pm\operatorname{Id}_{4}\}
.
∎

The following definition makes precise what is mmeant by groups of type
\mathrm{D}
in ranks
\leq 3
, e.g.
\mathrm{D}_{k,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
like
G_{\underline{k}}
above for
1\leq k\leq 3
. (Most textbooks consider types
\mathrm{D}_{l}
only for
l\geq 4
.) This will be used mainly in Chapters
5
and
6
.

Definition
\@upn
3.17

Assume
k\in\{1,2,3\}
and
{\epsilon}=\pm 1
. Then we denote by
\mathrm{D}_{k,{\mathrm{sc}}}({\mathbb{F}})
the subgroup
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{4,{\mathrm{sc}}}({\mathbb{F}})
defined in
Notation

3.15
. We also write
\mathrm{D}_{k,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
for
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}^{F}
where
F
is such that
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}=\mathrm{D}_{4,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
. Then we get the isomorphisms with groups of type
\mathrm{A}
and a torus recalled in (
3.2
)
of the above proof.

We gather some extra statements that will be used alongside
Theorem

3.16
.

Lemma
\@upn
3.18

Assume
k
and
\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}
are like in
Theorem

3.16
. If
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{\underline{k}})
is such that
({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})_{\chi}\leq G_{\underline{k}}\left\langle\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}\right\rangle
, then
({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}))_{\chi}\leq G_{\underline{k}}\left\langle\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}\right\rangle{\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Lemma

3.12
we abbreviate
{\widetilde{Z}}={\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}/G_{\underline{k}}
,
E={\underline{E}}({\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}})
,
\widehat{h}_{0}
the class of
\widehat{t}_{\underline{k}}
. Note that
\widehat{h}_{0}
is central in the semidirect product
{\widetilde{Z}}\rtimes E
thanks to
2.24
and that
E
also acts trivially on
{\widetilde{Z}}/\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle
since this is of order
\leq 2
. By
Theorem

3.16
(a) there exists
z\in{\widetilde{Z}}
such that
({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{{}^{z}\chi}=Z_{0}E_{0}
with
Z_{0}\leq{\widetilde{Z}}
and
E_{0}\leq E
. Since
{\widetilde{Z}}
is abelian we have
Z_{0}={\widetilde{Z}}\cap Z_{0}E_{0}=({\widetilde{Z}}\cap Z_{0}E_{0})^{z}={\widetilde{Z}}\cap(Z_{0}E_{0})^{z}={\widetilde{Z}}\cap({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi}={\widetilde{Z}}_{\chi}\leq\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle
by our hypothesis. Then
({\widetilde{Z}}E)_{\chi}=(Z_{0}E_{0})^{z}\leq(\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle E)^{z}\leq\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle E
since we have seen that
[{\widetilde{Z}},E]\leq\left\langle\widehat{h}_{0}\right\rangle
.
∎

Lemma
\@upn
3.19

Assume
k\in{\underline{l}}
, then maximal extendibility holds with respect to
G_{\underline{k}}\unlhd{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}
.

Proof.

If
k\geq 3
, then the statement is
[
S23a
, Thm 2.17]
thanks to our description of
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{\underline{k}}
given in the proof of
Theorem

3.16
. When
k=1
then
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}
is abelian, while when
k=2
and
F=F_{q}\gamma
the quotient
{\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{412222}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{326422}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{278933}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle G\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}}_{\underline{k}}/G_{\underline{k}}
is cyclic and
Proposition

2.2
(a) applies. When
k=2
and
F=F_{q}
then

G_{\underline{2}}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q)\unlhd{\mathrm{N}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q))\times{\mathrm{N}}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(q))
and we have again the cyclic quotient situation on each factor.
∎

4
The doubly regular case

In this chapter, we prove the inductive McKay condition (
iMK
) for the group
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
of
Section

2.E
and an odd prime
\ell
under an arithmetic assumption on
d_{\ell}(q)
, the order of
q
in
(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
. This condition of being a
doubly regular
number for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
Definition

4.4
below, ensures that the Sylow
\ell
-subgroups of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
have abelian centralizers in
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}=\mathrm{D}_{l,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
but also in the overgroup
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}=\mathrm{B}_{l,{\mathrm{sc}}}(q)
.
The criterion for (
iMK
) is the one of
Theorem

2.21
through the conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
of
2.19
and
2.20
defined for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and any integer
d\geq 1
, relating in particular to Sylow
d
-tori of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
as recalled in
Section

2.D
.

The main result of the present chapter is as follows.

Theorem
\@upn
4.1

Let
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
for
l\geq 4
,
{\epsilon}=\pm 1
,
q
a prime power.
Let
d\geq 3
and
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.

\text{Assume that $d\mid 2l$ and the ratio $\frac{2l}{d}$ is }\begin{cases}\text{ \text{even} \ when }&{\epsilon}=1,\textbf{}\\
\text{ \text{odd} \ when }&{\epsilon}=-1,\end{cases}

(in other words
d
is a doubly regular integer for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
Definition

4.4
).
Then:

(a)

Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
Section

2.D
hold for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.

(b)

If
\ell
is an odd prime with
\ell\nmid q
and
d_{\ell}(q)=d
, then (
iMK
) from
Definition

2.7
holds for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
\ell
with respect to
N={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
.

(c)

(
iMK
) holds for
\mathrm{D}_{4,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\epsilon}(q)
and any prime
\ell
.

Note that for
d\in\{1,2\}
and primes
\ell=2
or odd with
d_{\ell}(q)\in\{1,2\}
the above points (a) and (b) are known from
[
MS16
, Thm 1]
.

The proof will use a criterion for
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
[
CS17b
]
devised for the more general case where
d
is a regular number for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, see
Proposition

4.3
below. This will split the proof of
Theorem

4.1
(a) into two parts.
Section

4.C
deals with the Tits subgroup
\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\right\rangle\leq{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
. Then
Section

4.D
shows properties of the relative Weyl groups
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}},\xi)^{F}/{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
for
{\mathbf{S}}
a Sylow
d
-torus of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
\xi\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F})
. The proof of
Theorem

4.1

concludes in
Section

4.E
. After that we derive some consequences of the constructions made, in particular correspondences of characters not necessarily of
\ell^{\prime}
-degrees, that will be useful later in Section 6.A.

4.A
The conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
for regular numbers

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
be a simple simply connected algebraic group defined over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
as in
Section

2.C
. We recall the choice of a maximal torus and Borel subgroup
{\mathbf{T}}\leq{\mathbf{B}}
both
F
-stable and the associated notation. Let

\rho\colon{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})\longrightarrow W({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}

be the canonical surjection onto the Weyl group.
We see the latter as acting on the euclidean vector space
{\mathbb{V}}=\mathbb{R}\otimes X({\mathbf{T}})
, abbreviating the image of
W({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
as
W\leq\operatorname{GL}({\mathbb{V}})
. We recall
\varphi_{0}\in{\mathrm{N}}_{\operatorname{GL}({\mathbb{V}})}(W)
, the element of
\operatorname{GL}({\mathbb{V}})
such that
F
induces
q\varphi_{0}
on
X({\mathbf{T}})
, see
[
MT
, Def. 22.10]
. Writing
F=F_{p}^{f}\circ\sigma
for
\sigma
a graph automorphism as in
Section

2.C
,
\varphi_{0}
corresponds to the symmetry of the basis of the root system that defines
\sigma
.

For regular elements of Weyl groups and regular numbers we refer to
[
Br
, Ch. 5]
and
[
Sp74
]
. For the relation with
d
-tori, see
[
MT
, Thm 25.10]
and
[
GM
, Sect. 3.5]
.

Definition
\@upn
4.2 — Regular elements of
W\varphi_{0}
.

An element of
W\varphi_{0}
is called regular if it has an eigenvector in
\mathbb{C}\otimes{\mathbb{V}}
which is not contained in the reflecting hyperplane to any
\alpha\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
. It is called
\zeta
-regular if this eigenvector is for the eigenvalue
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
The integers
d\geq 1
such that there is a
\zeta
-regular element in
W\varphi_{0}
with
\zeta
of order
d
are called the
regular numbers
for
(W,\varphi_{0})
, or equivalently for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. An important property in relation with the polynomial order of
F
-stable subgroups of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is that

d
is a regular number for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
if and only if a Sylow
d
-torus
{\mathbf{S}}
of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
is such that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
is a torus

[
GM
, Example 3.5.7]
. In particular we get that if
w\varphi_{0}\in W\varphi_{0}
is
\zeta
-regular for some
\zeta
of order
d
and if
u\in\rho^{-1}(w)
then the Sylow
d
-torus of
({\mathbf{T}},uF)
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},uF)
.

See
[
S10a
, Table 1]
for a list of regular numbers for each type of
(W,\varphi_{0})
.

Keeping the pair
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
of arbitrary type, we define the extended Weyl group (or Tits subgroup, see
[
Tits66
]
) already mentioned

{V}:=\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\right\rangle\leq{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
and the toral group
{H}=V\cap{\mathbf{T}}
. They are both finite and we clearly have
\rho(V)=W({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\cong V/H
.

Recall the choice of a regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
and the group
E({{\mathbf{G}}})
acting on both
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
, see
Section

2.C
. We set
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}){\mathbf{T}}
. We form the semidirect product
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes E({{\mathbf{G}}})
and see its elements outside of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
as acting on
{{\mathbf{G}}}
.

Here is the slight variant of the criterion
[
CS19
, Thm 4.3]
for
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
of
Section

2.D
that we will use.

Proposition
\@upn
4.3

Let
d\geq 1
be a regular number for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. Assume there exists an element
u\in V
such that, denoting by
{\mathbf{S}}
the Sylow
d
-torus of
({\mathbf{T}},uF)
, the following properties hold:

(i)

\rho(u)\varphi_{0}\in W\varphi_{0}
is a
\zeta
-regular element of
W\varphi_{0}
for some
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
d
;

(ii)

\rho(V_{d})=W_{d}
with
{{V_{d}}}:=V^{uF}
and
{W_{d}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{W}(\rho(u)\varphi_{0})
.

(iii)

Set
{{{\widehat{V}}_{d}}}:=(VE({{\mathbf{G}}}))^{uF}/\left\langle uF\right\rangle\geq V_{d}\geq{H_{d}}:=H^{uF}
. There exists an extension map
\Lambda_{0}
with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd V_{d}
such that

(iii.1)

\Lambda_{0}
is
\widehat{V}_{d}
-equivariant; and

(iii.2)

if
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is not cyclic, then for any
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(H_{d})
,
\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)
extends to
(\widehat{V}_{d})_{\lambda}
.

(iv)

Set
{T:={\mathbf{T}}^{uF}}={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}
,
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}
,
\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}E({{\mathbf{G}}})}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}/\left\langle uF\right\rangle
and
\widehat{W}_{d}:=\widehat{N}/T
.
For
\widetilde{\xi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{uF})
set
W_{\widetilde{\xi}}:=N_{\widetilde{\xi}}/T\unlhd\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi}):=({\widehat{W}_{d}})_{\left.\widetilde{\xi}\right\rceil_{T}}
. Then for every
\widetilde{\xi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{uF})
, maximal extendibility holds for
W_{\widetilde{\xi}}\unlhd\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})
.

(v)

Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N\unlhd\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}
.

Then conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
2.19
and
2.20
are satisfied by
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.

Proof.

We abbreviate
{{\widetilde{T}}}:=\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}
. The first point of the definition of
\mathbf{B}(d)
in
2.20
is given by (v). For the second point in
\mathbf{B}(d)
, i.e., an equivariant form of maximal extendibility for
\widetilde{T}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
, it is a consequence of the assumptions (i), (ii) and (iii), as explained in the proof of
[
CS19
, Thm 4.2]
, see also
Proposition

2.3
.

We now turn to
\mathbf{A}(d)
. Note that the proposition reproduces
[
CS19
, Thm 4.3]
verbatim except for (iv). We follow below the original proof of
\mathbf{A}(d)
combining the ones of
[
CS17a
, Prop. 5.13]
and
[
CS17b
, Thm 4.3]
. We only stress the differences due to our simpler assumption (iv).

Assumption (iii) along with
Proposition

2.3
yield an
\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map
\Lambda
for
T\unlhd N
, see also
Proposition

4.8
(e) below for an alternative proof. Let us recall also that if
\widetilde{\xi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{T})=\mathrm{Lin}(\widetilde{T})
and
\xi:=\left.\widetilde{\xi}\right\rceil_{T}
, then
N_{\widetilde{\xi}}\unlhd N_{\xi}
and both are normal in
\widehat{N}_{\xi}
.
For every
t\in\widetilde{T}
the character
\nu_{t}\in\mathrm{Lin}(N_{\xi})
defined by

\Lambda(\xi)^{t}=\Lambda(\xi)\nu_{t}

satisfies
\nu_{t}(N_{\widetilde{\xi}})=1
. For a fixed
\widetilde{\xi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{T})
the map
t\mapsto\nu_{t}
is a surjection
\widetilde{T}\to\mathrm{Irr}(N_{\xi}/N_{\widetilde{\xi}})=\mathrm{Lin}(N_{\xi}/N_{\widetilde{\xi}})
with kernel
\widetilde{T}_{\Lambda(\xi)}
.
Clifford theory (
2.1
) provides an
\widehat{N}
-equivariant parametrization

\displaystyle\Pi:\

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(N)

\displaystyle(\xi,\eta)\longmapsto(\Lambda(\xi)\eta)^{N}

of
\mathrm{Irr}(N)
by the set
\mathcal{P}
of
N
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(\xi,\eta)
with
\xi\in\mathrm{Irr}(T)
and
\eta\in\mathrm{Irr}(W_{\xi})
. By the above one clearly has
\Pi(\xi,\eta)^{t}=\Pi(\xi,\eta\nu_{t})
for every
t\in\widetilde{T}
.

Letting now
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(N)
we must check
(\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi_{0}}=\widetilde{N}_{\chi_{0}}\widehat{N}_{\chi_{0}}
for some
\widetilde{T}
-conjugate
\chi_{0}
of
\chi
.

We choose
(\xi,\eta)
as above such that
\chi=\Pi(\xi,\eta)
. Let
\widetilde{\xi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{T})
be an extension of
\xi
.
Let
\eta_{0}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\eta\right\rceil_{W_{\widetilde{\xi}}})
. By the assumption (iv),

\eta_{0}
extends to
\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
and hence
there exists some extension
\widetilde{\eta}_{0}
of
\eta_{0}
to
(W_{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
that further extends to
\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
. The character
\eta^{\prime}:=\widetilde{\eta}_{0}^{W_{\xi}}
is
\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
-invariant and irreducible thanks to Clifford theory (
2.1
), so
(\xi,\eta^{\prime})
is another pair as above and we can define
\chi_{0}:=\Pi(\xi,\eta^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Irr}(N)
. Note that
\chi_{0}
is in the
\widetilde{T}
-orbit of
\chi
by the surjectivity of
t\mapsto\nu_{t}
recalled above.

Let
x\in(\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi_{0}}
. By the properties of
\Pi
we can assume
x=\widetilde{n}\widehat{n}
with
\widetilde{n}\in\widetilde{N}_{\xi}
and
\widehat{n}\in\widehat{N}_{\xi}
after factoring out an element of
N
.
Then
\widetilde{n}=tn
with
t\in\widetilde{T}
and
n\in N_{\xi}
.
Arguing as in the proof of
[
CS17a
, Prop. 5.13]
we get

\displaystyle\chi_{0}=\Pi(\xi,\eta^{\prime})=\Pi(\xi,\eta^{\prime})^{\widetilde{n}\widehat{n}}=

\displaystyle\ \Pi(\xi,(\eta^{\prime}\nu_{t})^{n\widehat{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Pi(\xi,(\eta^{\prime}\nu_{t})^{\widehat{n}})\text{ using }(\eta^{\prime})^{n}=\eta^{\prime}\text{ since }n\in N_{\xi}.

This implies
\eta^{\prime}=(\eta^{\prime}\nu_{t})^{\widehat{n}}
and therefore
\widehat{n}
stabilizes
\left.\eta\right\rceil_{W_{\widetilde{\xi}}}
.
Accordingly
\widehat{n}\in\widehat{N}_{\xi}
and
\widehat{n}T\in W_{\xi}\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
.
By what was recalled above,
\eta^{\prime}
is
\widehat{n}
-invariant. This yields
\Pi(\xi,\eta^{\prime})^{\widehat{n}}=\Pi(\xi,\eta^{\prime})
and therefore

x\in\widetilde{N}_{\chi_{0}}\widehat{N}_{\chi_{0}}
as claimed.

It remains to show that
\chi_{0}
extends to
\widehat{N}_{\chi_{0}}
. By assumption
\eta_{0}
extends to
\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
and hence there exists some extension
\widehat{\eta}_{0}
of
\widetilde{\eta}_{0}
to
(\widehat{N}_{\chi_{{}_{0}},\xi})_{\eta_{0}}/T
since the latter is a subgroup of
\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})_{\eta_{0}}
. The induced character
(\widehat{\eta}_{0})^{\widehat{K}(\widetilde{\xi})}
is the extension of
\eta^{\prime}
required in the end of the proof of
[
CS17b
, Thm 4.3]
and denoted there as Res
{}^{{\widehat{N}}_{\xi}}_{{\widehat{N}}_{\xi,\eta}}(\widehat{\eta})\in\mathrm{Irr}({{\widehat{N}}_{\xi,\eta}})
.
∎

4.B
Doubly regular numbers and Sylow tori

We now focus on the case when
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
and
\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}=\mathrm{B}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
with
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
, with Frobenius endomorphism
\overline{F}\colon{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}\to{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
,
F=\left.\overline{F}\right\rceil_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}^{\epsilon}(q)\leq{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{\overline{F}}=\mathrm{B}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
as in
2.E
. As in
[
S10a
,
S10b
,
MS16
]
we derive the results in type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
by some transfer of the analogous results in
\overline{{{\mathbf{G}}}}
. We also assume that
d\geq 3
satisfies the assumption of
Theorem

4.1
. In view of the list of regular numbers in types
\mathrm{D}
and
\mathrm{B}
, see for instance
[
S10a
, Table 1]
, it corresponds to the following.

Definition
\@upn
4.4 — Doubly regular integers for
\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
.

An integer
d\geq 3
is called
doubly regular
for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
if
d
is regular for both
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and
({\overline{\mathbf{G}}},\overline{F})
, i.e.
d\mid 2l
with ratio satisfying
(-1)^{\frac{2l}{d}}={\epsilon}
.

This is equivalent to the property that the centralizer in
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
of a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
is a (maximal) torus.
Using
Definition

4.2
, this can be checked by noting that the ratio of polynomial orders
P_{{\overline{\mathbf{G}}},\overline{F}}(X)/P_{{{\mathbf{G}}},F}(X)
is
X^{l}(X^{l}+{\epsilon})
, not divisible by the
d
-th cyclotomic polynomial if
d
is doubly regular.

Recall we identify the Weyl group
\overline{W}
of type
\mathrm{B}_{l}
with
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
, where for a set
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
{{{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}}}
denotes the group of permutations
\pi
of
I\sqcup-I
with
\pi(-i)=-\pi(i)
for every
i\in I
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
corresponds to
I={\underline{l}}
.
Recall also
W
the normal subgroup of
\overline{W}
corresponding to the Weyl group of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
. Then
\varphi_{0}
associated with
F\colon{{\mathbf{G}}}\to{{\mathbf{G}}}
is
1
if
{\epsilon}=1
, while
\varphi_{0}=\overline{\rho}(n_{1}^{\circ})=(1,-1)\in\overline{W}
when
{\epsilon}=-1
.
We collect some statements on regular elements of
\overline{W}
in the following.

Lemma
\@upn
4.5

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer and
\zeta_{d}
a primitive
d
-th root of unity.

(a)

The set of
\zeta_{d}
-regular elements of
W\varphi_{0}
forms a
W
-orbit.

(b)

w_{\text{Cox}}:=(1,2,\dots,l,-1,\dots,-l+1,-l)
is a Coxeter element of
\overline{W}
. Every regular element
w
of
\overline{W}
is conjugate to a power of
w_{Cox}
. If the order of
w
is
d
, every
w
-orbit in
{\underline{l}}\cup-{\underline{l}}
has length
d
.

(c)

If
w
is a regular element of
\overline{W}
of order
d
and
I\subseteq{\underline{l}}
is a union of
w
-orbits, then the projection
w_{I}
of
w
to
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
is a regular element of order
d
of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
. All regular elements of
\overline{W}
of a given order form a single conjugacy class of
\overline{W}
.

(d)

Assume
d\geq 3
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. If
w
is a regular element of
\overline{W}
of order
d
, then it is a
\zeta_{d}
-regular element of
W\varphi_{0}
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from the definition, see also
[
Br
, 5.14.1]
. Part (b) follows from Appendix 1 of
[
BM97
]
or from Remark 3.2 of
[
S10b
]
. For Part (c), recall that
d
is a regular number for
\overline{W}
and therefore
d\mid 2l
according to Table 1 of
[
S10a
]
. Now the projection
w_{I}
of
w
is a regular element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
since the orbits of
w
and
w_{I}
on
\pm I
have the structure required in Remark 3.2 of
[
S10b
]
. Part (d) is again derived from the characterization of
\zeta_{d}
-regular elements given in Table 1 of
[
S10b
]
. ∎

4.C
The doubly regular case. Extended Weyl groups

In the following, we assume
d
to be doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
in the sense of
Definition

4.4
and show that
Proposition

4.3
applies in that situation to establish
Theorem

4.1
(a).
We check assumptions (i)-(iii) of
Proposition

4.3
in the present section and assumptions (iv) and (v) in the next.

We define first an element
u\in V
. Recall that we have chosen
\varpi
to be a primitive
\gcd(2,q-1)^{2}
-th root of unity, see
2.E
.

Notation
\@upn
4.6

Recall
V=\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\right\rangle
,
H=V\cap{\mathbf{T}}
and set

V\leq{{\overline{V}}}:=\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi({\overline{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\right\rangle\leq{\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{T}})^{F_{p}}.

Let
{{{v_{\mathrm{B}}}}}\in{\overline{V}}
be chosen as in
[
CS19
, Sect. 5.A]
, i.e.

{v_{\mathrm{B}}}:=\big(\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)\mathbf{n}_{{\alpha}_{2}}(1)\cdots\mathbf{n}_{{\alpha}_{l}}(1)\big)^{\frac{2l}{d}}\ \ \ \text{ and set }\ \ \ t=\mathbf{h}_{{\underline{l}}}(\omega)=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathbf{h}_{e_{i}}(\omega)\in{\mathbf{T}}

with

{{\omega}}\in{\mathbb{F}^{\times}}
, a primitive
\gcd(2,q-1)^{3}
-th root of unity with
\omega^{2}=\varpi
. Let

\displaystyle{u}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}({v_{\mathrm{B}}})^{t}&\text{if }{\epsilon}=\,\,\,1,\\
({v_{\mathrm{B}}})^{t}\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(\varpi)^{-1}&\text{if }{\epsilon}=-1.\end{cases}

(4.1)

Let
{V_{d}}=V\cap{{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}
and
{H_{d}}=V_{d}\cap{\mathbf{T}}
.

Basic properties of the Tits subgroup imply the following.

Lemma
\@upn
4.7

(a)

\overline{V}\cap{{\mathbf{G}}}=V
and
\overline{V}\cap{\mathbf{T}}=V\cap{\mathbf{T}}=H
is elementary abelian of order
\gcd(2,q-1)^{l}
with
V/H\cong W
,
\overline{V}/H\cong\overline{W}
.

(b)

\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)^{t}=\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)\mathbf{h}_{e_{1}}(\omega^{2})=\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(\varpi)=n_{1}^{\circ}.

(c)

u\in V=V^{t}
.

Proof.

From the Chevalley relations or
[
Tits66
]
, we know for any type that the group
V=\left\langle\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(1)\mid{\alpha}\in\Phi({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\right\rangle
is generated by the
\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(1)
for
\delta\in\Delta
in the notation of
Section

2.C
. Those elements satisfy
\mathbf{n}_{\delta}(1)^{2}=\mathbf{h}_{\delta}(-1)
and the braid relations. From the Coxeter presentation of
W({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
it is then easy to see that
H
is the direct product
\Pi_{\delta\in\Delta}\left\langle\mathbf{h}_{\delta}(-1)\right\rangle
, with
V/H\cong W({{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})
by
\rho
. We get (a) by applying this simultaneously to
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
, noting that
\overline{V}\cap{\mathbf{T}}\geq V\cap{\mathbf{T}}
but have same cardinality.

(b) is clear from the definitions given in
Section

2.C
and
e_{i}(t)=\omega^{2}=\varpi
when
e_{i}
(
i\in{\underline{l}}
) is seen as an element of
\Phi({\overline{\mathbf{G}}},{\mathbf{T}})\subseteq X({\mathbf{T}})
. One shows similarly that
\mathbf{n}_{{\alpha}}(1)^{t}=\mathbf{n}_{\alpha}(\pm 1)
when
{\alpha}=\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}
with
i\neq j
in
{\underline{l}}
. This implies
V^{t}=V
.

When
{\epsilon}=1
the assumption that
d
is doubly regular implies that
2l/d
is even and therefore
v_{\mathrm{B}}\in V
since all squares of elements of
\overline{V}
are in
V
. When
{\epsilon}=-1
we have
v_{\mathrm{B}}\in\overline{V}\setminus V=\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)V
since
\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)\mathbf{n}_{{\alpha}_{2}}(1)\cdots\mathbf{n}_{{\alpha}_{l}}(1)\in\overline{V}\setminus V
and
2l/d
is odd. But then (b) implies
v_{\mathrm{B}}.{}^{t}\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(\varpi)^{-1}\in\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)V\mathbf{n}_{e_{1}}(1)^{-1}=V
and we get our claim that
u\in V^{t}=V
.
This finishes the proof of (c).
∎

We now prove the requirements (i)–(iii) of
Proposition

4.3
in a form slightly weakened for rank 4, replacing
E({{\mathbf{G}}})
with
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
as defined in
Section

2.E
.

Proposition
\@upn
4.8

Let
u\in V
be as in (
4.1
).
Let
\varphi_{0}
be defined from
F
as in
Corollary

3.7
.
Then

(a)

\rho(u)\varphi_{0}
is a
\zeta_{d}
-regular element of
W\varphi_{0}
for some primitive (complex)
d
-th root
\zeta_{d}
of
1
;

(b)

\rho(V_{d})={\operatorname{C}}_{W}(\rho(u)\varphi_{0})
;

(c)

there exists a
{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
-equivariant extension map
\Lambda_{0}
with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd V_{d}
and,

(d)

if
{\epsilon}=1
, then
\Lambda_{0}
can be chosen such that for every
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(H_{d})
,
\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)
extends to an irreducible character of
{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)_{\lambda}/\left\langle uF\right\rangle
.

(e)

Recall
{\mathbf{N}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
. There is a
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{N}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
-equivariant extension map with respect to
{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}\unlhd{\mathbf{N}}^{uF}
.

Proof.

Set
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}:={\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{V}}({}^{t}{(uF)})={\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{V}}(v_{\mathrm{B}})
as in
[
CS19
, Sect. 5.B]
,
\overline{H}_{d}={\overline{V}\!_{d}}\cap{\mathbf{T}}=\overline{V}\cap{\operatorname{C}}_{{\mathbf{T}}}({}^{t}{(uF)})=\overline{V}\cap{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}=H_{d}
thanks to
Lemma

4.7
(a). By
Lemma

2.23
,
W\varphi_{0}
and
W\overline{\rho}(n_{1}^{\circ})
are equal when
{\epsilon}=-1
. Regarding
\rho(u)\varphi_{0}
we have
\rho(u)\varphi_{0}=\overline{\rho}({}{v_{\mathrm{B}}}^{t})=\rho({}{v_{\mathrm{B}}})
and this is a
\zeta_{d}
-regular element in
W\varphi_{0}
since it is one in
\overline{W}
as proven in
[
CS19
, Lem. 5.4]
. Again, by
[
CS19
, Lem. 5.4]
the groups
\overline{{\mathbf{N}}}={\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{T}})
and
{\mathbf{T}}
satisfy
\overline{{\mathbf{N}}}^{{v_{\mathrm{B}}}F}=(\overline{{\mathbf{T}}})^{{v_{\mathrm{B}}}F}{\overline{V}\!_{d}}
or equivalently

\overline{\rho}({\overline{V}\!_{d}})={\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{W}}(\overline{\rho}({v_{\mathrm{B}}})).

[formula]

On the other hand
V_{d}={\operatorname{C}}_{V}(uF)={\operatorname{C}}_{V^{t}}(({v_{\mathrm{B}}})^{t})=({\operatorname{C}}_{V}({v_{\mathrm{B}}}))^{t}
by
Lemma

4.7
(c) and therefore
\rho(V_{d})=\rho({\operatorname{C}}_{V}(uF))={\operatorname{C}}_{W}(\rho({v_{\mathrm{B}}}))
as claimed. This ensures parts (a) and (b).

According to
[
CS19
, Thm 5.5]
maximal extendibility holds with respect to
H_{d}=\overline{H}_{d}\unlhd{\overline{V}\!_{d}}
, hence also with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
. Let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}
be an extension map with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
, which can be assumed to be
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
-equivariant. Since
V_{d}=V\cap{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}=V^{t}\cap{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}
and
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}=\overline{V}^{t}\cap{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}
, we have
V_{d}\unlhd{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
. Then maximal extendibility holds with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd V_{d}
with the extension map
\Lambda_{0}
defined by
\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)=\left.\Lambda_{\mathrm{B}}(\lambda)\right\rceil_{V_{d}}
for
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(H_{d})
. Moreover,
\Lambda_{0}
is
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
-equivariant. So, to obtain our claim (c) about equivariance, it suffices to show that the automorphisms of
V_{d}
induced by
{\operatorname{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
are also induced by
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}={\mathrm{C}}_{\overline{V}^{t}}{(uF)}
. Since
[F_{p},\gamma]=[F_{p},V]=1
one has
{\operatorname{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)={\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
where
{\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)={\operatorname{C}}_{V{}}(uF)=V_{d}
or
{\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)=V_{d}\left\langle n\gamma\right\rangle
for some
n\in V
. It now suffices to show that
nn_{1}^{\circ}\in{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
. Observe first that
\overline{V}^{t}=V\left\langle n_{1}^{\circ}\right\rangle
by
Lemma

4.7
(b) and therefore
V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle
acts on
\overline{V}^{t}
by inner automorphisms. Then
n\gamma
commutes with
uF
and therefore with
(v_{\mathrm{B}})^{t}
from the definition of
u
in
Notation

4.6
. Then
nn_{1}^{\circ}
commutes with
(v_{\mathrm{B}})^{t}
, therefore
nn_{1}^{\circ}\in{\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{V}^{t}}((v_{\mathrm{B}})^{t})={\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
. So the action of
n\gamma
on
V_{d}
is also induced by an element of
{\overline{V}\!_{d}}^{t}
. This proves (c).

For part (d), let
\pi_{u}\colon C:={\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)\to{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)/\left\langle uF\right\rangle
be the quotient map. One has
\pi_{u}(C)_{\lambda}=\pi_{u}(C)_{\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)}
by the equivariance of
\Lambda_{0}
we have just proved. So to get our claim it suffices to show that
\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)
extends to its stabilizer in
\pi_{u}(C)
. Now it extends first to
\pi_{u}(V_{d}\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle)_{\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)}=\pi_{u}((V_{d})_{\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)}\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle)
since
F_{p}
acts trivially on
V_{d}
. But now
\pi_{u}((V_{d})_{\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)}\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle)
has index
\leq 2
in
\pi_{u}(C)_{\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)}
since
V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\unrhd V\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
has index 2 and therefore the intersections with
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}
have index
\leq 2
and the same holds in turn for the stabilizers of
\Lambda_{0}(\lambda)
there. Then
Proposition

2.2
(a) gives our claim.

We now turn to (e). Set
T:={\mathbf{T}}^{uF}
,
N:={\mathbf{N}}^{uF}
. Point (b) implies
TV_{d}=N
while point (c) gives us the extension map
\Lambda_{0}
with respect to
H_{d}\unlhd V_{d}
. According to
Proposition

2.3
, we then get an extension map
\Lambda
with respect to
T\unlhd N
, where for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(T)
and since
\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{H_{d}}\in\mathrm{Irr}(H_{d})
,

\left.\Lambda(\lambda)\right\rceil_{(V_{d})_{\lambda}}=\Lambda_{0}(\left.\lambda\right\rceil_{H_{d}}).

Since
\Lambda_{0}
is
{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
-equivariant,
\Lambda
is therefore also
{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
-equivariant.

So to get our claim about equivariance it is enough to show that
{\operatorname{C}}_{{\mathbf{N}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
acts on
T
by elements of

{\mathrm{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
. A sufficient condition is that
{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}{\operatorname{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)={\operatorname{C}}_{{\mathbf{N}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF),
in
{{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}(G)
.
We clearly have
{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}\leq{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}{\operatorname{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)\leq{\operatorname{C}}_{{\mathbf{N}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)
and using Lang’s theorem the quotient mod
{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}
satisfies

{\operatorname{C}}_{V{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF)/H_{d}\leq{\operatorname{C}}_{W{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}(uF).

We have to show that this is an equality. Since
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})=\left\langle\gamma,F_{p}\right\rangle
with
F_{p}
acting trivially on both
V
and
W
, it suffices to show
{\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)/H_{d}={\operatorname{C}}_{W\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)
. As seen before, the term on the right is
{\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{W}{}}(\overline{\rho}(uF))={\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{W}{}}(\overline{\rho}(v_{\mathrm{B}}))
. On the other hand
{\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(uF)={\operatorname{C}}_{V\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(v_{\mathrm{B}}^{t})\ni n\gamma
implies
nn_{1}^{\circ}\in{\operatorname{C}}_{\overline{V}^{t}}(v_{\mathrm{B}}^{t})
in the notation of the proof of (c) above. This gives our claim by equation (1).
∎

4.D
The doubly regular case. Relative Weyl groups

Our next step in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
through the criterion in
Proposition

4.3
leads us to study certain subgroups of the so-called relative Weyl groups
W(\lambda):={\operatorname{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{uF}}({\mathbf{T}},\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathbf{T}}^{uF})
and
u
as in
Notation

4.6
. In the following, we ensure assumption (iv) of
Proposition

4.3
.

As in
[
CS19
, Sect. 6.1–2]
, we determine
W(\lambda)
using computations in the dual group, see also the proof of
[
MS16
, Thm 3.17]
. The character
\lambda
corresponds to some semisimple element
s\in{{\mathbf{H}}}^{F}
which centralizes a Sylow
d
-torus. As before, we assume that
d\geq 3
and that
d
is doubly regular in the sense of
Definition

4.4
.

Let us recall from
3.3
the notation
\overline{W}={\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}\unrhd W={{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm l}}
and for
I\subseteq{\underline{l}}
, the (parabolic) subgroup
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}
of
\overline{W}={\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
fixing every element of
{\underline{l}}\setminus I
and stabilizing
I
. We also recall the notation
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathbb{M}}=\prod_{I\in\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I}\cong\prod_{I\in\mathbb{M}}{\mathcal{S}}_{I}
for any partition
M=\sqcup_{I\in\mathbb{M}}I
of a subset
M\subseteq{\underline{l}}
. Recall
{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm I}}
the group of elements of
W
that fix any element of
\underline{l}\setminus I
.

The following statement on permutation groups is key for ensuring assumption
4.3
(iv).

Proposition
\@upn
4.9

Let
l\geq 1
,
{J^{\prime}},{J^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq{\underline{l}}
disjoint (possibly empty) subsets,
\mathbb{I}
a partition of
{\underline{l}}\setminus(J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime})
,

{P}:={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathbb{I}}\leq\overline{W},

{K}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{W}}(P)
and
w\in K
a regular element of
\overline{W}
seen as reflection group in
\operatorname{GL}_{{\mathbb{R}}}({\mathbb{R}}\overline{\Phi})
. Then maximal extendibility holds with respect to
{\mathrm{C}}_{P}(w)\unlhd{\mathrm{C}}_{K}(w)
.

Note that in general
P
is not a parabolic subgroup of
\overline{W}
.

Proof.

Let
d
be the order of
w
and set

P_{0}:={\mathrm{C}}_{P}(w){\text{ and }}K_{0}:={\mathrm{C}}_{K}(w).

Let us first determine the group
K
. Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{J\cup J^{\prime}}
be such that

-

\tau=1
if
|J^{\prime}|\neq|J^{\prime\prime}|
, or

-

\tau(J^{\prime})=J^{\prime\prime}
,
\tau^{2}=1
if
|J^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime\prime}|

Let
S\leq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{l}
be the subgroup of permutations
\pi
with

-

\pi(\mathbb{I})=\mathbb{I}
,

-

\pi(i)<\pi(i^{\prime})
for every
I\in\mathbb{I}
and
i,i^{\prime}\in I
with
i<i^{\prime}
, and

-

\pi(i)=i
for every
i\in J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime}
.

Any element of
\overline{W}
normalizing
P
must have an image in the symmetric group on
{\underline{l}}
stabilizing
J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime}
and its complement, so it’s easy to see that the determination of
K
boils down to the cases where

J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime}={\underline{l}}
, or
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
. We get in the general case

K={\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{W}}(P)=\left(({\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}})\rtimes\left\langle\tau\right\rangle\right)\times\left(\prod_{I\in\mathbb{I}}\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I},-\operatorname{id}_{I}\right\rangle\right)\rtimes S\ \leq\ {\mathcal{S}}_{\pm(J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime})}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm\cup_{I\in\mathbb{I}}I}.

It is clear that the question splits along the orbits of
K
on
{\underline{l}}
, the assumption of regularity remaining thanks to
Lemma

4.5
(c). We now assume that
K
is transitive on
{\underline{l}}
and treat separately the following cases

1a.

J^{\prime}={\underline{l}}
,

1b.

J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime}={\underline{l}}
and
|J^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime\prime}|
,

2.

J^{\prime}=J^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
and all elements of
\mathbb{I}
have same cardinality.

(1) Assume now

J^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime\prime}={\underline{l}}
, so that

P={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}\ \unlhd\ K=({\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}})\rtimes\left\langle\tau\right\rangle\ \ni\ w.

Assume first that
w(J^{\prime}\cup-J^{\prime})=J^{\prime}\cup-J^{\prime}
. Then we can write
w
as
w^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}
with
w^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}
and
w^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}
. This leads to

P_{0}=P_{0}^{\prime}\times P_{0}^{\prime\prime},

where
P_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}}(w^{\prime})
and
P_{0}^{\prime\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}}(w^{\prime\prime})
. Set
\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}}(w^{\prime})
and
\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}}(w^{\prime\prime})
.

In case (1a) above, i.e.
J^{\prime}={\underline{l}}
, the sought maximal extendibility holds by
Proposition

2.2
(a) since
\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}/P_{0}^{\prime}
is cyclic of order
\leq 2
.

Assume now (1b), i.e.
|J^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime\prime}|=l/2
. We first keep the assumption
w(J^{\prime}\cup-J^{\prime})=J^{\prime}\cup-J^{\prime}
. According to
Lemma

4.5
,
w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}
are regular elements of the same order of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime\prime}}
, respectively. As all regular elements of
\overline{W}
of the same order are
W
-conjugate by
Lemma

4.5
(a), there exists some involution
\tau_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm l}
with
(w^{\prime})^{\tau_{0}}=w^{\prime\prime}
. Then
K_{0}=(\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}\times\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime\prime})\rtimes\left\langle\tau_{0}\right\rangle
and
Proposition

2.2
(c) applies.

Assume now
w(\pm J^{\prime})=\pm J^{\prime\prime}.
Note that
w^{2}
is regular as well according to
Lemma

4.5
(b). By the structure of regular elements recalled in
Lemma

4.5
(b), if
P_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{D}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}}(w^{2})
, then
P_{0}^{\prime}
and
P_{0}
are isomorphic via
x\mapsto x\cdot x^{w}
.

Set
\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm J^{\prime}}}(w^{2})
and
\Delta\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}:=\{pp^{w}\mid p\in\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}\}
. We observe
K_{0}={\mathrm{C}}_{K}(w)=\left\langle\Delta\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime},w\right\rangle
with
w\in\operatorname{Z}(K_{0})
. We have maximal extendibility with respect to
P_{0}^{\prime}\unlhd\overline{P}^{\prime}_{0}
by
Proposition

2.2
(a), and therefore also with respect to
P_{0}\unlhd\Delta\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}
by the above description. But now, since
w\in\operatorname{Z}(K_{0})
, any character of an intermediate group
A
is stable under
w
and extends to
A\left\langle w\right\rangle
by
Proposition

2.2
(a) again. This shows maximal extendibility with respect to
P_{0}\unlhd K_{0}=\Delta\overline{P}_{0}^{\prime}\left\langle w\right\rangle
.

(2) We now consider the case where
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
and
K
permutes transitively the elements of
\mathbb{I}
.

We have seen that

P={\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbb{I}}\ \unlhd\ K=\left(\prod_{I\in\mathbb{I}}\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{I},-\operatorname{id}_{I}\right\rangle\right)\rtimes S\ \ni\ w,

where
S\leq{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm{\underline{l}}}
is the subgroup preserving
\mathbb{I}
and the ordering on each subset
I\in\mathbb{I}
. Note that
w
acts on
\mathbb{I}
since
w
stabilizes
P
. Recall that
d
is the order of
w
.

Assume now that all
w
-orbits in
\mathbb{I}
have the same length
b
(a divisor of
d
). Set
w^{\prime}:=w^{b}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm\mathbb{I}}
. Note that
w^{\prime}
is again a regular element according to
Lemma

4.5
(b). For each
I\in\mathbb{I}
let
w^{\prime}_{I}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
be the permutation induced by
w^{\prime}
. Note that
w^{\prime}_{I}
is a regular element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
according to
Lemma

4.5
(c), where we consider
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
as a reflection group of type
\mathrm{B}_{|I|}
.

The element
w^{b}
can be written as the product of elements
w^{\prime}_{I}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
(
I\in\mathbb{I}
). Every
w^{\prime}_{I}
is a regular element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I}
of order
\frac{d}{b}
.
We write
{\mathcal{O}}
for the set of
w
-orbits in
\mathbb{I}
and for every
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
let us fix some
I_{O}\in O
. We define
\Delta_{O}:{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm I_{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm\bigcup_{I\in O}I}
by
x\longmapsto x\cdot x^{w}\cdot x^{w^{2}}\cdots x^{w^{b-1}}
.

For
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
set
P_{I_{O}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{I_{O}}}(w^{\prime}_{I_{O}})
and
P_{O}:=\Delta_{O}(P_{I_{O}})
. The group
P_{0}
is the direct product of the groups
P_{O}
(
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
).

Recall
K={\mathrm{N}}_{\overline{W}}(P)=\left(\prod_{I\in\mathbb{I}}\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{I},-\operatorname{id}_{I}\right\rangle\right)\rtimes S
. Set
K_{I}:={\mathrm{C}}_{\left\langle{\mathcal{S}}_{I},-\operatorname{id}_{I}\right\rangle}(w^{\prime}_{I})
and
K_{O}:=\left\langle\Delta_{O}(K_{I_{O}}),w_{O}\right\rangle\leq K_{0}
where
w_{O}
is the projection of
w
on
{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm\bigcup_{I\in O}I}
.

Since all regular elements of order
\frac{d}{b}
are conjugate in
\overline{W}
thanks to
Lemma

4.5
(c), we find a subgroup
S^{\prime}\leq K
such that

K_{0}=\left\langle K_{O}\mid O\in{\mathcal{O}}\right\rangle\rtimes S^{\prime},

where
S^{\prime}\cong{\mathcal{S}}_{|{\mathcal{O}}|}
.
Note that
w_{O}\in\operatorname{Z}(K_{O})
by the definition of
K_{0}
.

We can then conclude, as at the end of (1) above, that maximal extendibility holds with respect to
P_{O}\unlhd K_{O}
since
\Delta_{O}(K_{I_{O}})/P_{O}\cong K_{I_{O}}/P_{I_{O}}
is cyclic and
w_{O}
is central in
K_{O}
. Then we get maximal extendibility with respect to
P_{0}\unlhd K_{0}
by
Proposition

2.2
(c).

Assume not all
w
-orbits on
\mathbb{I}
have same cardinality. For
b\geq 1
dividing
d
, let
\mathbb{I}_{b}
be the set of
I\in\mathbb{I}
such that
b
is the smallest divisor of
d
with
w^{b}(\pm I)=\pm I
.
Then
K_{0}
is the direct product of groups
K_{\mathbb{I}_{b}}:=K_{0}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{\pm\mathbb{I}_{b}}
(
b\geq 1
) while
P_{0}
is the direct product of its intersections with those factors. We are then reduced to the case of a single
b
treated above.
∎

We are now back with
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
,
{\mathbf{T}}
and a doubly regular
d\geq 3
as in
Section

4.C
. Recall also the regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}){{\mathbf{G}}}
with
F
extended to
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}=\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}){\mathbf{T}}
. The element
u\in{\operatorname{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
from
Notation

4.6
is chosen such that
{\mathbf{T}}
is the centralizer of a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},uF)
by
Definition

4.2
.

We show below that in this case of a doubly regular
d
the above proposition about centralizers in Weyl groups essentially implies the point (iv) in
Proposition

4.3
. The proof goes through a recasting of the question inside the dual group following ideas from
[
MS16
, §3.D]
,
[
CS19
, §6]
.

Corollary
\@upn
4.10

Let
\widetilde{}\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{uF})
. Set
{{\mathbf{W}}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}{}}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}\unlhd\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}
and
\lambda:=\left.\widetilde{}\lambda\right\rceil_{{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}}
.
Then maximal extendibility holds for
{{\mathbf{W}}}{}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\unlhd{\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}}
.

Proof.

As explained in the proof of
[
CS19
, Prop. 6.2]
, the duality between
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\geq{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{\widetilde{{{\mathbf{H}}}}}\twoheadrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}={{\mathbf{H}}}
with Frobenius endomorphism
uF
allows us to associate
({\mathbf{T}},\lambda)
to
({\mathbf{T}}^{*},s)
and
(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}},\widehat{}\lambda)
to
(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{*},\widehat{s})
with
\widetilde{s}\in(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{*})^{uF}
and
\widetilde{s}\mapsto s
. The stabilizers
{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\unlhd{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}
become
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{W}}}(\widetilde{s})^{uF}\unlhd{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{W}}}(s)^{uF}
, see also
[
CS13
, Cor. 3.3]
. Note that everything is now written inside
{{\mathbf{H}}}
since
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{{{\mathbf{H}}}}}}(\widetilde{s})/\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{H}})={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}^{\circ}(s)
. We denote by
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{W}}}^{\circ}(s):={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{H}}}^{\circ}(s)}({\mathbf{T}}^{*})/{\mathbf{T}}^{*}
the Weyl group of the latter.
The automorphism
\gamma
acts on
{{\mathbf{H}}}
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and we can see also
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}
as
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{H}}}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}(\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}^{*})/\overline{{\mathbf{T}}}^{*}
such that
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}_{\lambda}^{uF}
corresponds via duality to
{\mathrm{C}}_{\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}}(s)^{uF}
, hence we study
{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\lambda}^{uF}\lhd\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}_{\lambda}^{uF}
via

{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{W}}}^{\circ}(s)^{uF}\unlhd{\mathrm{C}}_{\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}}(s)^{uF}.

Note
{{\mathbf{W}}}\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle=\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}
is a Weyl group of type
\mathrm{B}_{l}
. In the latter this rewrites as
{\mathrm{C}}_{P}(u\varphi_{0})\unlhd{\mathrm{C}}_{K}(u\varphi_{0})
where
P={\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{W}}}^{\circ}(s)
,
K={\mathrm{C}}_{\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}}(s)
and
P\unlhd K
. By
Corollary

3.7
(a),
P
has the structure studied in
Proposition

4.9
above, as
d
was doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and hence
u\varphi_{0}
is a regular element of
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}
. So
Proposition

4.9
indeed gives our claim.
∎

4.E
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

In order to complete the proof of
Theorem

4.1
we now essentially have to check condition (v) of
Proposition

4.3
and that groups of type
\mathrm{D}_{4}
satisfy the condition (
iMK
) for all primes.

For the first point we keep the notation of
Notation

4.6
.

Lemma
\@upn
4.11

Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})^{uF}\unlhd{\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}})^{uF}
.

Proof.

Let us abbreviate
T={\mathbf{T}}^{uF}
,
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})^{uF}
,
\widetilde{T}=\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{uF}
,
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}})^{uF}=N\widetilde{T}
. Note that since
\widetilde{N}/N
is abelian, maximal extendibility can be proven by ensuring that the restriction of any irreducible character of
\widetilde{N}
to
N
is multiplicity-free (see for example
[
S23b
, 1.A]
).

Let us first recall the parameterization of
\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N})
. We know from
Proposition

4.8
(e) that there is an extension map
\Lambda
with respect to
T\unlhd N
. By
Proposition

2.3
, the map
\Lambda
allows us to construct an extension map
\widetilde{\Lambda}
with respect to
\widetilde{T}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
with the property that
\left.\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widetilde{}\lambda)\right\rceil_{N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}}=\left.\Lambda(\lambda)\right\rceil_{N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}}
for
\widetilde{}\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{T})
,
\lambda:=\left.\widetilde{}\lambda\right\rceil_{T}
and using
N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\leq N_{\lambda}
, see
Corollary

4.10
.
By Clifford theory (
2.1
), every
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N})
then writes as

\psi=(\widetilde{\Lambda}(\widetilde{}\lambda)\eta)^{\widetilde{N}}

for some
\widetilde{}\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{T})
and
\eta\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}/\widetilde{T})
. Since
\widetilde{N}=\widetilde{T}N
, we observe
\widetilde{N}=N\,\widetilde{N}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
and hence the Mackey formula shows

\left.\psi\right\rceil_{N}=(\left.(\widetilde{\Lambda}(\lambda)\eta)\right\rceil_{N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}})^{N}=(\Lambda(\lambda)\eta^{W(\lambda)})^{N},

where we set
W(\lambda):={N_{\lambda}}/T
, and see
\eta
as a character of
W(\widetilde{}\lambda):={N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}}/T
,
{\widetilde{N}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}}/\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{N}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
, respectively.
(This also uses the above definition of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
from
\Lambda
.)

By
Corollary

4.10
, maximal extendibility holds with respect to
W(\widetilde{}\lambda)\unlhd W(\lambda)
since the group
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}_{\lambda}^{uF}
considered there is an overgroup for
W(\lambda)
. But
W(\lambda)/W(\widetilde{}\lambda)=N_{\lambda}/N_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
is abelian (see the proof of
Proposition

4.3
), so maximal extendibility implies that
\eta^{W(\lambda)}
is multiplicity-free. But then by Clifford correspondence, the character
\left(\Lambda(\lambda)(\eta^{W(\lambda)\\
})\right)^{N}
is also multiplicity-free, whence our claim.
∎

We can now complete the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Set
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
(
l\geq 4
),
F:{{\mathbf{G}}}\longrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
d\geq 3
as in the assumptions. Notice first that (a) implies (b) thanks to
Theorem

2.21
since
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}/\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}})^{F}
is always a simple group and
[
Ma08
]
ensures that we can take
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
to play the rôle of its universal covering group. So we just verify (a) and (c).

We first check (a) by establishing

\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
through
Proposition

4.3
whose assumptions we now review. We let
V\leq{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{T}})
and
u\in V
as in
4.6
, so that
\rho(u)\varphi_{0}
is a
\zeta_{d}
-regular element of
W\varphi_{0}
thanks to
Proposition

4.8
(a). Then assumptions
4.3
(i) and (ii) are ensured by
Proposition

4.8
(a) and (b). Assumption
4.3
(v) holds according to
Lemma

4.11
since
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})={\mathbf{T}}
with
{\mathbf{S}}
the Sylow
d
-subtorus of
({\mathbf{T}},uF)
imply
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{T}})^{uF}
and
{\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{uF}={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}})^{uF}
.

Assume now
(l,{\epsilon})\neq(4,1)
, so that
E({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})={\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
. Then assumption
4.3
(iii) is ensured by
Proposition

4.8
(c) and (d). Assumption
4.3
(iv) amounts to a strengthening of the above
Corollary

4.10
where
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}
is replaced by the overgroup
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}=\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}\times\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
. The extendibility property is preserved since
F_{p}
is central.

We now concentrate on the case of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{4,{\mathrm{sc}}}(q)
to finish checking (a) and (c). By
Theorem

2.11
, we can content ourselves with taking a prime
\ell\nmid 6q
for (c). For (a), thanks to
Theorem

2.21
, we have to check
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
for
d=4
, the only integer doubly regular for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
\geq 3
.
The polynomial order of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
is

P_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(X)=X^{12}\mathbf{\Phi}_{1}^{4}\mathbf{\Phi}_{2}^{4}\mathbf{\Phi}_{3}\mathbf{\Phi}_{4}^{2}\mathbf{\Phi}_{6},

where
{{\mathbf{\Phi}_{i}}}
is the
i
-th cyclotomic polynomial,
see
[
C
, p. 75]
. For primes
\ell\nmid 2q
such that
d_{\ell}(q)=d\in\{3,6\}
we observe that
\ell>3
,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{d}
occurs with exponent 1 and
\mathbf{\Phi}_{d\ell^{a}}
is not present for
a>1
. Then
|{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}|_{\ell}=\mathbf{\Phi}_{d}(q)_{\ell}
, so a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is a subgroup of a Sylow
d
-torus of polynomial order
\mathbf{\Phi}_{d}
, hence cyclic (see for instance proof of
[
MT
, Prop. 25.7]
). For
\ell
-blocks of quasisimple groups with cyclic defect groups a so-called
inductive Alperin–McKay condition
holds according to
[
KS16
, Thm 1.1]
. This provides us with a stronger version of the required (
iMK
), see also 2nd paragraph of
[
CS19
, Sect. 6.C]
.

It remains to check
A
(4) and
B
(4). We resume reviewing the assumptions of
Proposition

4.3
, remembering that only
4.3
(iii) and
4.3
(iv) were left incomplete. Denote by
{{\gamma_{3}}}\in E({{\mathbf{G}}})
a graph automorphism of order 3.
Let
u\in V
,
V_{4}={\mathrm{C}}_{V}(u)\geq H_{4}
from
4.6
, and
\widehat{V}_{4}:={\mathrm{C}}_{V\rtimes{\left\langle\gamma,\gamma_{3},F_{p}\right\rangle}}(u)/\left\langle uF_{q}\right\rangle\geq\widecheck{V}_{4}:={\mathrm{C}}_{V\rtimes{\left\langle\gamma,F_{p}\right\rangle}}(u)/\left\langle uF_{q}\right\rangle\geq V_{4}
in
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
. Note that the centralizer of
\overline{\rho}(u)
in
\overline{W}
is a 2-group (use
Lemma

4.5
(b) to determine
\rho(u)
) while
H_{4}
is also a 2-group, hence
V_{4}
is also one.

What has been checked of assumption
4.3
(iii) by applying
Proposition

4.8
(c-d) ensures that the inclusion
H_{4}\unlhd\widecheck{V}_{4}
satisfies maximal extendibility. Now if
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(H_{4})
, then its extension
\widecheck{\lambda}
to
(\widecheck{V}_{4})_{\lambda}
can be chosen to have the image of
\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
(central) in its kernel, see the proof of
Proposition

4.8
(d). The quotient of
\widecheck{V}_{4}
by the image of
\left\langle F_{p}\right\rangle
is a 2-group, while
\widehat{V}_{4}/\widecheck{V}_{4}
has order 1 or 3, so
[
Is
, 6.28]
implies that
\widecheck{\lambda}
can be chosen to be
(\widehat{V}_{4})_{\lambda}
-invariant. It then further extends to
(\widehat{V}_{4})_{\lambda}
by
Proposition

2.2
(a). So we get maximal extendibility for
H_{4}\unlhd\widehat{V}_{4}{}
hence our claim since the associated extension map can always be chosen to be
\widehat{V}_{4}
-equivariant as recalled in
Notation

2.1
.

For assumption
4.3
(iv), by the argument used before when
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})={\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
it suffices to check maximal extendibility for
{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\unlhd\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}
where
\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}\left\langle\gamma,\gamma_{3}\right\rangle}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}\unrhd\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbf{T}}
. We have maximal extendibility for
{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\unlhd\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}
thanks to
Corollary

4.10
. But since
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}^{uF}
is a
2
-group, we get that
\overline{{{\mathbf{W}}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}/{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
is a Sylow 2-subgroup of
\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}/{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
while every Sylow 3-subgroup of
\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}/{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}
is cyclic of order 1 or 3. We then get the sought maximal extendibility for
{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\widetilde{}\lambda}\unlhd\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}^{uF}_{\lambda}
by applying
[
Is
, Cor. 11.31]
and
Proposition

2.2
(a).
∎

4.F
Extending Malle’s bijection

For later applications, we construct a character correspondence extending the one given by (
iMK
) only assuming Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
.

Proving (
iMK
) for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and a prime
\ell
not dividing
2q
with the choice
N={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
for
{\mathbf{S}}
a Sylow
d_{\ell}(q)
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
gives us a
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Aut}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\mathbf{S}}
-equivariant bijection

\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}:\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}),

which satisfies for every
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
the relation

({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}(\chi)},N,\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}(\chi))

or any variant obtained by applying the Butterfly
Theorem

2.8
.

In the setting of
Theorem

4.1
(b) we get the following.

Corollary
\@upn
4.12

Assume that
\ell
is a prime with
\ell\nmid 2q
such that
d:=d_{\ell}(q)
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and let
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. Set
T:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}\unlhd N:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{F}
.

(a)

There exists some
({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\mathbf{S}}
-equivariant extension map
\Lambda
with respect to
T\unlhd N
. Furthermore, for every
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(T)
, the character
\Lambda(\lambda)
extends to
({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{{\mathbf{S}},\lambda}
.

(b)

For
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(T)
with
\ell\nmid|N/N_{\lambda}|
, the character
\chi:=\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}^{-1}(\Lambda(\lambda)^{N})
belongs to
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, i.e.
({\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))_{\chi}={{\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}_{\chi}{E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}_{\chi}
and
\chi
extends to
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\chi}
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from
Proposition

4.8
(e) in the cases where
E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})={\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
. When
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}_{4,{\mathrm{sc}}}(q)
, then
d=4
and we have seen in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
(a) above that maximal extendibility holds for
H_{4}\unlhd\widehat{V}_{4}
. As explained in the proof of
Proposition

4.3
, this implies our claim by
Proposition

2.2
(e).
For (b), set
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(T)
,
\chi^{\prime}:=\Lambda(\lambda)^{N}
. We also abbreviate
G={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\unlhd{\widetilde{G}}={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
,
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{G}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{GE(G)}({\mathbf{S}})
. In the notation of the proof of
Proposition

4.3
, we have
\chi^{\prime}=\Pi(\xi,\eta)
for
(\xi,\eta)=(\lambda,1)
. The fact that
\eta=1
implies that this proof can be followed with both
\eta_{0}
,
\widetilde{\eta}_{0}
, and
\eta^{\prime}
being trivial and therefore the choice
\chi_{0}=\Pi(\lambda,1)
. This gives

({\widetilde{N}}{}{\widehat{N}}{})_{\chi^{\prime}}={{\widetilde{N}}{}}_{\chi^{\prime}}{{\widehat{N}}{}}_{\chi^{\prime}}

with
\chi^{\prime}
extending to
\widehat{N}_{\chi^{\prime}}
.

As
\Omega_{G,\ell}
is
\widetilde{N}\widehat{N}
-equivariant,
\chi:=\Omega_{G,\ell}^{-1}(\chi^{\prime})
satisfies

\displaystyle(\widetilde{G}E(G))_{\chi}

\displaystyle=G(\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi}=G(\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=G(\widetilde{N}_{\chi^{\prime}}\widehat{N}_{\chi^{\prime}})=G(\widetilde{N}_{\chi}\widehat{N}_{\chi})=(G\widetilde{N}_{\chi})\,(G\widehat{N}_{\chi})=\widetilde{G}_{\chi}E(G)_{\chi}.

As recalled above the bijection
\Omega_{G,\ell}
satisfies some
\geq_{c}
-relation that we can take to be

((\widetilde{G}E(G))_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}((\widetilde{N}\widehat{N})_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime}).

According to
Lemma

2.6
(c) this implies

(GE(G)_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}(\widehat{N}_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime}).

Now,
Lemma

2.6
(b) and the fact that
\chi^{\prime}
extends to
\widehat{N}_{\chi^{\prime}}
allow us to see that
\chi
extends to
GE(G)_{\chi}
.
∎

The groups that appear above depend on the integer
d
but not on the prime
\ell
leading to
d
. The condition (
iMK
) for two primes
\ell
and
r
with the same value
d
give two bijections, all mapping to some characters of
N={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}})
. Going back to the construction of
\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}
through
[
Ma07
]
and
[
CS17a
, Sect. 6]
, we associate to a fixed
d
a character set
\mathcal{G}_{d}\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
and a bijection

\Omega^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}_{d}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(N),

such that

(G\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\Omega^{\prime}(\chi)},N,\Omega^{\prime}(\chi))\text{ for every }\chi\in\mathcal{G}_{d}

and for every prime
\ell
with
d_{\ell}(q)=d
, the map
\Omega^{\prime}
restricts to
\Omega_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}},\ell}
on
\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)
.

For the construction of this map we assume
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
to be as in
Section

2.C
, i.e.,
{{\mathbf{G}}}
might be of type different from
\mathrm{D}
.

Definition
\@upn
4.13

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
be as in
Section

2.C
with a regular embedding
{{\mathbf{G}}}\leq{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
. Assume we have dual groups
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}\twoheadrightarrow{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}
with Frobenius endomorphisms denoted by the same letter
F
. Let
d\geq 1
and let
{\mathbf{S}}^{*}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*},F)
.

For any
\widetilde{s}\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}_{\text{ss}}{}^{F}
, let
\mathcal{E}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}},[\widetilde{s}])
be the associated rational series of characters of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
. Recall
\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})
the set of unipotent characters and the Jordan decomposition map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Uch}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\mathcal{E}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}},[\widetilde{s}]),

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi_{\widetilde{s},\lambda}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}.

For
{{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}
an
F
-stable connected reductive subgroup of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}
containing
{\mathbf{S}}^{*}
, let
\operatorname{Uch}_{d}({{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}{}^{F})
be the set of irreducible components of Lusztig’s generalized characters
\operatorname{R}^{{{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}{}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}}({\mathbf{S}}^{*})}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\operatorname{Uch}({{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}}({\mathbf{S}}^{*})^{F}})
.

Set

{{{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{d}}}=\bigcup_{\widetilde{s}\in{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}({\mathbf{S}}^{*})^{F}_{\text{ss}}}\{\chi_{\widetilde{s},\lambda}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\mid\lambda\in\operatorname{Uch}_{d}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})\}

and let

{{{\mathcal{G}}_{d}}}:=\bigcup_{\widetilde{\chi}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{d}}\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\widetilde{\chi}\right\rceil_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}})\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})

be the set of irreducible components of the restrictions
\left.\widetilde{\chi}\right\rceil_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}}
for
\widetilde{\chi}\in\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}
.

The following proposition is an adaptation of the construction recalled in
[
CS17a
, Sect. 6]
, assuming Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
. We give a proof in the case when
d
is regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
which simplifies a bit the notation. We later apply it only for
d
a doubly-regular number for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}=\mathrm{D}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbb{F}})
, where the assumptions are ensured by
Theorem

4.1
(a).

Proposition
\@upn
4.14

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
be as in
2.C
and let
d\geq 1
. Let
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
,
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}})
,
\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\rtimes E({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}({\mathbf{S}})
and
{\widetilde{N}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}})
.

(a)

Assume Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
. Then there exists some
\mathrm{Lin}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}/{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})\rtimes\widehat{N}
-equivariant bijective map

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}:\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{N}}),

such that

(a.1)

\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\chi\right\rceil_{\operatorname{Z}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}})})=\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}(\chi)\right\rceil_{\operatorname{Z}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}})}){\text{\quad\quad for every }}\chi\in\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}
, and

(a.2)

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}(\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}\mid\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})))=\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{N}}\mid\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N))
for every prime
\ell
with
d=d_{\ell}(q)
.

(b)

Assume Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
. Set
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Aut}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})_{\mathbf{S}}
. Then there exists a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijective map

\Omega^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}_{d}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(N)

such that

(G\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},G,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\Omega^{\prime}(\chi)},N,\Omega^{\prime}(\chi))\text{ for every $\chi\in\mathcal{G}_{d}$}

and
\Omega^{\prime}(\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(G))=\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)
for every odd prime
\ell
with
d_{\ell}(q)=d
.

Proof.

The proof essentially follows
[
CS17a
, Sect. 6]
.
We give a detailed proof for the case where
d
is regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, allowing us to parameterise both characters via a set of pairs
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. This is the only case used in the paper, namely for
{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
, etc. as defined in
Section

2.E
with
d
doubly regular. A simplification comes from the fact that centralizers of Sylow
d
-tori are tori in both
{{\mathbf{G}}}
,
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
and their dual groups. A more general case would require to deal with triples instead of pairs as parameters.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}_{d}={\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}={\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}({\mathbf{S}}^{*})
for
{\mathbf{S}}^{*}
a Sylow
d
-torus in
\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}
. Both are
F
-stable tori with same type the regular element
\rho(u)\in W
with regard to the maximally split tori in duality
{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}^{*}
. So
{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}^{*}_{d}
can be taken as effecting the duality between
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}
.

Let
{{{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}}}
be the set of pairs
(\widetilde{s},\eta)
where
\widetilde{s}\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F}
and
\eta\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})}({\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*})^{F}/{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F})
.

The so-called generalized
d
-Harish-Chandra theory, see
[
GM
, Sect. 4.6]
, implies that there is a bijection

\displaystyle\operatorname{Uch}\colon\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})}({\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*})^{F}/{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Uch}_{d}({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})^{F})

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Uch}(\eta)

defined as
\eta\mapsto\pm I_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*},1}^{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})}(\eta)
in the notation of
[
GM
, Thm 4.6.21]
.

One then sets

\Psi^{(G)}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathrm{Irr}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})\ \text{ by }(\widetilde{s},\eta)\mapsto\chi^{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}_{\widetilde{s},\operatorname{Uch}(\eta)}.

Note that
\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}
is defined to be its image, taking into account that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}({\mathbf{S}}^{*})={\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}
is a torus and therefore
\operatorname{Uch}({\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F})=\{1_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F}}\}
.

On the other hand Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
implies there exists some
\mathrm{Lin}({\widetilde{G}}/G)\rtimes\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map
\widetilde{\Lambda}
with respect to
\widetilde{C}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
. Let us recall the isomorphism

\widetilde{N}_{\chi_{\widetilde{s},1}^{{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}}}/{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{F}\cong{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})}({\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*})^{F}/{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F},

given by a well-defined duality map
i_{\widetilde{s},1}
, see the proof of
[
CS13
, Cor. 3.3]
. For
\eta\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{*}}(\widetilde{s})}({\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*})^{F}/{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}^{*}{}^{F})
we denote by
\eta^{*}=\eta\circ i_{\widetilde{s},1}
the corresponding character of
\widetilde{N}_{\chi_{\widetilde{s},1}^{{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}}}
. We obtain a surjective map

\Psi^{(N)}:\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N})\text{ given by }(\widetilde{s},\eta)\mapsto(\widetilde{\Lambda}(\chi_{\widetilde{s},1}^{{\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}}_{d}})\eta^{*})^{\widetilde{N}},

which makes sense by Clifford theory (
2.1
). By the considerations made in the proof of
[
CS17a
, Thm 6.1]
which only use that the characters of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
involved are in
\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}
, the maps
\Psi^{(G)}
and
\Psi^{(N)}
are constant on
\widetilde{N}^{*}
-orbits, and they are bijections once seen as on the quotient sets. Then one gets a bijection

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}\to\mathrm{Irr}(\widetilde{N})\text{ with }\Psi^{(G)}(\widetilde{s},\eta)\mapsto\Psi^{(N)}(\widetilde{s},\eta)

and the properties announced thanks to
[
CS17a
, Prop. 6.3]
and
[
CS17a
, Thm 6.1]
whose main arguments are independent of the prime
\ell
.

The above follows the constructions of Section 6 of
[
CS17a
]
and uses the fact that
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}({\mathbf{S}})
is a torus to simplify the technical construction of
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}
as otherwise the parameters used are triples. If
d
is not regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, the construction of
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\prime}
can be deduced from Section 6 of
[
CS17a
]
in a similar manner by omitting the assumptions involving
\ell
. Note that in both cases, as soon as
\ell
is an odd prime with
d=d_{\ell}(q)
, we also have
\mathrm{Irr}({{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}\mid\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}))\subseteq\widetilde{}\mathcal{G}_{d}
and
