, see (
5.44
). This implies (2). Then
2\mid f
according to
Proposition

5.25
(e) and hence
Hypothesis

5.15
does not hold. This leads to
n^{2}=h_{0}
and provides the
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{379200}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle T\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{379200}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle T\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{304069}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle T\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{263696}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle T\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
-equivariant isomorphism

M_{0}\left\langle n\right\rangle/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle\cong(G_{1}.G_{2})\left\langle\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\right\rangle/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle.

Note that
\phi_{2}
extends to

G_{2}\left\langle\widehat{t}_{2},\gamma_{2}\right\rangle
according to
Proposition

5.25
(b) and hence every extension of
\phi_{2}
to
G_{2}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma_{2}\right\rangle
is
\widehat{t}_{2}
-invariant. An extension of
\phi_{1}
to
G_{1}\left\langle\gamma_{1}\right\rangle
and an extension of
\phi_{2}
define an extension
\widetilde{\phi}
of
\phi
to
M_{0}\left\langle n\right\rangle
. Because of
[G_{1}\left\langle\gamma_{1}\right\rangle,\widehat{t}_{2}]=1
,
\widetilde{\phi}
is
\widehat{t}_{2}
-invariant. We observe that
\widetilde{\phi}
can be taken as
\chi_{0}
and
\chi=\widetilde{\phi}^{M}
. By construction,
\chi
is
\widehat{t}_{2}
-invariant. Since
\widehat{t}_{1}\widehat{t}_{2}\in M
by
Lemma

5.6
this leads to
\chi^{\widehat{t}_{1}}=\chi
, as required in (1). As above, we derive from
\phi_{1}\in\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}
that (4) holds.

If
\phi_{1}.\phi_{2}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{1}.\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{2}
, then the same argument applies and ensures (1), (2) and (4).

Finally, we prove (3), i.e.
2\mid[{\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{j})_{\phi_{j}}:E^{\prime}_{j}]
whenever
j\in\{1,2\}
with
\phi_{j}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
. We can assume
\widehat{M}/M
to be non-cyclic and
\phi_{1}.\phi_{2}\in({\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}_{1}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2})\sqcup({\mathbb{E}}_{1}.\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{2})\sqcup(\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{1}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2})
. This implies
{\epsilon}_{1}={\epsilon}_{2}=1
.
Fix
j\in\{1,2\}
with
\phi_{j}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
and let
j^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}\setminus\{j\}
. In our situation we observe
\phi_{j^{\prime}}\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{j^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{j^{\prime}}^{n}=\phi_{j^{\prime}}
according to
Proposition

5.25
.

Writing
\underline{F}_{p}\in{\underline{E}}(M)
for the image of
F_{p}
, we let
a_{i}
be a divisor of the order of
\underline{F}_{p}
such that
\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{i}}
generates
\left\langle\underline{F}_{p}\right\rangle_{\phi_{i}}
.
By definition
{\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{j})_{\phi_{j}}=\left\langle(\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j}}),\gamma_{j}\right\rangle
,
and every extension of
\phi_{j}
to
G_{j}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma_{j}\right\rangle
is not
\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j}}
-invariant because of
\phi_{j}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
.
Analogously

{\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{j^{\prime}})_{\phi_{j^{\prime}}}=\left\langle(\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j^{\prime}}}),\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\right\rangle
and the extension of
\phi_{j^{\prime}}
to
G_{j^{\prime}}\rtimes\left\langle\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\right\rangle
is
\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j^{\prime}}}
-invariant as
\phi_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{T}_{j^{\prime}}
.

Because of
{\epsilon}_{1}={\epsilon}_{2}=1
we have
n=n_{1}^{\circ}n_{2}^{\circ}
and there exists an isomorphism between
(G_{1}\left\langle\gamma_{1}\right\rangle)/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle\times(G_{2}\left\langle\gamma_{2}\right\rangle)/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle G_{1},G_{2},n,\gamma_{1}\right\rangle/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
.
The extensions
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{2}
define an extension
\widetilde{\phi}
of
\phi
to
M_{0}\left\langle n\right\rangle
with

{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})_{\widetilde{\phi}}=\left\langle n,\gamma\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\underline{F}_{p}^{2a_{j}}\right\rangle\cap\left\langle\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\right).

Recall that
E^{\prime}_{j}
denotes the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(G_{j})
induced by
{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})_{\chi_{0}}
, where
\chi_{0}
is an extension of
\phi
to
M_{\phi}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\chi_{0}
is an extension of
\widetilde{\phi}
and hence
{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})_{\chi_{0}}\leq{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})_{\widetilde{\phi}}
.
We then see that
E^{\prime}_{j}
has an even index in
{\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{j})_{\phi_{j}}=\left\langle\gamma_{j},\underline{F}_{p}^{a_{j}}\right\rangle
as required for (3).
∎

The above pretty much
finishes the verification of
Theorem

5.20
:

Proof of
Theorem

5.20
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an
\widetilde{M}
-orbit in
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
,
\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
and
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\chi^{\prime}\right\rceil_{M_{0}})
. According to
Proposition

5.26
there is some
t\in\widetilde{T}
such that
\phi:=(\phi^{\prime})^{t}
is contained in

\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{1}.\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{2}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.\mathbb{D}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}_{1}.\mathbb{D}_{2}^{\prime}.

The case
\phi\in{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.\mathbb{D}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}_{1}.\mathbb{D}_{2}^{\prime}
is only relevant if additionally
\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}=\{4\}
. Set
\chi:=(\chi^{\prime})^{t}
and hence
\chi\in\mathcal{O}
. By the choice of
\phi
we see that

\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{1}.\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{2})\,\cup\,\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid{\mathbb{E}}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.\mathbb{D}_{2}\sqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\prime}.{\mathbb{E}}_{2}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}}_{1}.\mathbb{D}_{2}^{\prime}).

Characters of these sets have been studied in
Proposition

5.30
and
Proposition

5.31
, respectively. Consequently

(\widetilde{M}\widehat{M})_{\chi}={\widetilde{M}}_{\chi}{\widehat{M}}_{\chi}
, and
\chi
extends to
M{\underline{E}}(M)_{\chi}
. This gives part (a) of
Theorem

5.20
.

Assume next
{\epsilon}_{1}={\epsilon}_{2}=-1
and
2\mid f
. According to
2.24
we see that
\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}=\{2\}
and therefore
\mathrm{Irr}(M_{0})=\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{1}.\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{2}
. Then
\mathrm{Irr}(M)=\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{1}.\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{2})
and we get part (b) of
Theorem

5.20
from
Proposition

5.30
.
∎

6
The Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)

Now we use the results on the character theory of the group
M
from the previous section to establish Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
2.19
and
2.20
for an integer
d\geq 3
fixed throughout the whole chapter. Condition
\mathbf{A}(d)
is about the character theory of
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F}
, where
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
.
Lemma

5.8
shows that outside of the “doubly
regular” case treated in Chapter
4
, we can essentially replace
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F}
by a group
M
as in Chapter
5
: In the setting of
Section

5.A

a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})
can be taken as a subgroup of either
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{1}
or
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2}
, and
d
is doubly regular there. Character correspondences between a subset of
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
and
\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F})
allow us to transfer statements on
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
to
\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F})
via centrally isomorphic character triples. These are constructed using results from the doubly regular case, where (
iMK
) was already shown, see
Theorem

4.1
.
As a second step in
Section

6.B
, we use character correspondences to define an extension map, later verifying Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
.

Proving
Theorem

6.9
finishes the proof of
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
. From this we derive (
iMK
) for quasisimple groups of type
\mathrm{D}_{l}
, the final step of the proof of Theorem B and indeed McKay’s equality.

6.A
Character correspondences

In the following, we continue using the notation introduced in Section
5
around the group
M
.
We establish a character correspondence between some characters of
M
and one of its subgroups under Assumption
6.1
that essentially sums up the case (iii) of
Lemma

5.8
.
This leads us to deduce some results about
\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})^{F})
for some Sylow
d
-torus
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
. In order to apply the results of
Section

4
we assume the following.

Assumption 6.1
.

Let
d
be an integer with
d\geq 3
. Assume that
d
is doubly regular (see
Definition

4.4
) for
({{\mathbf{G}}}_{j},{{\nu F_{q}}})
for some
j\in\{1,2\}
with
l_{j}\geq 4
.

Notation
\@upn
6.2

Let
{j}\in\{1,2\}
be such that
d
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}}_{j},{{\nu F_{q}}})
. Set
{{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}:={{\mathbf{G}}}_{j}}}
,
{{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}:={{\mathbf{G}}}_{3-j}}}
,
{\epsilon}^{\prime}_{1}:={\epsilon}_{j}={\epsilon}{\epsilon}^{\prime}_{2}
,
l^{\prime}_{1}:=l_{j}=l-l^{\prime}_{2}
,
K_{i}={{\mathbf{K}}}_{i}^{{\nu F_{q}}}
,
{{{\underline{E}}_{M}(K_{1})}}:={\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{j})
,
{{{\underline{E}}_{M}(K_{2})}}:={\underline{E}}_{M}(G_{3-j})
(see
Lemma

5.14
and
Lemma

5.19
) and let
{{{\mathbf{S}}}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
. We associate with
{\mathbf{S}}
the groups

{N_{1}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}}),\,{C:={\mathrm{C}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})}\unlhd{N:={\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})},\,{{\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M{\underline{E}}(M)}({\mathbf{S}})}}{\text{ and }}{{\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M\widetilde{T}}({\mathbf{S}})}},

where
\widetilde{T}:=({\mathbf{T}}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}))^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}
.

Assumption

6.1
implies that (
iMK
) holds for
K_{1}
and
\ell
with respect to
N_{1}
, whenever
\ell
is an odd prime with
d=d_{\ell}(q)
, see
Theorem

4.1
. Recall that
\mathrm{Aut}(K_{1})
is induced by bijective endomorphisms of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
commuting with
F
, see
Section

2.C
, and hence
\mathrm{Aut}(K_{1})
acts on the set of
F
-stable subgroups of
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}
. The group
{{\Gamma_{1}}}:=\mathrm{Aut}(K_{1})_{\mathbf{S}}
is therefore well-defined and according to
Theorem

4.1
(b) there exists a
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant bijection

\Omega_{1}^{\circ}:\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(K_{1})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N_{1}),

such that

(K_{1}\rtimes\Gamma_{1,\psi},K_{1},\psi)\geq_{c}(N_{1}\rtimes\Gamma_{1,\Omega_{1}^{\circ}(\psi)},N_{1},\Omega_{1}^{\circ}(\psi)){\text{\quad\quad for every }}\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(K_{1}).

Since
K_{1}={{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}\cong\mathrm{D}_{l^{\prime}_{1},{\mathrm{sc}}}^{{\epsilon}^{\prime}_{1}}(q)
according to
Definition

5.4
, and the integer
d=d_{\ell}(q)
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
,
Proposition

4.14
defines a character set
\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(K_{1})
and provides us with a character correspondence extending
\Omega_{1}^{\circ}
.

Recall
K_{1}\unlhd M
by
Lemma

5.6
(d). In the following, we continue to use the group
{\underline{E}}(M)
from
Lemma

5.6
(d), which acts by definition also on
\widetilde{M}:=\widetilde{T}M
. The following gives a character correspondence between some characters of
K_{1}
and characters of
{N_{1}={\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})}
, which is additionally
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant. Recall that here we use the order relation on character triples from
Section

2.B
.

Proposition
\@upn
6.3

We keep
Assumption

6.1
and follow
Notation

6.2
.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(K_{1})
be the set from
Definition

4.13
. There exists some
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant bijection

\Omega_{1}:\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Irr}{(N_{1})},

such that

(K_{1}\rtimes\Gamma_{1,\chi_{1}},K_{1},\chi_{1})\geq_{c}(N_{1}\rtimes\Gamma_{1,\Omega_{1}(\chi_{1})},N_{1},\Omega_{1}(\chi_{1})){\text{\quad\quad for every }}\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}).

(b)

Set
{A:=\widetilde{M}{\underline{E}}(M)}
. Then every
\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})
satisfies

(A_{\chi_{1}},K_{1},\chi_{1})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{A}({\mathbf{S}})_{\chi_{1}},N_{1},\Omega_{1}(\chi_{1})){\text{\quad\quad for every }}\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}).

(c)

Let
J
be a group with
K_{1}\leq J\unlhd A=\widetilde{M}{\underline{E}}(M)
. Then there exists an
{\mathrm{N}}_{A}({\mathbf{S}})
-equivariant bijection

\Pi:\mathrm{Irr}(J\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{J}({\mathbf{S}}))

such that every
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(J\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))
satisfies

(A_{\psi},J,\psi)\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{A}({\mathbf{S}})_{\Pi(\psi)},{\mathrm{N}}_{J}({\mathbf{S}}),\Pi(\psi)).

In particular

(i)

If
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(J\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))
and
\psi^{\prime}:=\Pi(\psi)
, then
A_{\psi}=J{\mathrm{N}}_{A}({\mathbf{S}})_{\psi^{\prime}}
. For any
J\leq U\leq A
, the character
\psi
extends to
U
if and only if
\psi^{\prime}
extends to
{\mathrm{N}}_{U}({\mathbf{S}})
.

(ii)

Let
\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{C}}_{J}(K_{1}))
such that
\phi_{1}.\phi_{2}
is a well-defined irreducible character of
K_{1}.{\mathrm{C}}_{J}(K_{1})
. Then

\displaystyle\Pi(\mathrm{Irr}(J\mid\phi_{1}.\phi_{2}))=\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{J}({\mathbf{S}})\mid\Omega_{1}(\phi_{1}).\phi_{2}).

(6.1)

Proof.

According to
Proposition

4.14
, the bijection
\Omega_{1}
exists as required in (a). Recall
K_{1}\unlhd\widetilde{M}{\underline{E}}(M)
by
Lemma

5.7
(c). According to the Butterfly Theorem
2.8
, part (a) implies (b), see also
Remark

2.9
. In Part (c), the existence of a bijection
\Pi
satisfying the
\geq_{c}
relation and (ii) is a consequence of (a) and (b) thanks to
[
Ro23a
, Prop. 2.4]
and the construction described there. Property (i) follows from
Lemma

2.6
(b).
∎

We continue using
Assumption

6.1
and the notation above. We deduce from
\Omega_{1}
and its properties the following bijection. The next statement ensures Condition
\mathbf{A}(d)
later, via an isomorphism between
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}
. Recall
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})
,
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M{\underline{E}}(M)}({\mathbf{S}})
.

Theorem
\@upn
6.4

We keep
Assumption

6.1
.
There exists some
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal
\mathbb{T}(N)
in
\mathrm{Irr}(N)
such that maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N\unlhd\widehat{N}
for
\mathbb{T}(N)
. Additionally if
2\mid f
,
{\epsilon}=1
and
{\epsilon}_{1}=-1
, then every
\chi\in\mathbb{T}(N)
with
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
has an extension
\widetilde{\chi}
to
\widehat{N}_{\chi}
with
vF_{q}\in\ker(\widetilde{\chi})
.

Proof.

Recall
\widehat{M}:=M{\underline{E}}(M)
and
\widetilde{M}:=M\widetilde{T}
. According to
Theorem

5.20
(a) there exists some
\widehat{M}
-stable
\widetilde{M}
-transversal
\mathbb{T}(M)
in
\mathrm{Irr}(M)
, such that every
\chi\in\mathbb{T}(M)
extends to
\widehat{M}_{\chi}
. On the other hand
\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})
is by definition
\mathrm{Aut}(K_{1})
-stable. Set
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(M):=\mathbb{T}(M)\cap\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))
.
Since
{\underline{E}}(M)
and
\widetilde{T}
permute the Sylow
d
-tori of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
and all Sylow
d
-tori of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
are
K_{1}
-conjugate (see
[
MT
, Thm 25.11]
) we see
M{\underline{E}}(M)=M\widehat{N}
and
\widetilde{M}=M\widetilde{N}
. Therefore,
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(M)
is also a
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))
.

For
J:=M
and
A:=\widehat{M}\widetilde{M}
, we apply Proposition
6.3(c)
and obtain the bijection

\Pi:\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}({\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}}))

with the properties stated there. Then
\Pi
is
\widehat{N}\widetilde{N}
-equivariant as
{\mathrm{N}}_{A}({\mathbf{S}})=\widehat{N}\widetilde{N}
. Note that the set
\mathbb{T}(N):=\Pi(\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(M))
is a
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(N)
by the equivariance of
\Pi
. Let
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}(N)
and set
\psi:=\Pi^{-1}(\psi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(M)
. By the properties of
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}(M)
, we see that
\psi
extends to its stabiliser in
\widehat{M}
. Furthermore by the properties of
\Pi
we have

(M{\underline{E}}(M)_{\psi},M,\psi)\geq_{c}(\widehat{N}_{\psi^{\prime}},N,\psi^{\prime}).

According to
Lemma

2.6
(b),
\psi^{\prime}
extends to
\widehat{N}_{\psi^{\prime}}
, since
\psi
extends to
\widehat{M}_{\psi}
.

Note that in the case of
2\mid f
,
{\epsilon}=1
and
{\epsilon}_{1}=-1
, every
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}(N)
with
h_{0}\in\ker(\psi^{\prime})
has an extension
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}
to
\widehat{N}_{\psi^{\prime}}
with
vF_{q}\in\ker(\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime})
, as
\psi
has the analogous property by
Theorem

5.20
(b).
∎

The following helps to ensure the first part of Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
2.20
.

Corollary
\@upn
6.5

Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N\unlhd\widetilde{N}
.

Proof.

As in the above proof of
Theorem

6.4
we apply Proposition
6.3(c)
with
J:=M
and use the bijection
\Pi:\mathrm{Irr}(M\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Irr}(N)
with

((\widetilde{M}{\underline{E}}(M))_{\psi},M,\psi)\geq_{c}((\widehat{N}\widetilde{N})_{\psi^{\prime}},N,\psi^{\prime})

for any
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(N)
and
\psi:=\Pi^{-1}(\psi^{\prime})
.

By
Lemma

2.6
(c) this restricts to

(\widetilde{M}_{\psi},M,\psi)\geq_{c}(\widetilde{N}_{\psi^{\prime}},N,\psi^{\prime}).

Recall that according to
Proposition

5.28
,
\psi
extends to
\widetilde{M}_{\psi}
, since maximal extendibility holds with respect to
M\unlhd\widetilde{M}
. According to
Lemma

2.6
(b) this implies that
\psi^{\prime}
extends to
\widetilde{N}_{\psi^{\prime}}
.
∎

6.B
Another extension map

The aim of this section is to build a version of the extension map required in the second part of Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
, see
2.20
. As before, we only verify an analogue for subgroups of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}
. Recall
M^{\circ}:=({{\mathbf{G}}}_{1}.{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2})^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}
and
{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ}):={\underline{E}}(M)\left\langle n,h_{0}\right\rangle
. We write
{{{\overline{\mathbb{T}}}(K_{i})}}
for the set from
Definition

5.24
with our choice of
K_{1}=G_{j}
and
K_{2}=G_{3-j}
. We choose
{{\mathbb{T}(K_{2})}}
to be an
{\underline{E}}_{M}(K_{2})
-stable subset of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}(K_{2})
, which is at the same time a
\widetilde{T}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(K_{2})
. (This is possible according to
Proposition

5.25
(a).) In the diagrams below double bars stand for normal inclusions of subgroups.

\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{M^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{0}\underline{E}(M^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{0}=K_{1}.K_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

In the groups
\widehat{M}
,
M_{0}{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})
,
M_{0}
and
M^{\circ}
, introduced before we consider their normalizers of
{\mathbf{S}}
. Note that there is a well-defined action of
{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})
on the
{{\nu F_{q}}}
-stable tori of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and hence the stabilizer of
{\mathbf{S}}
in those groups is well-defined.

Set
{{N^{\circ}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})}}
and
{{\widehat{N}^{\circ}}}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M_{0}{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})}({\mathbf{S}})
. Note that
N^{\circ}\cap\widehat{N}^{\circ}={\mathrm{N}}_{M_{0}}({\mathbf{S}})
since
M^{\circ}\cap M_{0}{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})=M_{0}
by
Lemma

5.13
(a) and
Lemma

5.18
(a).

By its definition
\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
is also
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
-stable and is a
{\widetilde{T}}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(K_{2})
.
Recall the description of the action of
M_{0}{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})
and, therefore, of
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
on
K_{1}
in
Lemma

5.14
and
Lemma

5.19
, respectively.
This allows us to transfer the main result from
Section

4.F
and we obtain an
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
-equivariant extension map
\Lambda_{1}
for
{\mathrm{C}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})\unlhd{\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})
.

\textstyle{\widehat{N}}
\textstyle{N^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{N}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}.K_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Lemma
\@upn
6.6

We follow
Notation

6.2
, keeping
Assumption

6.1
. Recall
{N_{1}={\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})}
and
C={\mathrm{C}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})\unlhd N^{\circ}
.

Let

{C_{1}:={\mathrm{C}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})}
and let
{{\Lambda_{1}}}
be the
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
-equivariant extension map with respect to
C_{1}\unlhd N_{1}
from
Corollary

4.12
(a).
Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(C_{1})
,
\phi_{2}\in\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
with
\lambda(h_{0})\phi_{2}(1)=\lambda(1)\phi_{2}(h_{0})
, so that
\lambda.\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(C_{1}.K_{2})
and
\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}}.\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(N_{1}.K_{2})
are well defined.

Then any
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(N^{\circ}\mid\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}}.\phi_{2})
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}
. Moreover,
\widehat{N}_{\psi}=N^{\circ}\widehat{N}_{\tau}
for
\{\tau\}=\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\lambda.\phi_{2})\cap\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\psi\right\rceil_{C})
.

Proof.

Let

\Omega_{1}:\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Irr}{(N_{1})},

and

\Pi:\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\circ}\mid\mathcal{G}_{d}(K_{1}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Irr}(N^{\circ})

be the bijections from Proposition
6.3(c)
obtained by choosing
M^{\circ}
as
J
and
\widetilde{M}{\underline{E}}(M)
as
A
.

Let
\psi\in\mathrm{Irr}(N^{\circ}\mid\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}}.\phi_{2})
and set
\chi:=\Pi^{-1}(\psi)
.
By definition
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\circ}\mid\Omega_{1}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}}).\phi_{2})
thanks to (
6.1
) in
Proposition

6.3
.
By
Corollary

4.12
(b), the character
\Omega_{1}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}})
of
K_{1}
satisfies

\Omega_{1}^{-1}(\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}})\in\overline{\mathbb{T}}(K_{1}).

\textstyle{\widehat{M}}
\textstyle{\chi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}.K_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)^{N_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{1}.K_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\lambda.\phi_{2}}
\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\lambda}

We now get
\chi\in\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\circ}\mid\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{1}.\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{2})
in the notation of
Definition

5.24
and for this character, Proposition
5.30(b)
tells us that
\chi
extends to
\widehat{M}_{\chi}
. By
Proposition

6.3
(c.i) we see that then also
\psi
extends to
\widehat{N}_{\psi}
.

We now have to compute
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. Clifford theory implies that
\widehat{N}_{\psi}\leq\widehat{N}_{\left.\psi\right\rceil_{C}}=N\widehat{N}_{\tau}
and hence it remains to ensure
\widehat{N}_{\psi}\geq\widehat{N}_{\tau}
.
Note first that
C_{1}.K_{2}
has index
2
or
1
in
C
. By Clifford theory, then
\tau
is either an extension of
\lambda.\phi_{2}
or equals
(\lambda.\phi_{2})^{C}
.

Assume that
\tau
is an extension of
\lambda.\phi_{2}
to
C
. Then there exists a common extension
\widetilde{\tau}
of
\tau
and
\left.\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)\right\rceil_{(N_{1})_{\tau}}.\phi_{2}
to
{(N_{1})_{\tau}}C
according to
Proposition

2.2
(e).
As
\Lambda_{1}
is
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
-equivariant, the character
\left.\Lambda_{1}(\lambda)\right\rceil_{(N_{1})_{\tau}}.\phi_{2}
is
(\widehat{N}^{\circ})_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}
-invariant. Then the character
\widetilde{\tau}
is
C\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\tau}=\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant, since
\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}C=\widehat{N}_{\left.\tau\right\rceil_{C_{1}.K_{2}}}\geq\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. Note that
\psi=\widetilde{\tau}^{N^{\circ}}
and hence
\psi
is
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant.

Now assume that
\tau=(\lambda.\phi_{2})^{C}
. Then
\widetilde{\tau}:=(\Lambda_{1}(\lambda).\phi_{2})^{C{(N_{1})_{\tau}}}
is irreducible and an extension of
\tau
. We observe again that
\widetilde{\tau}
by the construction using
\Lambda_{1}
is
C\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}
-invariant and
\widetilde{\tau}^{N}=\psi
by Clifford theory. It remains to see that
C\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}=\widehat{N}_{\tau}
.

In order to see this equality, we compare the actions of
C
and
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
on
C_{1}.K_{2}
. While
C
induces a diagonal automorphism on
K_{2}
which can also be induced by conjugation with an element of
\widetilde{T}
,
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
acts on
K_{2}
as an element of
{\underline{E}}_{M}(K_{2})
. Recall
\phi_{2}\in\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
and
\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
is a
\widehat{N}^{\circ}
-stable
\widetilde{T}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(K_{2})
. According to
[
S23a
, Lem. 2.4]
, the properties of
\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
lead to
(\widehat{N}^{\circ}\widetilde{T})_{\phi_{2}}=\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\phi_{2}}\widetilde{T}_{\phi_{2}}
, and hence

(C\widehat{N}^{\circ})_{\phi_{2}}=C_{\phi_{2}}\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\phi_{2}}.

Since
C_{1}\leq\operatorname{Z}(C)
we obtain that

\widehat{N}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}=(C\widehat{N}^{\circ})_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}=C_{\phi_{2}}\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}.

As
\tau=(\lambda.\phi_{2})^{C}
and
\widehat{N}=\widehat{N}^{\circ}C
, this leads to

\widehat{N}_{\tau}=C\widehat{N}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}=C(C_{\phi_{2}}\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}})=C\widehat{N}^{\circ}_{\lambda.\phi_{2}}.

Hence in all cases
\widetilde{\tau}
is
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant, implying that
\psi
is
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant, as well.

General Clifford theory shows that
\widehat{N}_{\psi}=N^{\circ}\widehat{N}_{\tau,\widetilde{\tau}}
. Taking into account that
\widetilde{\tau}
is
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant we obtain the equality
\widehat{N}_{\psi}=N^{\circ}\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. ∎

Above, before
Lemma

6.6
, we have defined
{\mathbb{T}(K_{2})}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{T}}(K_{2})
and the former is by definition an
M_{0}{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})
-stable
\widetilde{T}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(K_{2})
.
Recall
{\widehat{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M{\underline{E}}(M)}({\mathbf{S}})}
and
{C:={\mathrm{C}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})}
. The latter will be shown to be
{{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{\nu F_{q}}={\mathrm{C}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})}
in
Lemma

6.10
below.

Proposition
\@upn
6.7
— Extension map with respect to
{\mathrm{C}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})\unlhd{\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})
.

Set
{{\widetilde{C}:={\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})}}
.

(a)

The set
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
forms an
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{C}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(C)
, in particular
(\widetilde{C}\widehat{N})_{\rho}={\widetilde{C}}_{\rho}{\widehat{N}}_{\rho}
for every
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
.

(b)

There exists some
\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map
{{\Lambda}}
with respect to
C\unlhd N
for
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
.

Proof.

For part (a) observe that
\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
is
{\underline{E}}(M^{\circ})
-stable. Furthermore, because of
[N_{1},K_{2}]=1
, we get that the set
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
is
C\widehat{N}_{1}
-stable. The group
\widehat{N}
induces the same outer automorphisms of
K_{2}
as
C\widehat{N}_{1}
. Hence,
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
is
\widehat{N}
-stable. Since
\mathbb{T}(K_{2})
is a
\widetilde{T}
-transversal,
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
is a
\widetilde{C}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(C)
. This ensures part (a).

For the proof of part (b), we show that maximal extendibility holds with respect to
C\unlhd\widehat{N}
for
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
. This will imply the existence of an associated
\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map and by restriction we will actually get our claim with respect to
C\unlhd N
.

Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
and show that it extends to
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. Note that with
\lambda\in\mathrm{Irr}(C_{1})
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\left.\tau\right\rceil_{K_{2}})\cap\mathbb{T}(K_{2})

Lemma

6.6
applies.
Let
\widetilde{\tau}
be the
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
-invariant extension of
\tau
to
N_{\tau}
constructed in the proof of
Lemma

6.6
. Then
\psi=\widetilde{\tau}^{N^{\circ}}
and
\psi
extends to some
\widetilde{\psi}\in\mathrm{Irr}(\widehat{N}_{\psi})
.

By Clifford correspondence (
2.1
), some character
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}((\widehat{N}_{\psi})_{\tau}\mid\tau)
satisfies
\tau^{\prime\widehat{N}_{\psi}}=\widetilde{\psi}
. Recall that by
Lemma

6.6
we have
\widehat{N}_{\psi}=N^{\circ}\widehat{N}_{\tau}
and hence
(\widehat{N}_{\psi})_{\tau}=\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. Taking into account the degrees, we see that
\tau^{\prime}
is an extension of
\tau
and, by construction, even of
\widetilde{\tau}
to
\widehat{N}_{\tau}
. This finishes our proof.
∎

Next we establish a statement implying later Condition
\mathbf{B}(d)
via the isomorphism between
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}
. Recall that
{\mathbf{S}}
denotes a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
,
\widetilde{C}:={\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
,
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})
.

Proposition
\@upn
6.8
— Extension map with respect to
{\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})\unlhd{\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
.

We keep
Assumption

6.1
and follow
Notation

6.2
.
There exists some
\mathrm{Lin}(\widetilde{N}/N)\rtimes\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map
{{\widetilde{\Lambda}}}
with respect to
\widetilde{C}\unlhd\widetilde{N}
.

Proof.

We wish to apply
Proposition

2.3
with
X=\widetilde{C}
,
A=\widetilde{N}
,
\widehat{A}=\widehat{N}\widetilde{C}
, and
X_{0}=C
,
A_{0}=N
,
\widehat{A}_{0}=\widehat{N}
. The conclusion of
Proposition

2.3
provides exactly the extension map we need with the properties claimed.
We now review the assumptions of
Proposition

2.3
.

We have
C\unlhd\widetilde{C}
with an abelian quotient since
\widetilde{M}=M\widetilde{T}
. The other group theoretic assumptions are clear.

To check that maximal extendibility holds with respect to
C\unlhd\widetilde{C}
, notice first that
\widetilde{C}={{\mathbf{K}}}_{3}^{\nu F_{q}}
where
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{3}
is the connected reductive group
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})
with Frobenius endomorphism
\nu F_{q}
. Then
[{{\mathbf{K}}}_{3},{{\mathbf{K}}}_{3}]=[{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2},{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}]
since
{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})
is a torus. Therefore maximal extendibility holds for
[{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2},{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}]^{\nu F_{q}}\unlhd\widetilde{C}
thanks to
[
CE
, Thm 15.11]
). But then we also get maximal extendibility for
C\unlhd\widetilde{C}
by
Proposition

2.2
(b) and the inclusion
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}^{\nu F_{q}}\leq C
.

We have an
\widehat{N}
-equivariant extension map with respect to
C\unlhd N
for
\mathrm{Irr}(C\mid\mathbb{T}(K_{2}))
thanks to
Proposition

6.7
(b) above.

So we have all the required assumptions and
Proposition

2.3
gives our claim.
∎

6.C
Turning to groups related to Sylow
d
-tori

The aim of the following is the proof of

6.9
, namely the proof of Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
in the case where

is not doubly-regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
, thus completing the proof of

.

First we choose integers
l_{1},l_{2},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}
as in

with regard to

and
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and determine a corresponding group

and that

.

Then we establish in

an isomorphism

between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
, as well as between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
in most cases. Denoting
N:={\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})
,
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
,
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}:=\iota({\mathbf{S}})
,

-transversal in

from

into a

-transversal in

as required by
\mathbf{A}(d)
.

Theorem
\@upn
6.9

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
be as in
2.E
such that
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
and

,

is not doubly regular for

.
Let
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
be a Sylow

-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
,

,
\widetilde{C}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})
,
N^{\prime}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})
,
\widehat{N}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})
and
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})

(a)

There exists an
\widehat{N}^{\prime}
-stable
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
-transversal
\mathbb{T}(N^{\prime})
in
\mathrm{Irr}(N^{\prime})
, such that every
\psi\in\mathbb{T}(N^{\prime})
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}^{\prime}
.

(b)

Maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N^{\prime}\unlhd\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
.

(c)

There exist some
\widehat{N}^{\prime}
-stable

and an

-equivariant extension map with respect to
\widetilde{C}^{\prime}\unlhd\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
.

In particular, Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
Section

2.D
hold for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
.

We have first the following.

Lemma
\@upn
6.10

We assume the situation of
Theorem

6.9
.

(a)

We can choose integers

l_{1},l_{2},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}
, an element
v\in{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
as in
Notation

5.1
and
Definition

5.4
, and groups
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{1},{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2}
,
{\mathbf{M}}
and
M
such that:

(i)

M
satisfies
Assumption

6.1
for
d
.

(ii)

Let
\nu
be the automorphism of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
associated to
v
as in
Definition

5.4
. If
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}\in\{{{\mathbf{G}}}_{1},{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2}\}
is given as in

and

is a Sylow

-torus of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
, then
{\mathbf{S}}

-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})
.

(b)

Then
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})\leq{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}
and the group

satisfies additionally

{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq M={\mathbf{M}}^{{\nu F_{q}}},\ {\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq\widetilde{M},{\text{ and }}

{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq M{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})={\mathbf{M}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}).

Note that (b) implies
{C:={\mathrm{C}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})}={{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{\nu F_{q}}={\mathrm{C}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})}\unlhd N={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{\nu F_{q}}
, whence the consistency with the general notation of Section 2.D.

Proof.

The assumptions of
Theorem

6.9
clearly are satisfied only in the third case of
Lemma

5.8
. In this case, there exist

j\in\{1,2\}
,
v\in{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
,
l_{1},l_{2}>0
and
{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}
with

,
{\epsilon}={\epsilon}_{1}{\epsilon}_{2}
as in Notation
5.1
and
Definition

5.4
such that for the thereby defined groups
{{\mathbf{G}}}_{1},{{\mathbf{G}}}_{2}
and
M
, a Sylow
d
-torus
{\mathbf{S}}
of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})
, where
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}={{\mathbf{G}}}_{j}
.
This gives (i) and (ii) of (a).

The proof of
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq M={\mathbf{M}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}
in (b) uses the description of minimal
d
-split Levi subgroups
{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}({\mathbf{S}})
of
({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})
and their associated relative Weyl groups
({\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})/{\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}}))^{{\nu F_{q}}}
in
[
S10b
]
and
[
GM
, Ch. 3.5]
. Recall
{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}={{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}.{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}
is a central product of
{{\nu F_{q}}}
-stable groups.
Since
d
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
we see that
{\mathbf{T}}_{1}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})
is a torus and

{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})={\mathbf{T}}_{1}.{{\mathbf{K}}}_{2}.

From the description of the root system of the Levi subgroup
{\mathbf{C}}:={\mathrm{C}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
(see
[
S10b
, §7]
and
[
GM
, Example 3.5.15]
) we get first

{\mathrm{C}}_{{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})={\mathbf{C}}.

It essentially remains to show that the relative Weyl groups in
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}
and
M
have the same order. Denoting
d_{0}
and
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})}(d)
as in the proof of
Lemma

5.8
, we abbreviate the latter as
a
. The relative Weyl groups being insensitive to the center of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
, we can use the considerations of
[
GM
, Ch. 3.5]
in classical groups
{{\mathbf{G}}}/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle={\operatorname{SO}}_{2l}({\mathbb{F}})
. From the end of the description in
[
GM
, Example 3.5.15]
(where

is denoted as
e
) we get

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{C}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}/{{\mathbf{C}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}|={(2d_{0})^{a}\cdot a!}\ \ {\text{ and }}\ \ |{\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}\cap{\mathbf{C}})/{(K_{1}\cap{\mathbf{C}})}|=\frac{1}{2}{(2d_{0})^{a}\cdot a!}.

(6.2)

Note that
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{C}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}
since
{\mathbf{S}}
is the only Sylow
d
-torus of
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}({\mathbf{C}})
and for the same reason
{\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}\cap{\mathbf{C}})={\mathrm{N}}_{K_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})
. The latter implies in turn that the second equality in (
6.2
) above yields

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}/{{\mathbf{C}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}|=|({\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})/{{\mathbf{C}}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}|=|\left({\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}}({\mathbf{S}})/{({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}\cap{\mathbf{C}}})\right)^{{\nu F_{q}}}|=\frac{1}{2}{(2d_{0})^{a}\cdot a!}.

(6.3)

By the theory of Sylow
d
-tori (
[
MT
, Thm 25.11]
) all Sylow
d
-tori of
({\mathbf{M}}^{\circ},{{\nu F_{q}}})
are
M^{\circ}
-conjugate and hence
|{\mathrm{N}}_{M}({\mathbf{S}})/{\mathrm{N}}_{M^{\circ}}({\mathbf{S}})|=|M:M^{\circ}|=2
. Combining this with (
6.3
) and the first equality of (
6.2
), we get
|{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{M}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}|=|{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}|
and therefore
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}={\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathbf{M}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq M
as claimed.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}
be some
F
-stable maximal torus of
{\mathbf{M}}^{\circ}\operatorname{Z}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})
(and hence of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
) containing
{\mathbf{S}}
. We have
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}
and therefore
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}\leq(\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbf{M}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}=(\operatorname{Z}(\widetilde{{{\mathbf{G}}}}){\mathbf{M}})^{{\nu F_{q}}}=\widetilde{M}
by the inclusion proved before.

By the definition of
{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})
, we see easily that
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}
is
{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})
-stable and hence the
K_{1}
-orbit of
{\mathbf{S}}
is
{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})
-stable. Hence

{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}\leq{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}}({\mathbf{S}})^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}K_{1}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})\leq M{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}).

This implies
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}\leq M{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})
.
∎

Results from
6.B
concern subgroups of
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}
and for the proof of
Theorem

6.9
we construct an isomorphism between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
. Recall that
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
is the group of abstract automorphisms of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
generated by
F_{p}
and
\gamma
.

Lemma
\@upn
6.11

Recall
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
obtained by restriction of
{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
.

(a)

Assume
({\epsilon},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(1,-1,-1)
or equivalently
v\in{{\mathbf{G}}}\setminus\{1_{{\mathbf{G}}}\}
.
Let
x\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
be an element such that
(vF_{q})^{x}=F_{q}=F
. (Such an element exists according to Lang’s Theorem.) Then conjugation with
x
defines an isomorphism

\iota:{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\ \ \text{ by }\ \ y\mapsto y^{x}=y[y,x]

such that
\iota({{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}})={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
and
\iota({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}})={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
.
Recall
{\underline{E}}(M)={\underline{E}}({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}})=\left\langle F_{p},\gamma\right\rangle\leq\mathrm{Aut}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}})
from
Definition

5.10
.

(i)

If
\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}\neq\{4\}
, then
\iota({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
with
\iota(vF_{q})=F_{q}
inducing an isomorphism
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)/\left\langle h_{0},vF_{q}\right\rangle\cong{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
.

(ii)

If
\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}=\{4\}
, then the groups of automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
induced by
\iota({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}\underline{E}(M))
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
are conjugate by a diagonal automorphism. Moreover
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is cyclic.

(b)

Assume
({\epsilon},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(1,1,1)
or
({\epsilon},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(-1,-1,1)
with
\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}\neq\{4\}
. Recall
{\underline{E}}(M)\leq\mathrm{Aut}({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}})
defined as in the preceding case. The identity map
\iota:{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
induces an isomorphism between
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
.

(c)

Assume that
Hypothesis

5.15
holds so that
{{\nu F_{q}}}=F
. Let
\widehat{t}\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
be some element with
F(\widehat{t})=\widehat{t}h_{0}
and recall
{\underline{E}}(M)=\left\langle F_{p}^{2},\widehat{t}\gamma\right\rangle\leq{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{F})
from
Definition

5.10
.
Then the outer automorphism groups of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
induced by
{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
are conjugate by a diagonal automorphism. Moreover, some inner automorphism of
{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}
induces an isomorphism between
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
.

(d)

In all cases there exists an isomorphism

\iota^{\prime}:{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})\ \ \text{ by }\ \ y\mapsto y^{x^{\prime}}=y[y,x^{\prime}]

induced by conjugation with an element of
x^{\prime}\in{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
,
such that

\iota^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}),\ \iota^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}})={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\text{ and }}\iota^{\prime}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}).

Then one of the following holds:

(d.i)

\iota^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
;

(d.ii)

\iota^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)/\left\langle h_{0},vF_{q}\right\rangle
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
; or

(d.iii)

{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is cyclic.

Proof.

For part (a) we assume
({\epsilon},{\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(1,-1,-1)
and therefore
F=F_{q}
and
v\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
. We get clearly
\iota(vF_{q})=F_{q}
in the semidirect product
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
with
\iota({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
,
\iota({{\mathbf{G}}}^{vF_{q}})={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
\iota({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{vF_{q}})={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
.

Table

5.2
states

\displaystyle[F_{p},v]=1{\text{ and }}[\gamma_{1},v]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}\neq\{4\},\\
h_{0}&\text{if }\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}=\{4\}.\end{cases}

(6.4)

As in
[
S23b
, Prop. 3.6(b)]
or using the Three-Subgroup Lemma
[
Asch
, (8.7)]
,
[F_{p},v]=1
implies
\iota(F_{p})\in{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}F_{p}
.

In the first case of (
6.4
) above where
[\gamma_{1},v]=[\gamma_{1},vF_{q}]=1
, we then have
[[x,F],\gamma_{1}]=[[F,\gamma_{1}],x]=1
and get analogously
[[\gamma_{1},x],F]=1
or equivalently
\iota(\gamma_{1})\in\gamma_{1}{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\gamma_{1}
.
This ensures the statement in (a.i) as follows:
The group
{\underline{E}}(M)
is defined as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{vF_{q}})
hence
{\underline{E}}(M)={\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})/\left\langle F_{q}^{2}\right\rangle
, since
{\epsilon}_{1}=-1
. Now
\left\langle F_{q}^{2},vF_{q}\right\rangle=\left\langle vF_{q},h_{0}\right\rangle
because of
(vF_{q})^{2}=h_{0}F_{q}^{2}
. Note
\iota(\left\langle vF_{q},h_{0}\right\rangle)=\left\langle F_{q},h_{0}\right\rangle
. This implies then the stated isomorphism.

In order to get (a.ii) we now assume
({\epsilon}_{1},{\epsilon}_{2})=(-1,-1){\text{ and }}\{|Z_{1}|,|Z_{2}|\}=\{4\}
.
The latter leads to
2\nmid l_{1}l_{2}
and
2\nmid f
according to
2.24
, which then ensures that
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is cyclic. As above
\iota(F_{p})\in{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}F_{p}
. By construction, we also have
\iota(\gamma_{1})=\gamma_{1}g
for
g:=[\gamma_{1},x]\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
. We get

\displaystyle\iota({{\mathbf{G}}}^{vF_{q}}\underline{E}(M))={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle F_{p},\gamma_{1}g\right\rangle.

(6.5)

From
[\gamma_{1},vF_{q}]=h_{0}
in (
6.4
) and
\iota(vF_{q})=F=F_{q}
we obtain

h_{0}=\iota(h_{0})=\iota([\gamma_{1},vF_{q}])=[\gamma_{1}g,F_{q}]=[g,F_{q}].

Therefore,
\iota(\gamma_{1})=\gamma_{1}g
induces on
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F_{q}}
the product of
\gamma_{1}
and a diagonal automorphism of
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F_{q}}
associated with
h_{0}[\operatorname{Z}({{\mathbf{G}}}),F]
. Let
\widetilde{t}\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
with
F_{q}(\widetilde{t})=\widetilde{t}\mathbf{h}_{\underline{l}}(\varpi)
. Since
2\mid l=l_{1}+l_{2}
we have
[F_{p},\mathbf{h}_{\underline{l}}(\varpi)]=1
by
2.24
(c.i) and therefore
[[\widetilde{t},F_{q}],F_{p}]=[[F_{q},F_{p}],\widetilde{t}]=1
. Then by the Three-Subgroup Lemma again we get
F_{p}^{\widetilde{t}}\in{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}F_{p}
. This leads to

\displaystyle({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle F_{p},\gamma_{1}\right\rangle)^{\widetilde{t}}={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\left\langle F_{p},\gamma_{1}g\right\rangle

(6.6)

since we also have
\widetilde{t}^{\gamma_{1}}\in{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}\widetilde{t}g
. The latter can be seen from the action of
\gamma_{1}
on the group of diagonal outer automorphisms of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
corresponding to its action on
\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
, a group of order 4 where only
h_{0}
and 1 are fixed (see
2.24
(b)).
We now get the statement in (a.ii) from (
6.5
) and (
6.6
).

For part (b) the assumptions imply that
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})={\underline{E}}(M)
.
Part (c) is clear from
Lemma

5.19
.

Now in part (d) we define
\iota^{\prime}
as
\iota
, whenever
\iota({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
. It remains to consider the cases of (a.ii) and (c) and define
\iota^{\prime}
as the product of
\iota
with conjugation with some element
t\in{{\mathbf{G}}}
with
\mathcal{L}_{F}(t)=\mathbf{h}_{\underline{l}}(\varpi)
. Then
\iota^{\prime}
satisfies

\iota^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}),\ \iota^{\prime}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}})={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\text{ and }}\iota^{\prime}({{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}}))={{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}}).

In the case of (a.1),
\iota^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
between
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)/\left\langle h_{0},vF_{q}\right\rangle
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
as stated in (d.ii). In case of (a.2),
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is cyclic.
In case of (b),
\iota^{\prime}
is the identity (map) and hence
{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}{\underline{E}}(M)
and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
are isomorphic as stated in (d.i).
In the case of part (c),
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is also cyclic.
∎

In the next step, we can finally verify Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.9
.

Let
{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
. According to
Lemma

6.10
we can choose the groups
M
and
{{\mathbf{K}}}_{1}
, as well as
v\in{\overline{\mathbf{G}}}
and the corresponding
\nu\in{{\mathbf{G}}}\sqcup\{\gamma\}
, so that Assumption
6.1
is satisfied. Let
{\mathbf{S}}
be a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{K}}}_{1},{{\nu F_{q}}})
. Then
{\mathbf{S}}
is also a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},{{\nu F_{q}}})
and
{\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}}({\mathbf{S}})\leq M
, see
Lemma

6.10
(c).

Let
\iota^{\prime}:{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes\underline{E}({{\mathbf{G}}})\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\rtimes{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})
be the isomorphism of
Lemma

6.11
(d) with
\iota^{\prime}({{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}})={{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
. Then
\iota^{\prime}({\mathbf{S}})
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and we can assume
\iota^{\prime}({\mathbf{S}})={\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
by
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
-conjugacy of Sylow
d
-tori.
Set
\widetilde{N}:={\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{{\nu F_{q}}}}}({\mathbf{S}})
and
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}}^{F}}}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})=\iota^{\prime}(\widetilde{N})
.
Analogously, set
\widehat{N}^{\flat}:={\mathrm{N}}_{M{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})
and
(\widehat{N}^{\prime})^{\flat}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{\mathbf{G}}})}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})
.
According to
Lemma

6.10
(b), we have
{\mathrm{N}}_{M{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})={\mathrm{N}}_{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}{\underline{E}}({\mathbf{M}})}({\mathbf{S}})
and hence
(\widehat{N}^{\prime})^{\flat}=\iota^{\prime}(\widehat{N}^{\flat})
.

Let
\mathbb{T}(N)\subseteq\mathrm{Irr}(N)
be the set from
Theorem

6.4
, hence an
\widehat{N}
-stable
\widetilde{N}
-transversal. Note that
\widehat{N}
is a quotient of
\widehat{N}^{\flat}
.
The set
\mathbb{T}(N^{\prime})
, the image of
\mathbb{T}(N)
under
\iota^{\prime}
is then a
(\widehat{N}^{\prime})^{\flat}
-stable
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
-transversal in
\mathrm{Irr}(N^{\prime})
. This shows the first part of (a) of our theorem.

Let
\chi\in\mathbb{T}(N^{\prime})
. Then
\chi
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}^{\prime}
for
\widehat{N}^{\prime}={\mathrm{N}}_{{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})}({\mathbf{S}}^{\prime})
, whenever
{\underline{E}}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
and hence
\widehat{N}^{\prime}/N^{\prime}
is cyclic, see
Proposition

2.2
(a). If
\widehat{N}^{\prime}_{\chi}/N^{\prime}
is non-cyclic, then
h_{0}\in\ker(\chi)
. Then
\iota^{\prime}
maps
\widehat{N}/\left\langle vF_{q},h_{0}\right\rangle
to
\widehat{N}^{\prime}/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
. Let
\chi_{0}\in\mathbb{T}(N)
be the character mapped to
\chi
via
\iota
. Then
\chi_{0}
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}/\left\langle h_{0}\right\rangle
. By the isomorphisms of
Lemma

6.11
(d.i) or (d.ii), we see that also
\chi
extends to its stabilizer in
\widehat{N}^{\prime}
.

Since maximal extendibility holds with respect to
N\lhd\widetilde{N}
according to
Corollary

6.5
,
\iota^{\prime}(N)=N^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime}(\widetilde{N})=\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
, maximal extendibility also holds with respect to
N^{\prime}\lhd\widetilde{N}^{\prime}
. This is (b).

For the part (c) note that

{\mathrm{N}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}}({\mathbf{S}})\leq\widetilde{M}
from
Lemma

6.10
(b) implies that

\widetilde{C}^{\prime}=\iota({\mathrm{C}}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{{\nu F_{q}}}}({\mathbf{S}}))=\iota(\widetilde{C})
and
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}=\iota(\widetilde{N})
, where
\widetilde{C}:={\mathrm{C}}_{\widetilde{M}}({\mathbf{S}})
.
Using similar applications of
\iota^{\prime}
, part (c) follows from
Proposition

6.7
.
Analogously,
Proposition

6.8
implies part (d).
∎

6.D
Proof of McKay’s equality and Theorem B

We conclude by drawing the consequences from the above.

Theorem
\@upn
6.12

Let
q
be a power of a prime and let
\ell
be a prime not dividing
2q
. Then (
iMK
) holds for the quasisimple groups
\mathrm{D}_{l,{\mathrm{sc}}}^{{\epsilon}}(q)
(
l\geq 4,{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}
) and
\ell
.

Proof.

Let
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
be as in
3.15
such that
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}=\mathrm{D}^{\epsilon}_{l,\mathrm{sc}}(q)
. Thanks to
Theorem

4.1
(c) we can assume
l\geq 5
. Then
the quotient
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}/\operatorname{Z}({{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}})
is simple non-abelian and
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is its universal covering group, see
[
GLS
, Thm 6.1.4]
.

Let
d=d_{\ell}(q)
be the order of
q
in
{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}_{\ell}
. We can assume
d\geq 3
by
Theorem

2.21
and
Remark

2.22
.

Note that if
\mathbf{\Phi}_{m}
is the
m
-th cyclotomic polynomial (
m\geq 1
), then
\ell\mid\mathbf{\Phi}_{m}(q)
if and only if
m=d\ell^{a}
for some
a\geq 0
, see
[
Ma07
, Lem. 5.2(a)]
. Recall that by the definition of the multiplicities
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(m)
the following equation holds

|{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}|=q^{l^{2}-l}\prod_{m\geq 1}\mathbf{\Phi}_{m}(q)^{{}^{a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(m)}}.

If
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(d)\leq 1
, then as recalled in
Lemma

5.8
(ii),
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(d\ell^{a})=0
for any
a\geq 1
, and therefore a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
is included in some Sylow
d
-torus of
{{\mathbf{G}}}
. This
d
-torus has rank
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(d)\leq 1
, so the Sylow
\ell
-subgroups of
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
are cyclic (this also accounts for the case where
\ell\nmid|{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}|
). According to
[
KS16
, Thm 1.1]
the so-called inductive Alperin–McKay condition holds for every
\ell
-block of
{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}
, since such a block has a cyclic defect group. As seen already in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
this implies that (
iMK
) holds for
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
and
\ell
.

We now assume that
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(d)\geq 2
and we can check (
iMK
) by establishing Conditions
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
from
2.19
and
2.20
thanks to
Theorem

2.21
. If
d
is doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
the claim follows from
Theorem

4.1
.

In the remaining cases,
\ell\nmid q
,
d
is not doubly regular for
({{\mathbf{G}}},F)
and
a_{({{\mathbf{G}}},F)}(d)\geq 2
. By
Lemma

5.8
, the assumptions of
Theorem

6.9
are satisfied and according to this result,
\mathbf{A}(d)
and
\mathbf{B}(d)
do hold for the group
{{{{\mathbf{G}}}^{F}}}
.
∎

Theorem A is clearly a consequence of Theorem B, so we concentrate on the latter.

Proof of Theorem B.

Combining the above and
Theorem

2.11
we know that the universal covering group of any finite simple group satisfies (
iMK
) for any prime.
Theorem

2.10
then implies that any finite group satisfies (
iMK
) for any prime. This means that for any finite group
X
, any prime
\ell
and any Sylow
\ell
-subgroup
S\leq X
, there is a
\Gamma=\mathrm{Aut}(X)_{S}
-stable subgroup
N
such that
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\leq N\leq X
with
N\neq X
whenever
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\neq X
, and a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection

\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)\ \ \ \ \text{such that}\ \ \ \ (X\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},X,\chi)\geq_{c}(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime})

(6.7)

for each
\chi\mapsto\chi^{\prime}
by the above bijection. But Theorem B claims that this is true for
{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)
in place of
N
. We show this by induction on the index
|X:{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)|
, the case of a normal
S
being trivial.

By induction one may assume that there is a
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathrm{Aut}(N)_{S}
-equivariant bijection

\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}(N)\to\mathrm{Irr}_{\ell^{\prime}}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S))

with
(N\rtimes\Gamma^{\prime}_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\rtimes\Gamma^{\prime}_{\chi^{\prime\prime}},{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S),\chi^{\prime\prime})
for each
\chi^{\prime}\mapsto\chi^{\prime\prime}
. The latter bijection is also
\Gamma
-equivariant since
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Aut}(N)
contains the image
\Gamma^{0}
of
\Gamma
and the second
\geq_{c}
relation implies
(N\rtimes\Gamma^{0}_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\rtimes\Gamma^{0}_{\chi^{\prime\prime}},{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S),\chi^{\prime\prime})
by restriction (
Lemma

2.6
(c)). Then by the Butterfly
Theorem

2.8

(N\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi^{\prime}},N,\chi^{\prime})\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi^{\prime\prime}},{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S),\chi^{\prime\prime})
. Combined with the first
\geq_{c}
relation (
6.7
), it gives by transitivity of the
\geq_{c}
relation

(X\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},N,\chi)\geq_{c}({\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S)\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi^{\prime\prime}},{\mathrm{N}}_{X}(S),\chi^{\prime\prime})

for each
\chi\mapsto\chi^{\prime}\mapsto\chi^{\prime\prime}
. So we get the claim of Theorem B by composition of the two bijections we have.
∎
